# Family rubrics

Generated by `python3 experiments/plan-writing/rubric.py --write-doc` from [families.json](families.json), [families/](families/) and [examples.json](examples.json); do not edit by hand. [rubric.md](rubric.md) explains how these rubrics relate to the aggregate rubric.

Where a source publishes no score levels, criteria use these levels (scale 1 to 5), worded by the rubric authors: 1: Does not do what the criterion describes.; 3: Partly does what the criterion describes.; 5: Fully does what the criterion describes.

## Requirement standards

Sources: [INCOSE Guide to Writing Requirements (GtWR), version 4](https://www.incose.org/docs/default-source/working-groups/requirements-wg/gtwr/incose_rwg_gtwr_v4_040423_final_drafts.pdf); [ISO/IEC/IEEE 29148:2018 Systems and software engineering — Life cycle processes — Requirements engineering](https://www.iso.org/standard/72089.html); [U.S. National Archives (NARA) Information Services (IR) templates: Quality Standards for StRS, RVTM and PDD](https://www.archives.gov/files/contracts/hipgfi/ir-templates/ir-quality-standards-strs-pdd.docx); [NASA Systems Engineering Handbook (NASA/SP-2016-6105 Rev 2)](https://www.nasa.gov/reference/systems-engineering-handbook/); [RFC 2119 — Key words for use in RFCs to Indicate Requirement Levels (S. Bradner)](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc2119); [RFC 8174 — Ambiguity of Uppercase vs Lowercase in RFC 2119 Key Words](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8174).

Rubric id `requirement_standards`: Requirement-statement and requirement-set quality (ISO/IEC/IEEE 29148, INCOSE GtWR v4, NASA SE Handbook App. C, RFC 2119/8174).

### iso29148_implementation_free — Implementation-Free

> The requirement, while addressing what is necessary and sufficient in the system, avoids placing unnecessary constraints on the architectural design. The objective is to be implementation-independent. The requirement states what is required, not how the requirement should be met.

Source: [ISO/IEC/IEEE 29148:2018 Systems and software engineering — Life cycle processes — Requirements engineering](https://www.iso.org/standard/72089.html), ISO/IEC/IEEE 29148:2011, characteristics of individual requirements, as quoted in NARA IR Quality Standards, Table 2 'Implementation-Free'; quoted as checked against [U.S. National Archives (NARA) Information Services (IR) templates: Quality Standards for StRS, RVTM and PDD](https://www.archives.gov/files/contracts/hipgfi/ir-templates/ir-quality-standards-strs-pdd.docx).

Document kind: requirement.

### iso29148_unambiguous — Unambiguous

> The requirement is stated in such a way so that it can be interpreted in only one way. The requirement is stated simply and is easy to understand.

Source: [ISO/IEC/IEEE 29148:2018 Systems and software engineering — Life cycle processes — Requirements engineering](https://www.iso.org/standard/72089.html), ISO/IEC/IEEE 29148:2011, characteristics of individual requirements, as quoted in NARA IR Quality Standards, Table 2 'Unambiguous'; quoted as checked against [U.S. National Archives (NARA) Information Services (IR) templates: Quality Standards for StRS, RVTM and PDD](https://www.archives.gov/files/contracts/hipgfi/ir-templates/ir-quality-standards-strs-pdd.docx).

Document kind: requirement.

### iso29148_consistent — Consistent

> The requirement is free of conflicts with other requirements.

Source: [ISO/IEC/IEEE 29148:2018 Systems and software engineering — Life cycle processes — Requirements engineering](https://www.iso.org/standard/72089.html), ISO/IEC/IEEE 29148:2011, characteristics of individual requirements, as quoted in NARA IR Quality Standards, Table 2 'Consistent'; quoted as checked against [U.S. National Archives (NARA) Information Services (IR) templates: Quality Standards for StRS, RVTM and PDD](https://www.archives.gov/files/contracts/hipgfi/ir-templates/ir-quality-standards-strs-pdd.docx).

Document kind: requirement.

### iso29148_traceable — Traceable

> The requirement is upwards traceable to specific documented stakeholder statement(s) of need (e.g., the Business Case or Business Need.) The requirement is also downwards traceable to the specific requirements in the lower tier requirements specification or other system definition artifacts.

Source: [ISO/IEC/IEEE 29148:2018 Systems and software engineering — Life cycle processes — Requirements engineering](https://www.iso.org/standard/72089.html), ISO/IEC/IEEE 29148:2011, characteristics of individual requirements, as quoted in NARA IR Quality Standards, Table 2 'Traceable'; quoted as checked against [U.S. National Archives (NARA) Information Services (IR) templates: Quality Standards for StRS, RVTM and PDD](https://www.archives.gov/files/contracts/hipgfi/ir-templates/ir-quality-standards-strs-pdd.docx).

Document kind: requirement.

### iso29148_set_complete — Complete

> The set of requirements needs no further amplification because it contains everything pertinent to the definition of the system or system element being specified. In addition, the set contains no To Be Defined (TBD), To Be Specified (TBS), or To Be Resolved (TBR) clauses.

Source: [ISO/IEC/IEEE 29148:2018 Systems and software engineering — Life cycle processes — Requirements engineering](https://www.iso.org/standard/72089.html), ISO/IEC/IEEE 29148:2011, characteristics of a set of requirements, as quoted in NARA IR Quality Standards, Table 1 'Complete'; quoted as checked against [U.S. National Archives (NARA) Information Services (IR) templates: Quality Standards for StRS, RVTM and PDD](https://www.archives.gov/files/contracts/hipgfi/ir-templates/ir-quality-standards-strs-pdd.docx).

Applies: Resolution of the TBx designations may be iterative and there is an acceptable timeframe for TBx items, determined by risks and dependencies.

Document kind: set of requirements.

### iso29148_priority — Prioritized

> The stakeholder priority of each requirement should be identified. The priority is not intended to imply that some requirements are not necessary, but it may indicate what requirements are candidates for the trade space when decisions regarding alternatives are necessary.

Source: [ISO/IEC/IEEE 29148:2018 Systems and software engineering — Life cycle processes — Requirements engineering](https://www.iso.org/standard/72089.html), ISO/IEC/IEEE 29148:2011 (requirement attribute, not a characteristic), as quoted in NARA IR Quality Standards, Table 2 'Prioritized'; quoted as checked against [U.S. National Archives (NARA) Information Services (IR) templates: Quality Standards for StRS, RVTM and PDD](https://www.archives.gov/files/contracts/hipgfi/ir-templates/ir-quality-standards-strs-pdd.docx).

Document kind: requirement.

### incose_c1_necessary — C1 - Necessary

> The need statement or requirement statement defines capability, characteristic, constraint, or quality factor needed or required to satisfy a lifecycle concept, need, source, or higher-level requirement.

Source: [INCOSE Guide to Writing Requirements (GtWR), version 4](https://www.incose.org/docs/default-source/working-groups/requirements-wg/gtwr/incose_rwg_gtwr_v4_040423_final_drafts.pdf), INCOSE GtWR v4 (INCOSE-TP-2010-006-04, 1 July 2023), Section 2.2, Definition, p. 35.

Document kind: individual need or requirement statement.

### incose_c2_appropriate — C2 - Appropriate

> The specific intent and amount of detail of the need or requirement statement is appropriate to the level (the level of abstraction, organization, or system architecture) of the entity to which it refers.

Source: [INCOSE Guide to Writing Requirements (GtWR), version 4](https://www.incose.org/docs/default-source/working-groups/requirements-wg/gtwr/incose_rwg_gtwr_v4_040423_final_drafts.pdf), INCOSE GtWR v4 (INCOSE-TP-2010-006-04, 1 July 2023), Section 2.3, Definition, p. 38.

Document kind: individual need or requirement statement.

### incose_c3_unambiguous — C3 - Unambiguous

> Need statements and requirement statements must be stated such that their intent is clear and can be interpreted in only one way by all the intended audiences.

Source: [INCOSE Guide to Writing Requirements (GtWR), version 4](https://www.incose.org/docs/default-source/working-groups/requirements-wg/gtwr/incose_rwg_gtwr_v4_040423_final_drafts.pdf), INCOSE GtWR v4 (INCOSE-TP-2010-006-04, 1 July 2023), Section 2.4, Definition, p. 41.

Document kind: individual need or requirement statement.

### incose_c4_complete — C4 - Complete

> The need statement sufficiently describes the necessary capability, characteristic, constraint, conditions, or quality factor to meet the lifecycle concept or source from which it was transformed. The requirement statement sufficiently describes the necessary capability, characteristic, constraint, conditions, or quality factor to meet the need, source, or higher-level requirement from which it was transformed.

Source: [INCOSE Guide to Writing Requirements (GtWR), version 4](https://www.incose.org/docs/default-source/working-groups/requirements-wg/gtwr/incose_rwg_gtwr_v4_040423_final_drafts.pdf), INCOSE GtWR v4 (INCOSE-TP-2010-006-04, 1 July 2023), Section 2.5, Definition, p. 43.

Document kind: individual need or requirement statement.

### incose_c5_singular — C5 - Singular

> The need statement or requirement statement should state a single capability, characteristic, constraint, or quality factor.

Source: [INCOSE Guide to Writing Requirements (GtWR), version 4](https://www.incose.org/docs/default-source/working-groups/requirements-wg/gtwr/incose_rwg_gtwr_v4_040423_final_drafts.pdf), INCOSE GtWR v4 (INCOSE-TP-2010-006-04, 1 July 2023), Section 2.6, Definition, p. 44.

Document kind: individual need or requirement statement.

### incose_c6_feasible — C6 - Feasible

> The need or requirement can be realized within entity constraints (for example: cost, schedule, technical, legal, ethical, safety) with acceptable risk.

Source: [INCOSE Guide to Writing Requirements (GtWR), version 4](https://www.incose.org/docs/default-source/working-groups/requirements-wg/gtwr/incose_rwg_gtwr_v4_040423_final_drafts.pdf), INCOSE GtWR v4 (INCOSE-TP-2010-006-04, 1 July 2023), Section 2.7, Definition, p. 46.

Document kind: individual need or requirement statement.

### incose_c7_verifiable_validatable — C7 – Verifiable/Validatable

> The need statement is structured and worded such that its realization can be validated to the approving authority’s satisfaction. The requirement statement is structured and worded such that its realization can be verified to the approving authority’s satisfaction.

Source: [INCOSE Guide to Writing Requirements (GtWR), version 4](https://www.incose.org/docs/default-source/working-groups/requirements-wg/gtwr/incose_rwg_gtwr_v4_040423_final_drafts.pdf), INCOSE GtWR v4 (INCOSE-TP-2010-006-04, 1 July 2023), Section 2.8, Definition, p. 47.

Document kind: individual need or requirement statement.

### incose_c8_correct — C8 - Correct

> The need statement must be an accurate representation of the lifecycle concept or source from which it was transformed. The requirement statement must be an accurate representation of the need, source, or higher- level requirement from which it was transformed.

Source: [INCOSE Guide to Writing Requirements (GtWR), version 4](https://www.incose.org/docs/default-source/working-groups/requirements-wg/gtwr/incose_rwg_gtwr_v4_040423_final_drafts.pdf), INCOSE GtWR v4 (INCOSE-TP-2010-006-04, 1 July 2023), Section 2.9, Definition, p. 49.

Document kind: individual need or requirement statement.

### incose_c9_conforming — C9 - Conforming

> Statements and expressions of individual needs and requirements should conform to an approved standard pattern and style guide or standard for writing and managing needs and requirements.

Source: [INCOSE Guide to Writing Requirements (GtWR), version 4](https://www.incose.org/docs/default-source/working-groups/requirements-wg/gtwr/incose_rwg_gtwr_v4_040423_final_drafts.pdf), INCOSE GtWR v4 (INCOSE-TP-2010-006-04, 1 July 2023), Section 2.10, Definition, p. 51.

Document kind: individual need or requirement statement.

### incose_c10_complete — C10 - Complete

> The set of needs and set of requirements for an entity should stand alone such that it sufficiently describes the necessary capabilities, characteristics, functionality, performance, drivers, constraints, conditions, interactions, standards, regulations, safety, security, resilience, and quality factors without requiring other sets of needs or sets of requirements at the appropriate level of abstraction.

Source: [INCOSE Guide to Writing Requirements (GtWR), version 4](https://www.incose.org/docs/default-source/working-groups/requirements-wg/gtwr/incose_rwg_gtwr_v4_040423_final_drafts.pdf), INCOSE GtWR v4 (INCOSE-TP-2010-006-04, 1 July 2023), Section 3.1, Definition, p. 53.

Document kind: set of needs or set of requirements.

### incose_c11_consistent — C11 - Consistent

> A set of needs and a set of requirements is consistent if contains individual needs or requirements that are: • unique; • do not conflict with or overlap with others in the set; • makes use of homogeneous units and measurement systems; and • are developed using a consistent language (that is, the same words are used throughout the set to mean the same thing); and use terms that are consistent with the architectural model, project glossary, and project data dictionary.

Source: [INCOSE Guide to Writing Requirements (GtWR), version 4](https://www.incose.org/docs/default-source/working-groups/requirements-wg/gtwr/incose_rwg_gtwr_v4_040423_final_drafts.pdf), INCOSE GtWR v4 (INCOSE-TP-2010-006-04, 1 July 2023), Section 3.2, Definition, p. 56.

Document kind: set of needs or set of requirements.

### incose_c12_feasible — C12 - Feasible

> A set of needs and a set of requirements is feasible if it can be realized within entity constraints (such as cost, schedule, technical) with acceptable risk.

Source: [INCOSE Guide to Writing Requirements (GtWR), version 4](https://www.incose.org/docs/default-source/working-groups/requirements-wg/gtwr/incose_rwg_gtwr_v4_040423_final_drafts.pdf), INCOSE GtWR v4 (INCOSE-TP-2010-006-04, 1 July 2023), Section 3.3, Definition, p. 58.

Document kind: set of needs or set of requirements.

### incose_c13_comprehensible — C13 - Comprehensible

> The set of needs and the set of resulting requirements must each be written such that it is clear as to what is expected of the entity and its relation to the macro system of which it is a part.

Source: [INCOSE Guide to Writing Requirements (GtWR), version 4](https://www.incose.org/docs/default-source/working-groups/requirements-wg/gtwr/incose_rwg_gtwr_v4_040423_final_drafts.pdf), INCOSE GtWR v4 (INCOSE-TP-2010-006-04, 1 July 2023), Section 3.4, Definition, p. 60.

Document kind: set of needs or set of requirements.

### incose_c14_able_to_be_validated — C14 - Able to be Validated

> It must be possible to validate that the set of needs will lead to the achievement of the product goals and objectives, stakeholder expectations, risks, and lifecycle concepts within the constraints (such as cost, schedule, technical, legal and regulatory compliance) with acceptable risk. It must be possible to validate that the set of requirements will lead to the achievement of the set of needs and higher-level requirements within the constraints (such as cost, schedule, technical, and regulatory compliance) with acceptable risk.

Source: [INCOSE Guide to Writing Requirements (GtWR), version 4](https://www.incose.org/docs/default-source/working-groups/requirements-wg/gtwr/incose_rwg_gtwr_v4_040423_final_drafts.pdf), INCOSE GtWR v4 (INCOSE-TP-2010-006-04, 1 July 2023), Section 3.5, Definition, p. 61.

Document kind: set of needs or set of requirements.

### incose_c15_correct — C15 - Correct

> The set of needs must be an accurate representation of the lifecycle concepts or sources from which it was transformed. The set of requirements must be an accurate representation of the needs, sources, or higher- level requirements from which it was transformed.

Source: [INCOSE Guide to Writing Requirements (GtWR), version 4](https://www.incose.org/docs/default-source/working-groups/requirements-wg/gtwr/incose_rwg_gtwr_v4_040423_final_drafts.pdf), INCOSE GtWR v4 (INCOSE-TP-2010-006-04, 1 July 2023), Section 3.6, Definition, p. 62.

Document kind: set of needs or set of requirements.

### nasa_c1_use_of_correct_terms — C.1 Use of Correct Terms

> Shall = requirement Will = facts or declaration of purpose Should = goal

Source: [NASA Systems Engineering Handbook (NASA/SP-2016-6105 Rev 2)](https://www.nasa.gov/reference/systems-engineering-handbook/), NASA/SP-2016-6105 Rev 2, Appendix C 'How to Write a Good Requirement - Checklist', C.1, p. 236.

Document kind: requirements document (requirement statements).

### nasa_c2_personnel_requirement — Personnel Requirement

> The requirement is in the form “responsible party shall perform such and such.” In other words, use the active, rather than the passive voice.

Source: [NASA Systems Engineering Handbook (NASA/SP-2016-6105 Rev 2)](https://www.nasa.gov/reference/systems-engineering-handbook/), NASA/SP-2016-6105 Rev 2, Appendix C 'How to Write a Good Requirement - Checklist', C.2 Editorial Checklist, p. 236.

Document kind: requirements document (requirement statements).

### nasa_c2_product_requirement — Product Requirement

> The requirement is in the form “product ABC shall XYZ.” … The requirement uses consistent terminology to refer to the product and its lower-level entities. Complete with tolerances for qualitative/performance values

Source: [NASA Systems Engineering Handbook (NASA/SP-2016-6105 Rev 2)](https://www.nasa.gov/reference/systems-engineering-handbook/), NASA/SP-2016-6105 Rev 2, Appendix C 'How to Write a Good Requirement - Checklist', C.2 Editorial Checklist, p. 236.

Document kind: requirements document (requirement statements).

### nasa_c2_free_of_implementation — Is the requirement free of implementation?

> Is the requirement free of implementation? (Requirements should state WHAT is needed, NOT HOW to provide it; i.e., state the problem not the solution. Ask, “Why do you need the requirement?” The answer may point to the real requirement.)

Source: [NASA Systems Engineering Handbook (NASA/SP-2016-6105 Rev 2)](https://www.nasa.gov/reference/systems-engineering-handbook/), NASA/SP-2016-6105 Rev 2, Appendix C 'How to Write a Good Requirement - Checklist', C.2 Editorial Checklist, p. 236.

Document kind: requirements document (requirement statements).

### nasa_c2_free_of_operations — Free of descriptions of operations?

> Free of descriptions of operations? (Is this a need the product should satisfy or an activity involving the product? Sentences like “The operator shall…” are almost always operational statements not requirements.)

Source: [NASA Systems Engineering Handbook (NASA/SP-2016-6105 Rev 2)](https://www.nasa.gov/reference/systems-engineering-handbook/), NASA/SP-2016-6105 Rev 2, Appendix C 'How to Write a Good Requirement - Checklist', C.2 Editorial Checklist, p. 236.

Document kind: requirements document (requirement statements).

### nasa_c3_grammar_spelling — C.3 General Goodness Checklist (grammar and spelling)

> The requirement is grammatically correct. The requirement is free of typos, misspellings, and punctuation errors.

Source: [NASA Systems Engineering Handbook (NASA/SP-2016-6105 Rev 2)](https://www.nasa.gov/reference/systems-engineering-handbook/), NASA/SP-2016-6105 Rev 2, Appendix C 'How to Write a Good Requirement - Checklist', C.3, p. 236.

Document kind: requirements document (requirement statements).

### nasa_c3_template_style — C.3 General Goodness Checklist (template and style)

> The requirement complies with the project’s template and style rules.

Source: [NASA Systems Engineering Handbook (NASA/SP-2016-6105 Rev 2)](https://www.nasa.gov/reference/systems-engineering-handbook/), NASA/SP-2016-6105 Rev 2, Appendix C 'How to Write a Good Requirement - Checklist', C.3, p. 237.

Document kind: requirements document (requirement statements).

### nasa_c3_stated_positively — C.3 General Goodness Checklist (stated positively)

> The requirement is stated positively (as opposed to negatively, i.e., “shall not”).

Source: [NASA Systems Engineering Handbook (NASA/SP-2016-6105 Rev 2)](https://www.nasa.gov/reference/systems-engineering-handbook/), NASA/SP-2016-6105 Rev 2, Appendix C 'How to Write a Good Requirement - Checklist', C.3, p. 237.

Document kind: requirements document (requirement statements).

### nasa_c3_tbd_tbr — C.3 General Goodness Checklist (TBD/TBR)

> The use of “To Be Determined” (TBD) values should be minimized. It is better to use a best estimate for a value and mark it “To Be Resolved” (TBR) with the rationale along with what should be done to eliminate the TBR, who is responsible for its elimination, and by when it should be eliminated.

Source: [NASA Systems Engineering Handbook (NASA/SP-2016-6105 Rev 2)](https://www.nasa.gov/reference/systems-engineering-handbook/), NASA/SP-2016-6105 Rev 2, Appendix C 'How to Write a Good Requirement - Checklist', C.3, p. 237.

Document kind: requirements document (requirement statements).

### nasa_c3_rationale — C.3 General Goodness Checklist (rationale)

> The requirement is accompanied by an intelligible rationale, including any assumptions. Can you validate (concur with) the assumptions? Assumptions should be confirmed before baselining.

Source: [NASA Systems Engineering Handbook (NASA/SP-2016-6105 Rev 2)](https://www.nasa.gov/reference/systems-engineering-handbook/), NASA/SP-2016-6105 Rev 2, Appendix C 'How to Write a Good Requirement - Checklist', C.3, p. 237.

Document kind: requirements document (requirement statements).

### nasa_c3_proper_section — C.3 General Goodness Checklist (location)

> The requirement is located in the proper section of the document (e.g., not in an appendix).

Source: [NASA Systems Engineering Handbook (NASA/SP-2016-6105 Rev 2)](https://www.nasa.gov/reference/systems-engineering-handbook/), NASA/SP-2016-6105 Rev 2, Appendix C 'How to Write a Good Requirement - Checklist', C.3, p. 237.

Document kind: requirements document (requirement statements).

### nasa_c4_clarity — Clarity

> Are the requirements clear and unambiguous? (Are all aspects of the requirement understandable and not subject to misinterpretation? Is the requirement free from indefinite pronouns (this, these) and ambiguous terms (e.g., “as appropriate,” “etc.,” “and/or,” “but not limited to”)?) Are the requirements concise and simple? Do the requirements express only one thought per requirement statement

Source: [NASA Systems Engineering Handbook (NASA/SP-2016-6105 Rev 2)](https://www.nasa.gov/reference/systems-engineering-handbook/), NASA/SP-2016-6105 Rev 2, Appendix C 'How to Write a Good Requirement - Checklist', C.4 Requirements Validation Checklist, p. 237.

Document kind: requirements document (requirement statements).

### nasa_c4_completeness — Completeness

> Are requirements stated as completely as possible? Have all incomplete requirements been captured as TBDs or TBRs and a complete listing of them maintained with the requirements? Are any requirements missing? … Have all assumptions been explicitly stated?

Source: [NASA Systems Engineering Handbook (NASA/SP-2016-6105 Rev 2)](https://www.nasa.gov/reference/systems-engineering-handbook/), NASA/SP-2016-6105 Rev 2, Appendix C 'How to Write a Good Requirement - Checklist', C.4 Requirements Validation Checklist, p. 237.

Document kind: requirements document (requirement statements).

### nasa_c4_compliance — Compliance

> Are all requirements at the correct level (e.g., system, segment, element, subsystem)? Are requirements free of implementation specifics? (Requirements should state what is needed, not how to provide it.) Are requirements free of descriptions of operations?

Source: [NASA Systems Engineering Handbook (NASA/SP-2016-6105 Rev 2)](https://www.nasa.gov/reference/systems-engineering-handbook/), NASA/SP-2016-6105 Rev 2, Appendix C 'How to Write a Good Requirement - Checklist', C.4 Requirements Validation Checklist, pp. 237-238.

Document kind: requirements document (requirement statements).

### nasa_c4_consistency — Consistency

> Are the requirements stated consistently without contradicting themselves or the requirements of related systems? Is the terminology consistent with the user and sponsor’s terminology? With the project glossary? Is the terminology consistently used throughout the document?

Source: [NASA Systems Engineering Handbook (NASA/SP-2016-6105 Rev 2)](https://www.nasa.gov/reference/systems-engineering-handbook/), NASA/SP-2016-6105 Rev 2, Appendix C 'How to Write a Good Requirement - Checklist', C.4 Requirements Validation Checklist, p. 238.

Document kind: requirements document (requirement statements).

### nasa_c4_traceability — Traceability

> Are all requirements needed? Is each requirement necessary to meet the parent requirement? … Are all requirements (functions, structures, and constraints) bidirectionally traceable to higher-level requirements or mission or system-of-interest scope

Source: [NASA Systems Engineering Handbook (NASA/SP-2016-6105 Rev 2)](https://www.nasa.gov/reference/systems-engineering-handbook/), NASA/SP-2016-6105 Rev 2, Appendix C 'How to Write a Good Requirement - Checklist', C.4 Requirements Validation Checklist, p. 238.

Document kind: requirements document (requirement statements).

### nasa_c4_correctness — Correctness

> Is each requirement correct? Is each stated assumption correct? Assumptions should be confirmed before the document can be baselined. Are the requirements technically feasible?

Source: [NASA Systems Engineering Handbook (NASA/SP-2016-6105 Rev 2)](https://www.nasa.gov/reference/systems-engineering-handbook/), NASA/SP-2016-6105 Rev 2, Appendix C 'How to Write a Good Requirement - Checklist', C.4 Requirements Validation Checklist, p. 238.

Document kind: requirements document (requirement statements).

### nasa_c4_functionality — Functionality

> Are all described functions necessary and together sufficient to meet mission and system goals and objectives?

Source: [NASA Systems Engineering Handbook (NASA/SP-2016-6105 Rev 2)](https://www.nasa.gov/reference/systems-engineering-handbook/), NASA/SP-2016-6105 Rev 2, Appendix C 'How to Write a Good Requirement - Checklist', C.4 Requirements Validation Checklist, p. 238.

Document kind: requirements document (requirement statements).

### nasa_c4_performance — Performance

> Are all required performance specifications and margins listed (e.g., consider timing, throughput, storage size, latency, accuracy and precision)? Is each performance requirement realistic? Are the tolerances overly tight?

Source: [NASA Systems Engineering Handbook (NASA/SP-2016-6105 Rev 2)](https://www.nasa.gov/reference/systems-engineering-handbook/), NASA/SP-2016-6105 Rev 2, Appendix C 'How to Write a Good Requirement - Checklist', C.4 Requirements Validation Checklist, p. 238.

Document kind: requirements document (requirement statements).

### nasa_c4_interfaces — Interfaces

> Are all external interfaces clearly defined? … Are all internal interfaces clearly defined? Are all interfaces necessary, sufficient, and consistent with each other?

Source: [NASA Systems Engineering Handbook (NASA/SP-2016-6105 Rev 2)](https://www.nasa.gov/reference/systems-engineering-handbook/), NASA/SP-2016-6105 Rev 2, Appendix C 'How to Write a Good Requirement - Checklist', C.4 Requirements Validation Checklist, pp. 238-239.

Document kind: requirements document (requirement statements).

### nasa_c4_maintainability — Maintainability

> Have the requirements for maintainability of the system been specified in a measurable, verifiable manner? Are requirements written so that ripple effects from changes are minimized (i.e., requirements are as weakly coupled as possible)?

Source: [NASA Systems Engineering Handbook (NASA/SP-2016-6105 Rev 2)](https://www.nasa.gov/reference/systems-engineering-handbook/), NASA/SP-2016-6105 Rev 2, Appendix C 'How to Write a Good Requirement - Checklist', C.4 Requirements Validation Checklist, p. 239.

Document kind: requirements document (requirement statements).

### nasa_c4_reliability — Reliability

> Are clearly defined, measurable, and verifiable reliability requirements specified? Are there error detection, reporting, handling, and recovery requirements? Are undesired events (e.g., single-event upset, data loss or scrambling, operator error) considered and their required responses specified?

Source: [NASA Systems Engineering Handbook (NASA/SP-2016-6105 Rev 2)](https://www.nasa.gov/reference/systems-engineering-handbook/), NASA/SP-2016-6105 Rev 2, Appendix C 'How to Write a Good Requirement - Checklist', C.4 Requirements Validation Checklist, p. 239.

Document kind: requirements document (requirement statements).

### nasa_c4_verifiability_testability — Verifiability/Testability

> Can the system be tested, demonstrated, inspected, or analyzed to show that it satisfies requirements? … Does a means exist to measure the accomplishment of the requirement and verify compliance? Can the criteria for verification be stated? … Are the requirements free of unverifiable terms (e.g., flexible, easy, sufficient, safe, ad hoc, adequate, accommodate, user-friendly, usable

Source: [NASA Systems Engineering Handbook (NASA/SP-2016-6105 Rev 2)](https://www.nasa.gov/reference/systems-engineering-handbook/), NASA/SP-2016-6105 Rev 2, Appendix C 'How to Write a Good Requirement - Checklist', C.4 Requirements Validation Checklist, p. 239.

Document kind: requirements document (requirement statements).

### nasa_c4_data_usage — Data Usage

> are “don’t care” conditions truly “don’t care”? … Are “don’t care” conditions values explicitly stated?

Source: [NASA Systems Engineering Handbook (NASA/SP-2016-6105 Rev 2)](https://www.nasa.gov/reference/systems-engineering-handbook/), NASA/SP-2016-6105 Rev 2, Appendix C 'How to Write a Good Requirement - Checklist', C.4 Requirements Validation Checklist, p. 239.

Applies: Where applicable

Document kind: requirements document (requirement statements).

### rfc2119_key_words — MUST / MUST NOT / SHOULD / SHOULD NOT / MAY

> MUST This word, or the terms "REQUIRED" or "SHALL", mean that the definition is an absolute requirement of the specification. … SHOULD This word, or the adjective "RECOMMENDED", mean that there may exist valid reasons in particular circumstances to ignore a particular item, but the full implications must be understood and carefully weighed before choosing a different course.

Source: [RFC 2119 — Key words for use in RFCs to Indicate Requirement Levels (S. Bradner)](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc2119), RFC 2119, Sections 1-5.

Document kind: specification using BCP 14 key words.

### rfc2119_guidance_imperatives — Guidance in the use of these Imperatives

> Imperatives of the type defined in this memo must be used with care and sparingly. In particular, they MUST only be used where it is actually required for interoperation or to limit behavior which has potential for causing harm (e.g., limiting retransmisssions) For example, they must not be used to try to impose a particular method on implementors where the method is not required for interoperability.

Source: [RFC 2119 — Key words for use in RFCs to Indicate Requirement Levels (S. Bradner)](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc2119), RFC 2119, Section 6.

Document kind: specification using BCP 14 key words.

### rfc2119_security_considerations — Security Considerations

> Document authors should take the time to elaborate the security implications of not following recommendations or requirements as most implementors will not have had the benefit of the experience and discussion that produced the specification.

Source: [RFC 2119 — Key words for use in RFCs to Indicate Requirement Levels (S. Bradner)](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc2119), RFC 2119, Section 7.

Document kind: specification using BCP 14 key words.

### rfc8174_uppercase_only — Clarifying Capitalization of Key Words

> The words have the meanings specified herein only when they are in all capitals. o When these words are not capitalized, they have their normal English meanings and are not affected by this document.

Source: [RFC 8174 — Ambiguity of Uppercase vs Lowercase in RFC 2119 Key Words](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8174), RFC 8174, Section 2 (NEW text).

Document kind: specification using BCP 14 key words.

### rfc8174_key_words_optional — Clarifying Capitalization of Key Words

> These words can be used as defined here, but using them is not required. Specifically, normative text does not require the use of these key words. They are used for clarity and consistency when that is what's wanted, but a lot of normative text does not use them and is still normative.

Source: [RFC 8174 — Ambiguity of Uppercase vs Lowercase in RFC 2119 Key Words](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8174), RFC 8174, Section 2 (NEW text).

Document kind: specification using BCP 14 key words.

### rfc8174_boilerplate — Clarifying Capitalization of Key Words

> Authors who follow these guidelines should incorporate this phrase near the beginning of their document: The key words "MUST", "MUST NOT", "REQUIRED", "SHALL", "SHALL NOT", "SHOULD", "SHOULD NOT", "RECOMMENDED", "NOT RECOMMENDED", "MAY", and "OPTIONAL" in this document are to be interpreted as described in BCP 14 [RFC2119] [RFC8174] when, and only when, they appear in all capitals, as shown here.

Source: [RFC 8174 — Ambiguity of Uppercase vs Lowercase in RFC 2119 Key Words](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8174), RFC 8174, Section 2 (NEW text).

Applies: Authors who follow these guidelines

Document kind: specification using BCP 14 key words.

Contributes rather than criteria:

- [NASA Systems Engineering Handbook (NASA/SP-2016-6105 Rev 2)](https://www.nasa.gov/reference/systems-engineering-handbook/) (methods): Appendix D (Requirements Verification Matrix) gives a verification-planning method (one row per 'shall', with source and verification method) rather than a writing criterion.
- [INCOSE Guide to Writing Requirements (GtWR), version 4](https://www.incose.org/docs/default-source/working-groups/requirements-wg/gtwr/incose_rwg_gtwr_v4_040423_final_drafts.pdf) (methods): Sections 1 and 5-6 (needs vs requirements, attributes, patterns/templates such as 'When <trigger>, the <SOI> shall <response>') describe how to derive and structure statements; they support C9 but are methods, not further characteristics.

Gaps: ISO/IEC/IEEE 29148: full text is paywalled. The ISO OBP page returned 403 to scripts, and the public VDE preview of the 2018 edition (saved as iso29148_ed2_preview_vde.txt) stops at clause 3, before 5.2.5/5.2.6. The only public source found that quotes the standard's own definitions is NARA's IR Quality Standards (saved as nara_strs.txt). It marks quoted text 'IEEE:', it is the 2011 edition, and NARA says its standards are 'based upon, but are not identical to' 29148:2011. So the six iso29148_* criteria are second-hand quotes. The traceable wording looks NARA-edited. Not quotable from any saved public source: 2011 individual necessary, complete, singular, feasible and verifiable; 2011 set consistent, affordable and bounded; and every 2018-edition definition. The 2018 names (necessary, appropriate, unambiguous, complete, singular, feasible, verifiable, correct, conforming; set: complete, consistent, feasible, comprehensible, able to be validated) appear only in a secondary paraphrase (arXiv 2408.10886 Table I, saved). They match INCOSE GtWR C1-C14 one for one, and GtWR v4 says it draws on 29148. NARA prints the text of 29148's individual 'Complete' under its set-level 'Consistent' row, so that text was not used. INCOSE GtWR v4 was retrieved from a Wayback capture (2024-04-20) of the incose.org PDF. Only the 15 characteristic definitions are included. The 42 rules R1-R42 (Section 4, e.g. R4 defined terms, R7 vague terms) are criteria in their own right and are not included here. NASA App. C is a checklist. Long checklist items are quoted in part, with ' … ' marking each omission. No source in this family publishes performance levels, so levels is null throughout. No examples are attached. None of the verified examples comes from an ISO, INCOSE or NASA document. The RFC errata (b0-b2, b6-b8) are about coverage and self-contradiction, not about RFC 2119/8174 keyword use, and b5 (Berry et al.) is a different source.

## Architecture and design practice

Sources: [Design Docs at Google (M. Ubl, industrialempathy.com)](https://www.industrialempathy.com/posts/design-docs-at-google/); [ISO/IEC FDIS 42010:2011 (IEEE P42010/D9) Systems and software engineering — Architecture description (final draft posted on the official ISO/IEC/IEEE 42010 website)](https://web.archive.org/web/20120207051458/http://www.iso-architecture.org/ieee-1471/docs/ISO-IEC-FDIS-42010.pdf); [RFC 4101 — Writing Protocol Models (E. Rescorla)](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc4101).

Rubric id `architecture_design`: Architecture description and design-doc quality (ISO/IEC/IEEE 42010, Design Docs at Google, RFC 4101).

### iso42010_identification_overview — Architecture description identification and overview

> An architecture description shall identify the system-of-interest and include supplementary information as determined by the project and/or organization.

Source: [ISO/IEC FDIS 42010:2011 (IEEE P42010/D9) Systems and software engineering — Architecture description (final draft posted on the official ISO/IEC/IEEE 42010 website)](https://web.archive.org/web/20120207051458/http://www.iso-architecture.org/ieee-1471/docs/ISO-IEC-FDIS-42010.pdf), ISO/IEC FDIS 42010:2011 (IEEE P42010/D9), Clause 5, 5.2.

Applies: The verb include when used in Clause 5 indicates that either the information is present in the architecture description or reference to that information is provided therein.

Document kind: architecture description.

### iso42010_evaluation_results — Architecture description identification and overview

> Results from any evaluations of the architecture or its architecture description shall be included.

Source: [ISO/IEC FDIS 42010:2011 (IEEE P42010/D9) Systems and software engineering — Architecture description (final draft posted on the official ISO/IEC/IEEE 42010 website)](https://web.archive.org/web/20120207051458/http://www.iso-architecture.org/ieee-1471/docs/ISO-IEC-FDIS-42010.pdf), ISO/IEC FDIS 42010:2011 (IEEE P42010/D9), Clause 5, 5.2.

Applies: The verb include when used in Clause 5 indicates that either the information is present in the architecture description or reference to that information is provided therein.

Document kind: architecture description.

### iso42010_stakeholders — Identification of stakeholders and concerns

> An architecture description shall identify the system stakeholders having concerns considered fundamental to the architecture of the system-of-interest.

Source: [ISO/IEC FDIS 42010:2011 (IEEE P42010/D9) Systems and software engineering — Architecture description (final draft posted on the official ISO/IEC/IEEE 42010 website)](https://web.archive.org/web/20120207051458/http://www.iso-architecture.org/ieee-1471/docs/ISO-IEC-FDIS-42010.pdf), ISO/IEC FDIS 42010:2011 (IEEE P42010/D9), Clause 5, 5.3.

Applies: The following stakeholders shall be considered and when applicable, identified in the architecture description:

Document kind: architecture description.

### iso42010_concerns — Identification of stakeholders and concerns

> An architecture description shall identify the concerns considered fundamental to the architecture of the system-of-interest. The following concerns shall be considered and when applicable, identified in the architecture description: — the purposes of the system; — the suitability of the architecture for achieving the system’s purposes; — the feasibility of constructing and deploying the system;

Source: [ISO/IEC FDIS 42010:2011 (IEEE P42010/D9) Systems and software engineering — Architecture description (final draft posted on the official ISO/IEC/IEEE 42010 website)](https://web.archive.org/web/20120207051458/http://www.iso-architecture.org/ieee-1471/docs/ISO-IEC-FDIS-42010.pdf), ISO/IEC FDIS 42010:2011 (IEEE P42010/D9), Clause 5, 5.3.

Applies: when applicable

Document kind: architecture description.

### iso42010_concern_stakeholder_association — Identification of stakeholders and concerns

> An architecture description shall associate each identified concern with the identified stakeholders having that concern.

Source: [ISO/IEC FDIS 42010:2011 (IEEE P42010/D9) Systems and software engineering — Architecture description (final draft posted on the official ISO/IEC/IEEE 42010 website)](https://web.archive.org/web/20120207051458/http://www.iso-architecture.org/ieee-1471/docs/ISO-IEC-FDIS-42010.pdf), ISO/IEC FDIS 42010:2011 (IEEE P42010/D9), Clause 5, 5.3.

Document kind: architecture description.

### iso42010_viewpoints — Architecture viewpoints

> An architecture description shall include each architecture viewpoint used therein. Each included architecture viewpoint shall be specified in accordance with the provisions of Clause 7. Each concern identified in accordance with 5.3 shall be framed by at least one viewpoint.

Source: [ISO/IEC FDIS 42010:2011 (IEEE P42010/D9) Systems and software engineering — Architecture description (final draft posted on the official ISO/IEC/IEEE 42010 website)](https://web.archive.org/web/20120207051458/http://www.iso-architecture.org/ieee-1471/docs/ISO-IEC-FDIS-42010.pdf), ISO/IEC FDIS 42010:2011 (IEEE P42010/D9), Clause 5, 5.4.

Applies: The verb include when used in Clause 5 indicates that either the information is present in the architecture description or reference to that information is provided therein.

Document kind: architecture description.

### iso42010_views — Architecture views

> An architecture description shall include exactly one architecture view for each architecture viewpoint used. Each architecture view shall adhere to the conventions of its governing architecture viewpoint.

Source: [ISO/IEC FDIS 42010:2011 (IEEE P42010/D9) Systems and software engineering — Architecture description (final draft posted on the official ISO/IEC/IEEE 42010 website)](https://web.archive.org/web/20120207051458/http://www.iso-architecture.org/ieee-1471/docs/ISO-IEC-FDIS-42010.pdf), ISO/IEC FDIS 42010:2011 (IEEE P42010/D9), Clause 5, 5.5.

Applies: The verb include when used in Clause 5 indicates that either the information is present in the architecture description or reference to that information is provided therein.

Document kind: architecture description.

### iso42010_view_known_issues — Architecture views

> recording of any known issues within a view with respect to its governing viewpoint. … “Known issues” per d) include unresolved issues, exceptions and deviations from the conventions.

Source: [ISO/IEC FDIS 42010:2011 (IEEE P42010/D9) Systems and software engineering — Architecture description (final draft posted on the official ISO/IEC/IEEE 42010 website)](https://web.archive.org/web/20120207051458/http://www.iso-architecture.org/ieee-1471/docs/ISO-IEC-FDIS-42010.pdf), ISO/IEC FDIS 42010:2011 (IEEE P42010/D9), Clause 5, 5.5 d) and NOTE 3.

Document kind: architecture description.

### iso42010_models — Architecture models

> An architecture view shall be composed of one or more architecture models. Each architecture model shall include version identification as specified by the organization and/or project. Each architecture model shall identify its governing model kind and adhere to the conventions of that model kind (see 5.4).

Source: [ISO/IEC FDIS 42010:2011 (IEEE P42010/D9) Systems and software engineering — Architecture description (final draft posted on the official ISO/IEC/IEEE 42010 website)](https://web.archive.org/web/20120207051458/http://www.iso-architecture.org/ieee-1471/docs/ISO-IEC-FDIS-42010.pdf), ISO/IEC FDIS 42010:2011 (IEEE P42010/D9), Clause 5, 5.6.

Document kind: architecture description.

### iso42010_known_inconsistencies — Consistency within an architecture description

> An architecture description shall record any known inconsistencies across its architecture models and its views.

Source: [ISO/IEC FDIS 42010:2011 (IEEE P42010/D9) Systems and software engineering — Architecture description (final draft posted on the official ISO/IEC/IEEE 42010 website)](https://web.archive.org/web/20120207051458/http://www.iso-architecture.org/ieee-1471/docs/ISO-IEC-FDIS-42010.pdf), ISO/IEC FDIS 42010:2011 (IEEE P42010/D9), Clause 5, 5.7.1.

Applies: While consistent architecture descriptions are to be preferred, it is sometimes infeasible or impractical to resolve all inconsistencies for reasons of time, effort, or insufficient information. In such situations, known inconsistencies are to be recorded.

Document kind: architecture description.

### iso42010_consistency_analysis — Consistency within an architecture description

> An architecture description should include an analysis of consistency of its architecture models and its views.

Source: [ISO/IEC FDIS 42010:2011 (IEEE P42010/D9) Systems and software engineering — Architecture description (final draft posted on the official ISO/IEC/IEEE 42010 website)](https://web.archive.org/web/20120207051458/http://www.iso-architecture.org/ieee-1471/docs/ISO-IEC-FDIS-42010.pdf), ISO/IEC FDIS 42010:2011 (IEEE P42010/D9), Clause 5, 5.7.1.

Document kind: architecture description.

### iso42010_correspondences — Correspondences

> Each correspondence in an architecture description shall be identified and identify its participating AD elements.

Source: [ISO/IEC FDIS 42010:2011 (IEEE P42010/D9) Systems and software engineering — Architecture description (final draft posted on the official ISO/IEC/IEEE 42010 website)](https://web.archive.org/web/20120207051458/http://www.iso-architecture.org/ieee-1471/docs/ISO-IEC-FDIS-42010.pdf), ISO/IEC FDIS 42010:2011 (IEEE P42010/D9), Clause 5, 5.7.2.

Document kind: architecture description.

### iso42010_correspondence_rules — Correspondence rules

> An architecture description shall include each correspondence rule applying to it. … For each identified correspondence rule, an architecture description shall record whether the rule holds or otherwise record all known violations.

Source: [ISO/IEC FDIS 42010:2011 (IEEE P42010/D9) Systems and software engineering — Architecture description (final draft posted on the official ISO/IEC/IEEE 42010 website)](https://web.archive.org/web/20120207051458/http://www.iso-architecture.org/ieee-1471/docs/ISO-IEC-FDIS-42010.pdf), ISO/IEC FDIS 42010:2011 (IEEE P42010/D9), Clause 5, 5.7.3.

Applies: The verb include when used in Clause 5 indicates that either the information is present in the architecture description or reference to that information is provided therein.

Document kind: architecture description.

### iso42010_viewpoint_rationale — Rationale recording

> An architecture description shall include a rationale for each architecture viewpoint included for use per 5.4 in terms of its stakeholders, concerns, model kinds, notations and methods.

Source: [ISO/IEC FDIS 42010:2011 (IEEE P42010/D9) Systems and software engineering — Architecture description (final draft posted on the official ISO/IEC/IEEE 42010 website)](https://web.archive.org/web/20120207051458/http://www.iso-architecture.org/ieee-1471/docs/ISO-IEC-FDIS-42010.pdf), ISO/IEC FDIS 42010:2011 (IEEE P42010/D9), Clause 5, 5.8.1.

Applies: The verb include when used in Clause 5 indicates that either the information is present in the architecture description or reference to that information is provided therein.

Document kind: architecture description.

### iso42010_decision_rationale — Rationale recording

> An architecture description shall include rationale for each decision considered to be a key architecture decision (per 5.8.2).

Source: [ISO/IEC FDIS 42010:2011 (IEEE P42010/D9) Systems and software engineering — Architecture description (final draft posted on the official ISO/IEC/IEEE 42010 website)](https://web.archive.org/web/20120207051458/http://www.iso-architecture.org/ieee-1471/docs/ISO-IEC-FDIS-42010.pdf), ISO/IEC FDIS 42010:2011 (IEEE P42010/D9), Clause 5, 5.8.1.

Applies: The verb include when used in Clause 5 indicates that either the information is present in the architecture description or reference to that information is provided therein.

Document kind: architecture description.

### iso42010_alternatives — Rationale recording

> An architecture description should provide evidence of the consideration of alternatives and the rationale for the choices made.

Source: [ISO/IEC FDIS 42010:2011 (IEEE P42010/D9) Systems and software engineering — Architecture description (final draft posted on the official ISO/IEC/IEEE 42010 website)](https://web.archive.org/web/20120207051458/http://www.iso-architecture.org/ieee-1471/docs/ISO-IEC-FDIS-42010.pdf), ISO/IEC FDIS 42010:2011 (IEEE P42010/D9), Clause 5, 5.8.1.

Document kind: architecture description.

### iso42010_decision_recording — Decision recording

> An architecture description should record architecture decisions considered to be key to the architecture of the system-of-interest. It is not practical to record every architecture decision about a system.

Source: [ISO/IEC FDIS 42010:2011 (IEEE P42010/D9) Systems and software engineering — Architecture description (final draft posted on the official ISO/IEC/IEEE 42010 website)](https://web.archive.org/web/20120207051458/http://www.iso-architecture.org/ieee-1471/docs/ISO-IEC-FDIS-42010.pdf), ISO/IEC FDIS 42010:2011 (IEEE P42010/D9), Clause 5, 5.8.2.

Applies: A decision recording and sharing strategy should be applied by the organization and/or project to establish criteria for selecting key decisions to be recorded and supported with rationales in the architecture description.

Document kind: architecture description.

### iso42010_decision_record_contents — Decision recording

> When recording decisions, the following should be considered: — the decision is uniquely identified; — the decision is stated; — the decision is linked to the system concerns to which it pertains; — the owner of the decision is identified;

Source: [ISO/IEC FDIS 42010:2011 (IEEE P42010/D9) Systems and software engineering — Architecture description (final draft posted on the official ISO/IEC/IEEE 42010 website)](https://web.archive.org/web/20120207051458/http://www.iso-architecture.org/ieee-1471/docs/ISO-IEC-FDIS-42010.pdf), ISO/IEC FDIS 42010:2011 (IEEE P42010/D9), Clause 5, 5.8.2.

Document kind: architecture description.

### google_dd_form — Anatomy of a design doc

> Design docs are informal documents and thus don’t follow a strict guideline for their content. Rule #1 is: Write them in whatever form makes the most sense for the particular project.

Source: [Design Docs at Google (M. Ubl, industrialempathy.com)](https://www.industrialempathy.com/posts/design-docs-at-google/), Design Docs at Google (Ubl, 2020), 'Anatomy of a design doc'.

Document kind: software design doc.

### google_dd_context_and_scope — Context and scope

> This section gives the reader a very rough overview of the landscape in which the new system is being built and what is actually being built. This isn’t a requirements doc. Keep it succinct! The goal is that readers are brought up to speed but some previous knowledge can be assumed and detailed info can be linked to. This section should be entirely focused on objective background facts.

Source: [Design Docs at Google (M. Ubl, industrialempathy.com)](https://www.industrialempathy.com/posts/design-docs-at-google/), Design Docs at Google (Ubl, 2020), 'Context and scope'.

Document kind: software design doc.

### google_dd_goals_and_non_goals — Goals and non-goals

> A short list of bullet points of what the goals of the system are, and, sometimes more importantly, what non-goals are. Note, that non-goals aren’t negated goals like “The system shouldn’t crash”, but rather things that could reasonably be goals, but are explicitly chosen not to be goals.

Source: [Design Docs at Google (M. Ubl, industrialempathy.com)](https://www.industrialempathy.com/posts/design-docs-at-google/), Design Docs at Google (Ubl, 2020), 'Goals and non-goals'.

Document kind: software design doc.

### google_dd_actual_design — The actual design

> This section should start with an overview and then go into details. The design doc is the place to write down the trade-offs you made in designing your software. Focus on those trade-offs to produce a useful document with long-term value. That is, given the context (facts), goals and non-goals (requirements), the design doc is the place to suggest solutions and show why a particular solution best satisfies those goals.

Source: [Design Docs at Google (M. Ubl, industrialempathy.com)](https://www.industrialempathy.com/posts/design-docs-at-google/), Design Docs at Google (Ubl, 2020), 'The actual design'.

Document kind: software design doc.

### google_dd_system_context_diagram — System-context-diagram

> In many docs a system-context-diagram can be very useful. Such a diagram shows the system as part of the larger technical landscape and allows readers to contextualize the new design given its environment that they are already familiar with.

Source: [Design Docs at Google (M. Ubl, industrialempathy.com)](https://www.industrialempathy.com/posts/design-docs-at-google/), Design Docs at Google (Ubl, 2020), 'The actual design' > 'System-context-diagram'.

Applies: In many docs

Document kind: software design doc.

### google_dd_apis — APIs

> If the system under design exposes an API, then sketching out that API is usually a good idea. In most cases, however, one should withstand the temptation to copy-paste formal interface or data definitions into the doc as these are often verbose, contain unnecessary detail and quickly get out of date. Instead focus on the parts that are relevant to the design and its trade-offs.

Source: [Design Docs at Google (M. Ubl, industrialempathy.com)](https://www.industrialempathy.com/posts/design-docs-at-google/), Design Docs at Google (Ubl, 2020), 'The actual design' > 'APIs'.

Applies: If the system under design exposes an API

Document kind: software design doc.

### google_dd_data_storage — Data storage

> Systems that store data should likely discuss how and in what rough form this happens. Similar to the advice on APIs, and for the same reasons, copy-pasting complete schema definitions should be avoided. Instead focus on the parts that are relevant to the design and its trade-offs.

Source: [Design Docs at Google (M. Ubl, industrialempathy.com)](https://www.industrialempathy.com/posts/design-docs-at-google/), Design Docs at Google (Ubl, 2020), 'The actual design' > 'Data storage'.

Applies: Systems that store data

Document kind: software design doc.

### google_dd_code_and_pseudocode — Code and pseudo-code

> Design docs should rarely contain code, or pseudo-code except in situations where novel algorithms are described. As appropriate, link to prototypes that show the implementability of the design.

Source: [Design Docs at Google (M. Ubl, industrialempathy.com)](https://www.industrialempathy.com/posts/design-docs-at-google/), Design Docs at Google (Ubl, 2020), 'The actual design' > 'Code and pseudo-code'.

Applies: except in situations where novel algorithms are described

Document kind: software design doc.

### google_dd_degree_of_constraint — Degree of constraint

> On one end of the extreme is the “greenfield software project”, where all we know are the goals, and the solution can be whatever makes the most sense. Such a document may be wide-ranging, but it also needs to quickly define a set of rules that allow zooming in on a manageable set of solutions.

Source: [Design Docs at Google (M. Ubl, industrialempathy.com)](https://www.industrialempathy.com/posts/design-docs-at-google/), Design Docs at Google (Ubl, 2020), 'The actual design' > 'Degree of constraint'.

Document kind: software design doc.

### google_dd_alternatives_considered — Alternatives considered

> This section lists alternative designs that would have reasonably achieved similar outcomes. The focus should be on the trade-offs that each respective design makes and how those trade-offs led to the decision to select the design that is the primary topic of the document.

Source: [Design Docs at Google (M. Ubl, industrialempathy.com)](https://www.industrialempathy.com/posts/design-docs-at-google/), Design Docs at Google (Ubl, 2020), 'Alternatives considered'.

Document kind: software design doc.

### google_dd_cross_cutting_concerns — Cross-cutting concerns

> This is where your organization can ensure that certain cross-cutting concerns such as security, privacy, and observability are always taken into consideration. These are often relatively short sections that explain how the design impacts the concern and how the concern is addressed.

Source: [Design Docs at Google (M. Ubl, industrialempathy.com)](https://www.industrialempathy.com/posts/design-docs-at-google/), Design Docs at Google (Ubl, 2020), 'Cross-cutting concerns'.

Applies: In case of dedicated docs for these topics, the central design doc can, of course, reference them instead of going into detail.

Document kind: software design doc.

### google_dd_length — The length of a design doc

> Design docs should be sufficiently detailed but short enough to actually be read by busy people. The sweet spot for a larger project seems to be around 10-20ish pages. If you get way beyond that, it might make sense to split up the problem into more manageable sub problems.

Source: [Design Docs at Google (M. Ubl, industrialempathy.com)](https://www.industrialempathy.com/posts/design-docs-at-google/), Design Docs at Google (Ubl, 2020), 'The length of a design doc'.

Applies: It should also be noted that it is absolutely possible to write a 1-3 page “mini design doc”. This is especially helpful for incremental improvements or sub tasks in an agile project

Document kind: software design doc.

### google_dd_not_implementation_manual — When not to write a design doc.

> A clear indicator that a doc might not be necessary are design docs that are really implementation manuals. If a doc basically says “This is how we are going to implement it” without going into trade-offs, alternatives, and explaining decision making (or if the solution is so obvious as to mean there were no trade-offs), then it would probably have been a better idea to write the actual program right away.

Source: [Design Docs at Google (M. Ubl, industrialempathy.com)](https://www.industrialempathy.com/posts/design-docs-at-google/), Design Docs at Google (Ubl, 2020), 'When not to write a design doc.'.

Document kind: software design doc.

### rfc4101_model_first — Introduction

> One way to reduce this load is to present the reviewer with a MODEL -- a short description of the system in overview form. … The intention is that the model would either be the first section of the protocol document or be a separate document provided with the protocol.

Source: [RFC 4101 — Writing Protocol Models (E. Rescorla)](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc4101), RFC 4101, Section 1.

Document kind: protocol model (overview section or companion document of a protocol specification).

### rfc4101_three_questions — The Purpose of a Protocol Model

> A protocol model needs to answer three basic questions: 1. What problem is the protocol trying to achieve? 2. What messages are being transmitted and what do they mean? 3. What are the important, but unobvious, features of the protocol?

Source: [RFC 4101 — Writing Protocol Models (E. Rescorla)](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc4101), RFC 4101, Section 2.

Document kind: protocol model (overview section or companion document of a protocol specification).

### rfc4101_not_complete_description — The Purpose of a Protocol Model

> The purpose of a protocol model is explicitly not to provide a complete or alternate description of the protocol being discussed. Instead, it is to provide a big picture overview of the protocol so that readers can quickly understand the essential elements of how it works.

Source: [RFC 4101 — Writing Protocol Models (E. Rescorla)](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc4101), RFC 4101, Section 2.

Document kind: protocol model (overview section or companion document of a protocol specification).

### rfc4101_less_is_more — Less is more

> Humans are only capable of keeping a very small number of pieces of information in their head at once. Because we're interested in ensuring that people get the big picture, we have to dispense with a lot of detail. That's good, not bad. The simpler you can make things the better.

Source: [RFC 4101 — Writing Protocol Models (E. Rescorla)](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc4101), RFC 4101, Section 3.1.

Document kind: protocol model (overview section or companion document of a protocol specification).

### rfc4101_abstraction_is_good — Abstraction is good

> A key technique for representing complex systems is to try to abstract away pieces. … Don't be afraid to compress multiple protocol elements into a single abstract piece for pedagogical purposes.

Source: [RFC 4101 — Writing Protocol Models (E. Rescorla)](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc4101), RFC 4101, Section 3.2.

Document kind: protocol model (overview section or companion document of a protocol specification).

### rfc4101_well_chosen_details — A few well-chosen details sometimes help

> Thus, it's often a good approach to talk about the material in the abstract and then provide a concrete description of one specific piece to bring it into focus. Authors should focus on the normal path. Error cases and corner cases should only be discussed where they help illustrate an important point.

Source: [RFC 4101 — Writing Protocol Models (E. Rescorla)](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc4101), RFC 4101, Section 3.3.

Document kind: protocol model (overview section or companion document of a protocol specification).

### rfc4101_visual_form — Writing Protocol Models

> We recommend a presentation format centered around a few key diagrams, with explanatory text for each. These diagrams should be simple and typically consist of "boxes and arrows"

Source: [RFC 4101 — Writing Protocol Models (E. Rescorla)](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc4101), RFC 4101, Section 4.

Document kind: protocol model (overview section or companion document of a protocol specification).

### rfc4101_describe_problem — Describe the problem you're trying to solve

> The most critical task that a protocol model must perform is to explain what the protocol is trying to achieve. … The "Problem" section of the model should start with a short statement of the environments in which the protocol is expected to be used. This section should describe the relevant entities and the likely scenarios under which they would participate in the protocol.

Source: [RFC 4101 — Writing Protocol Models (E. Rescorla)](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc4101), RFC 4101, Section 4.1.

Document kind: protocol model (overview section or companion document of a protocol specification).

### rfc4101_trust_relationships — Describe the problem you're trying to solve

> It is particularly important to lay out the trust relationships between the various parties, as these are often unobvious.

Source: [RFC 4101 — Writing Protocol Models (E. Rescorla)](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc4101), RFC 4101, Section 4.1.

Document kind: protocol model (overview section or companion document of a protocol specification).

### rfc4101_broad_overview — Describe the protocol in broad overview

> This diagram should be accompanied with explanatory text that describes the purpose of each message and the MAJOR data elements. This section SHOULD NOT contain detailed descriptions of the protocol messages or of each data element. In particular, bit diagrams, ASN.1 modules, and XML schema SHOULD NOT be shown.

Source: [RFC 4101 — Writing Protocol Models (E. Rescorla)](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc4101), RFC 4101, Section 4.2.

Document kind: protocol model (overview section or companion document of a protocol specification).

### rfc4101_minimal_state_machines — Describe the protocol in broad overview

> However, such state machines should be kept as minimal as possible. Remember that the purpose is to promote high-level comprehension, not complete understanding.

Source: [RFC 4101 — Writing Protocol Models (E. Rescorla)](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc4101), RFC 4101, Section 4.2.

Applies: In certain cases, it may be helpful to provide a state machine description of the behavior of network elements.

Document kind: protocol model (overview section or companion document of a protocol specification).

### rfc4101_important_features — Describe any important protocol features

> The final section (if there is one) should contain an explanation of any important protocol features that are not obvious from the previous sections.

Source: [RFC 4101 — Writing Protocol Models (E. Rescorla)](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc4101), RFC 4101, Section 4.3.

Applies: If there are no important points to be made, they should not populate this section.

Document kind: protocol model (overview section or companion document of a protocol specification).

Contributes rather than criteria:

- [Design Docs at Google (M. Ubl, industrialempathy.com)](https://www.industrialempathy.com/posts/design-docs-at-google/) (methods): 'The design doc lifecycle' (creation, review, implementation and iteration, maintenance) describes a review process and when to update the doc, not properties of the text.
- [RFC 4101 — Writing Protocol Models (E. Rescorla)](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc4101) (examples): Section 4.1.1 (STUN) and Section 6 onward (IKE and others) are worked protocol models that illustrate the criteria; no reviewer judgement is attached, so none were added as rubric examples.
- [ISO/IEC FDIS 42010:2011 (IEEE P42010/D9) Systems and software engineering — Architecture description (final draft posted on the official ISO/IEC/IEEE 42010 website)](https://web.archive.org/web/20120207051458/http://www.iso-architecture.org/ieee-1471/docs/ISO-IEC-FDIS-42010.pdf) (methods): Clauses 6-7 (architecture frameworks, architecture description languages, viewpoint specification) say how to define viewpoints and frameworks, which is method guidance for building an AD rather than a criterion for judging one.

Gaps: ISO/IEC/IEEE 42010: the 2022 edition (reference iso42010) is paywalled, and ISO OBP is not script-accessible. The quotes come from the 2011 FDIS (IEEE P42010/D9) that the official 42010 website once posted. That link is now 404, so a Wayback copy from 2012-02-07 was used, saved as iso42010_fdis_2011.txt. The 2022 edition reworded and restructured parts of Clause 5, so treat these as 2011 requirements. The site's informal AD overview (iso42010_ads.txt) and the 2022 conceptual-model page (iso42010_cm.txt) were saved but not quoted. Design Docs at Google states 'Rule #1 is: Write them in whatever form makes the most sense for the particular project', so its section criteria are advisory. That rule is kept as criterion google_dd_form. RFC 4101 governs a protocol model, an overview placed first or shipped separately, and not the full specification. No source publishes levels. No examples are attached. None of the verified examples is a 42010 architecture description, a Google design doc or a reviewed protocol model. c14 (the TinyMT32 draft) is an IETF document, but its reviewer comment concerns where a warning sits in the abstract, not RFC 4101's model sections.

## Process templates of the corpus

Sources: [Go design document template (golang/proposal design/TEMPLATE.md)](https://github.com/golang/proposal/blob/master/design/TEMPLATE.md); [Go proposal process (golang/proposal README)](https://github.com/golang/proposal/blob/master/README.md); [Kubernetes KEP template (kubernetes/enhancements NNNN-kep-template)](https://github.com/kubernetes/enhancements/blob/master/keps/NNNN-kep-template/README.md); [kubernetes/enhancements pull request 4439: KEP-127 user namespaces, graduate to beta](https://github.com/kubernetes/enhancements/pull/4439); [Oxide RFD 1 — Requests for Discussion](https://rfd.shared.oxide.computer/rfd/0001); [PEP 1 — PEP Purpose and Guidelines](https://peps.python.org/pep-0001/); [PEP 12 — Sample reStructuredText PEP Template](https://peps.python.org/pep-0012/); [python/peps pull request 4050: PEP 661, add missing sections](https://github.com/python/peps/pull/4050); [python/peps pull request 2997: PEP 705 TypedMapping review](https://github.com/python/peps/pull/2997); [RFC 3552 — Guidelines for Writing RFC Text on Security Considerations](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc3552); [RFC 7322 — RFC Style Guide](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7322); [rust-lang/rfcs pull request 2539: cfg_attr expanding to multiple attributes](https://github.com/rust-lang/rfcs/pull/2539); [rust-lang/rfcs pull request 3013: checking conditional compilation at compile time](https://github.com/rust-lang/rfcs/pull/3013); [Rust RFC template (rust-lang/rfcs 0000-template.md)](https://github.com/rust-lang/rfcs/blob/master/0000-template.md); [Shape Up, Chapter 6: Write the Pitch (R. Singer, Basecamp)](https://basecamp.com/shapeup/1.5-chapter-06); [Shape Up, Principles of Shaping (R. Singer, Basecamp)](https://basecamp.com/shapeup/1.1-chapter-02).

Rubric id `process_templates`: Genre-specific section requirements of the corpus templates (PEP 1/12, Rust RFC, KEP, Go design doc, RFC 7322, RFC 3552, Oxide RFD 1, Shape Up).

### pep1_single_focused_proposal — Start with an idea for Python

> It is highly recommended that a single PEP contain a single key proposal or new idea; the more focused the PEP, the more successful it tends to be.

Source: [PEP 1 — PEP Purpose and Guidelines](https://peps.python.org/pep-0001/), PEP 1, 'Start with an idea for Python'.

Document kind: Python Enhancement Proposal (PEP).

### pep1_acceptance_minimum — PEP Review & Resolution (minimum criteria)

> For a PEP to be accepted it must meet certain minimum criteria. It must be a clear and complete description of the proposed enhancement. The enhancement must represent a net improvement. The proposed implementation, if applicable, must be solid and must not complicate the interpreter unduly.

Source: [PEP 1 — PEP Purpose and Guidelines](https://peps.python.org/pep-0001/), PEP 1, 'PEP Review & Resolution'.

Applies: The proposed implementation, if applicable

Document kind: Python Enhancement Proposal (PEP).

### pep1_preamble — Preamble

> Preamble -- :rfc:`2822` style headers containing meta-data about the PEP, including the PEP number, a short descriptive title (limited to a maximum of 44 characters), the names, and optionally the contact info for each author, etc.

Source: [PEP 1 — PEP Purpose and Guidelines](https://peps.python.org/pep-0001/), PEP 1, 'What belongs in a successful PEP?', item 1.

Document kind: Python Enhancement Proposal (PEP).

### pep1_abstract — Abstract

> Abstract -- a short (~200 word) description of the technical issue being addressed.

Source: [PEP 1 — PEP Purpose and Guidelines](https://peps.python.org/pep-0001/), PEP 1, 'What belongs in a successful PEP?', item 2.

Document kind: Python Enhancement Proposal (PEP).

### pep1_motivation — Motivation

> Motivation -- The motivation is critical for PEPs that want to change the Python language, library, or ecosystem. It should clearly explain why the existing language specification is inadequate to address the problem that the PEP solves. … PEP submissions without sufficient motivation may be rejected.

Source: [PEP 1 — PEP Purpose and Guidelines](https://peps.python.org/pep-0001/), PEP 1, 'What belongs in a successful PEP?', item 3.

Applies: for PEPs that want to change the Python language, library, or ecosystem

Document kind: Python Enhancement Proposal (PEP).

### pep1_specification — Specification

> Specification -- The technical specification should describe the syntax and semantics of any new language feature. The specification should be detailed enough to allow competing, interoperable implementations for at least the current major Python platforms (CPython, Jython, IronPython, PyPy).

Source: [PEP 1 — PEP Purpose and Guidelines](https://peps.python.org/pep-0001/), PEP 1, 'What belongs in a successful PEP?', item 4.

Document kind: Python Enhancement Proposal (PEP).

### pep1_rationale — Rationale

> Rationale -- The rationale fleshes out the specification by describing why particular design decisions were made. It should describe alternate designs that were considered and related work, e.g. how the feature is supported in other languages. The rationale should provide evidence of consensus within the community and discuss important objections or concerns raised during discussion.

Source: [PEP 1 — PEP Purpose and Guidelines](https://peps.python.org/pep-0001/), PEP 1, 'What belongs in a successful PEP?', item 5.

Document kind: Python Enhancement Proposal (PEP).

### pep1_backwards_compatibility — Backwards Compatibility

> Backwards Compatibility -- All PEPs that introduce backwards incompatibilities must include a section describing these incompatibilities and their severity. The PEP must explain how the author proposes to deal with these incompatibilities. PEP submissions without a sufficient backwards compatibility treatise may be rejected outright.

Source: [PEP 1 — PEP Purpose and Guidelines](https://peps.python.org/pep-0001/), PEP 1, 'What belongs in a successful PEP?', item 6.

Applies: All PEPs that introduce backwards incompatibilities

Document kind: Python Enhancement Proposal (PEP).

Examples: `pep661-pr4050-1` ([python/peps pull request 4050: PEP 661, add missing sections](https://github.com/python/peps/pull/4050)).

### pep1_security_implications — Security Implications

> Security Implications -- If there are security concerns in relation to the PEP, those concerns should be explicitly written out to make sure reviewers of the PEP are aware of them.

Source: [PEP 1 — PEP Purpose and Guidelines](https://peps.python.org/pep-0001/), PEP 1, 'What belongs in a successful PEP?', item 7.

Applies: If there are security concerns in relation to the PEP

Document kind: Python Enhancement Proposal (PEP).

Examples: `pep661-pr4050-1` ([python/peps pull request 4050: PEP 661, add missing sections](https://github.com/python/peps/pull/4050)).

### pep1_how_to_teach_this — How to Teach This

> How to Teach This -- For a PEP that adds new functionality or changes language behavior, it is helpful to include a section on how to teach users, new and experienced, how to apply the PEP to their work.

Source: [PEP 1 — PEP Purpose and Guidelines](https://peps.python.org/pep-0001/), PEP 1, 'What belongs in a successful PEP?', item 8.

Applies: For a PEP that adds new functionality or changes language behavior

Document kind: Python Enhancement Proposal (PEP).

Examples: `pep661-pr4050-1` ([python/peps pull request 4050: PEP 661, add missing sections](https://github.com/python/peps/pull/4050)).

### pep1_reference_implementation — Reference Implementation

> The final implementation must include test code and documentation appropriate for either the Python language reference or the standard library reference.

Source: [PEP 1 — PEP Purpose and Guidelines](https://peps.python.org/pep-0001/), PEP 1, 'What belongs in a successful PEP?', item 9.

Applies: The reference implementation must be completed before any PEP is given status "Final", but it need not be completed before the PEP is accepted.

Document kind: Python Enhancement Proposal (PEP).

### pep1_rejected_ideas — Rejected Ideas

> Rejected Ideas -- Throughout the discussion of a PEP, various ideas will be proposed which are not accepted. Those rejected ideas should be recorded along with the reasoning as to why they were rejected.

Source: [PEP 1 — PEP Purpose and Guidelines](https://peps.python.org/pep-0001/), PEP 1, 'What belongs in a successful PEP?', item 10.

Document kind: Python Enhancement Proposal (PEP).

### pep1_open_issues — Open Issues

> Open Issues -- While a PEP is in draft, ideas can come up which warrant further discussion. Those ideas should be recorded so people know that they are being thought about but do not have a concrete resolution.

Source: [PEP 1 — PEP Purpose and Guidelines](https://peps.python.org/pep-0001/), PEP 1, 'What belongs in a successful PEP?', item 11.

Applies: While a PEP is in draft

Document kind: Python Enhancement Proposal (PEP).

Examples: `pep661-pr4050-1` ([python/peps pull request 4050: PEP 661, add missing sections](https://github.com/python/peps/pull/4050)).

### pep1_acknowledgements — Acknowledgements

> Acknowledgements -- Useful to thank and acknowledge people who have helped develop, discuss, or draft the PEP, or for any other purpose.

Source: [PEP 1 — PEP Purpose and Guidelines](https://peps.python.org/pep-0001/), PEP 1, 'What belongs in a successful PEP?', item 12.

Document kind: Python Enhancement Proposal (PEP).

### pep1_footnotes — Footnotes

> Footnotes -- A collection of footnotes cited in the PEP, and a place to list non-inline hyperlink targets.

Source: [PEP 1 — PEP Purpose and Guidelines](https://peps.python.org/pep-0001/), PEP 1, 'What belongs in a successful PEP?', item 13.

Document kind: Python Enhancement Proposal (PEP).

### pep1_change_history — Change History

> Change History -- A summary of major changes the PEP has undergone, based on discussions and feedback.

Source: [PEP 1 — PEP Purpose and Guidelines](https://peps.python.org/pep-0001/), PEP 1, 'What belongs in a successful PEP?', item 14.

Applies: This isn't mandatory, so it's left to the PEP author's discretion

Document kind: Python Enhancement Proposal (PEP).

### pep1_copyright — Copyright/license

> Copyright/license -- Each new PEP must be placed under a dual license of public domain and CC0-1.0-Universal_

Source: [PEP 1 — PEP Purpose and Guidelines](https://peps.python.org/pep-0001/), PEP 1, 'What belongs in a successful PEP?', item 15.

Document kind: Python Enhancement Proposal (PEP).

### pep12_header_fields — Header fields

> For reference, here are all of the possible header fields (everything in brackets should either be replaced or have the field removed if it has a leading ``*`` marking it as optional and it does not apply to your PEP):

Source: [PEP 12 — Sample reStructuredText PEP Template](https://peps.python.org/pep-0012/), PEP 12, 'How to Use This Template'.

Applies: if it has a leading ``*`` marking it as optional and it does not apply to your PEP

Document kind: Python Enhancement Proposal (PEP).

### pep12_section_depth — Section Headings

> You shouldn't have more than five levels of sections in your PEP. If you do, you should consider rewriting it.

Source: [PEP 12 — Sample reStructuredText PEP Template](https://peps.python.org/pep-0012/), PEP 12, 'Section Headings'.

Document kind: Python Enhancement Proposal (PEP).
