### rust_rfc_summary — Summary

> One paragraph explanation of the feature.

Source: [Rust RFC template (rust-lang/rfcs 0000-template.md)](https://github.com/rust-lang/rfcs/blob/master/0000-template.md), Rust RFC template (0000-template.md), section 'Summary'.

Document kind: Rust RFC.

### rust_rfc_motivation — Motivation

> Any changes to Rust should focus on solving a problem that users of Rust are having. This section should explain this problem in detail, including necessary background. It should also contain several specific use cases where this feature can help a user, and explain how it helps.

Source: [Rust RFC template (rust-lang/rfcs 0000-template.md)](https://github.com/rust-lang/rfcs/blob/master/0000-template.md), Rust RFC template (0000-template.md), section 'Motivation'.

Document kind: Rust RFC.

### rust_rfc_guide_level_explanation — Guide-level explanation

> Explain the proposal as if it was already included in the language and you were teaching it to another Rust programmer. That generally means: - Introducing new named concepts. - Explaining the feature largely in terms of examples. - Explaining how Rust programmers should think about the feature, and how it should impact the way they use Rust. It should explain the impact as concretely as possible.

Source: [Rust RFC template (rust-lang/rfcs 0000-template.md)](https://github.com/rust-lang/rfcs/blob/master/0000-template.md), Rust RFC template (0000-template.md), section 'Guide-level explanation'.

Applies: For implementation-oriented RFCs (e.g. for compiler internals), this section should focus on how compiler contributors should think about the change, and give examples of its concrete impact. For policy RFCs, this section should provide an example-driven introduction to the policy, and explain its impact in concrete terms.

Document kind: Rust RFC.

### rust_rfc_reference_level_explanation — Reference-level explanation

> This is the technical portion of the RFC. Explain the design in sufficient detail that: - Its interaction with other features is clear. - It is reasonably clear how the feature would be implemented. - Corner cases are dissected by example. The section should return to the examples given in the previous section, and explain more fully how the detailed proposal makes those examples work.

Source: [Rust RFC template (rust-lang/rfcs 0000-template.md)](https://github.com/rust-lang/rfcs/blob/master/0000-template.md), Rust RFC template (0000-template.md), section 'Reference-level explanation'.

Document kind: Rust RFC.

### rust_rfc_drawbacks — Drawbacks

> Why should we not do this?

Source: [Rust RFC template (rust-lang/rfcs 0000-template.md)](https://github.com/rust-lang/rfcs/blob/master/0000-template.md), Rust RFC template (0000-template.md), section 'Drawbacks'.

Document kind: Rust RFC.

Examples: `rust-rfc-3013-1` ([rust-lang/rfcs pull request 3013: checking conditional compilation at compile time](https://github.com/rust-lang/rfcs/pull/3013)), `rust-rfc-3013-2` ([rust-lang/rfcs pull request 3013: checking conditional compilation at compile time](https://github.com/rust-lang/rfcs/pull/3013)), `rust-rfc-2909-1` ([rust-lang/rfcs pull request 2909: destructuring assignment](https://github.com/rust-lang/rfcs/pull/2909)).

### rust_rfc_rationale_and_alternatives — Rationale and alternatives

> - Why is this design the best in the space of possible designs? - What other designs have been considered and what is the rationale for not choosing them? - What is the impact of not doing this?

Source: [Rust RFC template (rust-lang/rfcs 0000-template.md)](https://github.com/rust-lang/rfcs/blob/master/0000-template.md), Rust RFC template (0000-template.md), section 'Rationale and alternatives'.

Document kind: Rust RFC.

Examples: `rust-rfc-2539-1` ([rust-lang/rfcs pull request 2539: cfg_attr expanding to multiple attributes](https://github.com/rust-lang/rfcs/pull/2539)), `rust-rfc-1861-1` ([rust-lang/rfcs pull request 1861: extern types](https://github.com/rust-lang/rfcs/pull/1861)).

### rust_rfc_library_or_macro — Rationale and alternatives

> If this is a language proposal, could this be done in a library or macro instead? Does the proposed change make Rust code easier or harder to read, understand, and maintain?

Source: [Rust RFC template (rust-lang/rfcs 0000-template.md)](https://github.com/rust-lang/rfcs/blob/master/0000-template.md), Rust RFC template (0000-template.md), section 'Rationale and alternatives'.

Applies: If this is a language proposal

Document kind: Rust RFC.

### rust_rfc_prior_art — Prior art

> Discuss prior art, both the good and the bad, in relation to this proposal.

Source: [Rust RFC template (rust-lang/rfcs 0000-template.md)](https://github.com/rust-lang/rfcs/blob/master/0000-template.md), Rust RFC template (0000-template.md), section 'Prior art'.

Applies: If there is no prior art, that is fine - your ideas are interesting to us whether they are brand new or if it is an adaptation from other languages.

Document kind: Rust RFC.

### rust_rfc_unresolved_questions — Unresolved questions

> - What parts of the design do you expect to resolve through the RFC process before this gets merged? - What parts of the design do you expect to resolve through the implementation of this feature before stabilization? - What related issues do you consider out of scope for this RFC that could be addressed in the future independently of the solution that comes out of this RFC?

Source: [Rust RFC template (rust-lang/rfcs 0000-template.md)](https://github.com/rust-lang/rfcs/blob/master/0000-template.md), Rust RFC template (0000-template.md), section 'Unresolved questions'.

Document kind: Rust RFC.

Examples: `rust-rfc-3641-1` ([rust-lang/rfcs pull request 3641: export_ordinal attribute](https://github.com/rust-lang/rfcs/pull/3641)).

### rust_rfc_future_possibilities — Future possibilities

> Think about what the natural extension and evolution of your proposal would be and how it would affect the language and project as a whole in a holistic way.

Source: [Rust RFC template (rust-lang/rfcs 0000-template.md)](https://github.com/rust-lang/rfcs/blob/master/0000-template.md), Rust RFC template (0000-template.md), section 'Future possibilities'.

Applies: If you have tried and cannot think of any future possibilities, you may simply state that you cannot think of anything.

Document kind: Rust RFC.

### kep_minimum_sections — Fill out this file as best you can.

> At minimum, you should fill in the "Summary" and "Motivation" sections.

Source: [Kubernetes KEP template (kubernetes/enhancements NNNN-kep-template)](https://github.com/kubernetes/enhancements/blob/master/keps/NNNN-kep-template/README.md), KEP template (keps/NNNN-kep-template/README.md), template instructions.

Applies: Avoid getting hung up on specific details and instead aim to get the goals of the KEP clarified and merged quickly. The best way to do this is to just start with the high-level sections and fill out details incrementally in subsequent PRs.

Document kind: Kubernetes Enhancement Proposal (KEP).

Source: [Kubernetes KEP template (kubernetes/enhancements NNNN-kep-template)](https://github.com/kubernetes/enhancements/blob/master/keps/NNNN-kep-template/README.md), KEP template (keps/NNNN-kep-template/README.md), PRR > 'Scalability'.

Applies: For alpha, this section is encouraged: reviewers should consider these questions and attempt to answer them. For beta, this section is required: reviewers must answer these questions.

Document kind: Kubernetes Enhancement Proposal (KEP).

### kep_prr_apiserver_unavailable — How does this feature react if the API server and/or etcd is unavailable?

> How does this feature react if the API server and/or etcd is unavailable?

Source: [Kubernetes KEP template (kubernetes/enhancements NNNN-kep-template)](https://github.com/kubernetes/enhancements/blob/master/keps/NNNN-kep-template/README.md), KEP template (keps/NNNN-kep-template/README.md), PRR > 'Troubleshooting'.

Applies: This section must be completed when targeting beta to a release.

Document kind: Kubernetes Enhancement Proposal (KEP).

### kep_prr_failure_modes — What are other known failure modes?

> What are other known failure modes?

Source: [Kubernetes KEP template (kubernetes/enhancements NNNN-kep-template)](https://github.com/kubernetes/enhancements/blob/master/keps/NNNN-kep-template/README.md), KEP template (keps/NNNN-kep-template/README.md), PRR > 'Troubleshooting'.

Applies: This section must be completed when targeting beta to a release.

Document kind: Kubernetes Enhancement Proposal (KEP).

### kep_prr_slo_not_met — What steps should be taken if SLOs are not being met to determine the problem?

> What steps should be taken if SLOs are not being met to determine the problem?

Source: [Kubernetes KEP template (kubernetes/enhancements NNNN-kep-template)](https://github.com/kubernetes/enhancements/blob/master/keps/NNNN-kep-template/README.md), KEP template (keps/NNNN-kep-template/README.md), PRR > 'Troubleshooting'.

Applies: This section must be completed when targeting beta to a release.

Document kind: Kubernetes Enhancement Proposal (KEP).

### kep_implementation_history — Implementation History

> Major milestones in the lifecycle of a KEP should be tracked in this section.

Source: [Kubernetes KEP template (kubernetes/enhancements NNNN-kep-template)](https://github.com/kubernetes/enhancements/blob/master/keps/NNNN-kep-template/README.md), KEP template (keps/NNNN-kep-template/README.md), 'Implementation History'.

Document kind: Kubernetes Enhancement Proposal (KEP).

### kep_drawbacks — Drawbacks

> Why should this KEP _not_ be implemented?

Source: [Kubernetes KEP template (kubernetes/enhancements NNNN-kep-template)](https://github.com/kubernetes/enhancements/blob/master/keps/NNNN-kep-template/README.md), KEP template (keps/NNNN-kep-template/README.md), 'Drawbacks'.

Document kind: Kubernetes Enhancement Proposal (KEP).

### kep_alternatives — Alternatives

> What other approaches did you consider, and why did you rule them out? These do not need to be as detailed as the proposal, but should include enough information to express the idea and why it was not acceptable.

Source: [Kubernetes KEP template (kubernetes/enhancements NNNN-kep-template)](https://github.com/kubernetes/enhancements/blob/master/keps/NNNN-kep-template/README.md), KEP template (keps/NNNN-kep-template/README.md), 'Alternatives'.

Document kind: Kubernetes Enhancement Proposal (KEP).

### kep_infrastructure_needed — Infrastructure Needed (Optional)

> Use this section if you need things from the project/SIG. Examples include a new subproject, repos requested, or GitHub details.

Source: [Kubernetes KEP template (kubernetes/enhancements NNNN-kep-template)](https://github.com/kubernetes/enhancements/blob/master/keps/NNNN-kep-template/README.md), KEP template (keps/NNNN-kep-template/README.md), 'Infrastructure Needed (Optional)'.

Applies: (Optional)

Document kind: Kubernetes Enhancement Proposal (KEP).

### go_design_abstract — Abstract

> [A short summary of the proposal.]

Source: [Go design document template (golang/proposal design/TEMPLATE.md)](https://github.com/golang/proposal/blob/master/design/TEMPLATE.md), Go design doc template (golang/proposal design/TEMPLATE.md), section 'Abstract'.

Document kind: Go proposal design doc.

### go_design_background — Background

> [An introduction of the necessary background and the problem being solved by the proposed change.]

Source: [Go design document template (golang/proposal design/TEMPLATE.md)](https://github.com/golang/proposal/blob/master/design/TEMPLATE.md), Go design doc template (golang/proposal design/TEMPLATE.md), section 'Background'.

Document kind: Go proposal design doc.

### go_design_proposal — Proposal

> [A precise statement of the proposed change.]

Source: [Go design document template (golang/proposal design/TEMPLATE.md)](https://github.com/golang/proposal/blob/master/design/TEMPLATE.md), Go design doc template (golang/proposal design/TEMPLATE.md), section 'Proposal'.

Document kind: Go proposal design doc.

### go_design_rationale — Rationale

> [A discussion of alternate approaches and the trade offs, advantages, and disadvantages of the specified approach.]

Source: [Go design document template (golang/proposal design/TEMPLATE.md)](https://github.com/golang/proposal/blob/master/design/TEMPLATE.md), Go design doc template (golang/proposal design/TEMPLATE.md), section 'Rationale'.

Document kind: Go proposal design doc.

### go_design_compatibility — Compatibility

> [A discussion of the change with regard to the [compatibility guidelines](https://go.dev/doc/go1compat).]

Source: [Go design document template (golang/proposal design/TEMPLATE.md)](https://github.com/golang/proposal/blob/master/design/TEMPLATE.md), Go design doc template (golang/proposal design/TEMPLATE.md), section 'Compatibility'.

Document kind: Go proposal design doc.

### go_design_implementation — Implementation

> [A description of the steps in the implementation, who will do them, and when. This should include a discussion of how the work fits into [Go's release cycle](https://go.dev/wiki/Go-Release-Cycle).]

Source: [Go design document template (golang/proposal design/TEMPLATE.md)](https://github.com/golang/proposal/blob/master/design/TEMPLATE.md), Go design doc template (golang/proposal design/TEMPLATE.md), section 'Implementation'.

Document kind: Go proposal design doc.

### go_design_open_issues — Open issues (if applicable)

> [A discussion of issues relating to this proposal for which the author does not know the solution.

Source: [Go design document template (golang/proposal design/TEMPLATE.md)](https://github.com/golang/proposal/blob/master/design/TEMPLATE.md), Go design doc template (golang/proposal design/TEMPLATE.md), section 'Open issues (if applicable)'.

Applies: This section may be omitted if there are none.

Document kind: Go proposal design doc.

### go_design_address_concerns — Design Documents

> The design doc should address any specific concerns raised during the initial discussion.

Source: [Go proposal process (golang/proposal README)](https://github.com/golang/proposal/blob/master/README.md), Go proposal README, 'Design Documents'.

Document kind: Go proposal design doc.

### go_design_one_sentence_per_line — Design Documents

> For ease of review with Gerrit, design documents should be wrapped around the 80 column mark. [Each sentence should start on a new line](https://rhodesmill.org/brandon/2012/one-sentence-per-line/) so that comments can be made accurately and the diff kept shorter.

Source: [Go proposal process (golang/proposal README)](https://github.com/golang/proposal/blob/master/README.md), Go proposal README, 'Design Documents'.

Document kind: Go proposal design doc.

### go_language_change_criteria — Language changes

> As explained in the blog entry, language change proposals should - address an important issue for many people, - have minimal impact on everybody else, and - come with a clear and well-understood solution.

Source: [Go proposal process (golang/proposal README)](https://github.com/golang/proposal/blob/master/README.md), Go proposal README, 'Language changes'.

Applies: language change proposals

Document kind: Go language change proposal.

### rfc7322_clear_consistent_readable — RFC Editor's Philosophy

> Make the document as clear, consistent, and readable as possible. - Correct larger content/clarity issues; flag any unclear passages for author review. - Fix inconsistencies (e.g., terms that appear in various forms, text that appears multiple times, or inconsistent capitalization).

Source: [RFC 7322 — RFC Style Guide](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7322), RFC 7322, Section 2.

Applies: If the original wording is a more accurate representation of the technical content being described in the document, it takes precedence over editorial conventions.

Document kind: RFC (RFC Series document / Internet-Draft).

### rfc7322_language — Language

> The RFC publication language is English. Spelling may be either American or British, as long as an individual document is internally consistent.

Source: [RFC 7322 — RFC Style Guide](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7322), RFC 7322, Section 3.1.

Document kind: RFC (RFC Series document / Internet-Draft).

### rfc7322_punctuation — Punctuation

> No overstriking (or underlining) is allowed. * When a sentence ended by a period is immediately followed by another sentence, there must be two blank spaces after the period. * A comma is used before the last item of a series

Source: [RFC 7322 — RFC Style Guide](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7322), RFC 7322, Section 3.2.

Document kind: RFC (RFC Series document / Internet-Draft).

### rfc7322_dns_names_uris — DNS Names and URIs

> DNS names, whether or not in URIs, that are used as generic examples in RFCs should use the particular examples defined in "Reserved Top Level DNS Names" [BCP32], to avoid accidental conflicts.

Source: [RFC 7322 — RFC Style Guide](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7322), RFC 7322, Section 3.3.

Document kind: RFC (RFC Series document / Internet-Draft).

### rfc7322_capitalization — Capitalization

> Capitalization must be consistent within the document and ideally should be consistent with related RFCs.

Source: [RFC 7322 — RFC Style Guide](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7322), RFC 7322, Section 3.4.

Document kind: RFC (RFC Series document / Internet-Draft).

### rfc7322_citations — Citations

> References and citations must match. That is, there must be a reference for each citation used, and vice versa.

Source: [RFC 7322 — RFC Style Guide](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7322), RFC 7322, Section 3.5.

Document kind: RFC (RFC Series document / Internet-Draft).

### rfc7322_abbreviations — Abbreviation Rules

> Abbreviations should be expanded in document titles and upon first use in the document. The full expansion of the text should be followed by the abbreviation itself in parentheses.

Source: [RFC 7322 — RFC Style Guide](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7322), RFC 7322, Section 3.6.

Applies: The exception is an abbreviation that is so common that the readership of RFCs can be expected to recognize it immediately; examples include (but are not limited to) TCP, IP, SNMP, and HTTP.

Document kind: RFC (RFC Series document / Internet-Draft).

### rfc7322_structure_order — Structure of an RFC

> Within the body of the memo, the order shown above is strongly recommended. Exceptions may be questioned. Outside the body of the memo, the order above is required.

Source: [RFC 7322 — RFC Style Guide](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7322), RFC 7322, Section 4.

Document kind: RFC (RFC Series document / Internet-Draft).

### rfc7322_title — Full Title

> Choosing a good title for an RFC can be a challenge. A good title should fairly represent the scope and purpose of the document without being either too general or too specific and lengthy.

Source: [RFC 7322 — RFC Style Guide](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7322), RFC 7322, Section 4.2.

Document kind: RFC (RFC Series document / Internet-Draft).

### rfc7322_abstract — Abstract Section

> Every RFC must have an Abstract that provides a concise and comprehensive overview of the purpose and contents of the entire document, to give a technically knowledgeable reader a general overview of the function of the document.

Source: [RFC 7322 — RFC Style Guide](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7322), RFC 7322, Section 4.3.

Document kind: RFC (RFC Series document / Internet-Draft).

### rfc7322_abstract_self_contained — Abstract Section

> Note also that an Abstract is not a substitute for an Introduction; the RFC should be self-contained as if there were no Abstract. Similarly, the Abstract should be complete in itself. It will appear in isolation in publication announcements and in the online index of RFCs. Therefore, the Abstract must not contain citations.

Source: [RFC 7322 — RFC Style Guide](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7322), RFC 7322, Section 4.3.

Document kind: RFC (RFC Series document / Internet-Draft).

### rfc7322_table_of_contents — Table of Contents Section

> A Table of Contents (TOC) is required in all RFCs. It must be positioned after the Copyright Notice and before the Introduction.

Source: [RFC 7322 — RFC Style Guide](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7322), RFC 7322, Section 4.7.

Document kind: RFC (RFC Series document / Internet-Draft).

### rfc7322_introduction_motivation — Body of the Memo

> Each RFC must include an Introduction section that (among other things) explains the motivation for the RFC and (if appropriate) describes the applicability of the document

Source: [RFC 7322 — RFC Style Guide](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7322), RFC 7322, Section 4.8.

Applies: (if appropriate)

Document kind: RFC (RFC Series document / Internet-Draft).

### rfc7322_body_abstract_separable — Body of the Memo

> The body of the memo and the Abstract must be self-contained and separable. This may result in some duplication of text between the Abstract and the Introduction; this is acceptable.

Source: [RFC 7322 — RFC Style Guide](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7322), RFC 7322, Section 4.8.

Document kind: RFC (RFC Series document / Internet-Draft).

### rfc7322_introduction_first — Introduction Section

> The Introduction section should always be the first section following the TOC (except in the case of MIB module documents).

Source: [RFC 7322 — RFC Style Guide](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7322), RFC 7322, Section 4.8.1.

Applies: (except in the case of MIB module documents)

Document kind: RFC (RFC Series document / Internet-Draft).

### rfc7322_requirements_language — Requirements Language Section

> If this interpretation is used, RFC 2119 must be cited (as specified in RFC 2119) and included as a normative reference. Otherwise, the correct interpretation must be specified in the document.

Source: [RFC 7322 — RFC Style Guide](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7322), RFC 7322, Section 4.8.2.

Applies: Some documents use certain capitalized words ("MUST", "SHOULD", etc.) to specify precise requirement levels for technical features.

Document kind: RFC (RFC Series document / Internet-Draft).

### rfc7322_iana_considerations — IANA Considerations Section

> It is helpful for authors to clearly identify where text should be updated to reflect the newly assigned values. For example, the use of "TBD1", "TBD2", etc., is recommended in the IANA Considerations section and in the body of the memo.

Source: [RFC 7322 — RFC Style Guide](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7322), RFC 7322, Sections 4 and 4.8.3.

Applies: The RFC Editor will remove an IANA Considerations section that says there are no IANA considerations (although such a section is required in the Internet-Draft preceding the RFC).

Document kind: RFC (RFC Series document / Internet-Draft).

### rfc7322_internationalization — Internationalization Considerations Section

> All RFCs that deal with internationalization issues should have a section describing those issues

Source: [RFC 7322 — RFC Style Guide](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7322), RFC 7322, Section 4.8.4.

Applies: All RFCs that deal with internationalization issues

Document kind: RFC (RFC Series document / Internet-Draft).

### rfc7322_security_considerations — Security Considerations Section

> All RFCs must contain a section that discusses the security considerations relevant to the specification; see "Guidelines for Writing RFC Text on Security Considerations" [BCP72] for more information.

Source: [RFC 7322 — RFC Style Guide](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7322), RFC 7322, Section 4.8.5.

Document kind: RFC (RFC Series document / Internet-Draft).

### rfc7322_references — References Section

> Reference lists must indicate whether each reference is normative or informative, where normative references are essential to implementing or understanding the content of the RFC and informative references provide additional information.

Source: [RFC 7322 — RFC Style Guide](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7322), RFC 7322, Section 4.8.6.

Document kind: RFC (RFC Series document / Internet-Draft).

### rfc7322_reference_style — References Section

> The reference list is solely for recording reference entries. Introductory text is not allowed. The RFC style allows the use of any of a variety of reference styles, as long as they are used consistently within a document.

Source: [RFC 7322 — RFC Style Guide](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7322), RFC 7322, Section 4.8.6.

Document kind: RFC (RFC Series document / Internet-Draft).

### rfc7322_acknowledgements — Acknowledgements Section

> This optional section may be used instead of, or in addition to, a Contributors section. It is often used by authors to publicly thank those who have provided feedback regarding a document and to note any documents from which text was borrowed.

Source: [RFC 7322 — RFC Style Guide](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7322), RFC 7322, Section 4.10.

Applies: This optional section

Document kind: RFC (RFC Series document / Internet-Draft).

### rfc7322_contributors — Contributors Section

> This optional section acknowledges those who have made significant contributions to the document.

Source: [RFC 7322 — RFC Style Guide](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7322), RFC 7322, Section 4.11.

Applies: This optional section

Document kind: RFC (RFC Series document / Internet-Draft).

### rfc7322_authors_addresses — "Author's Address" or "Authors' Addresses" Section

> This required section gives contact information for the author(s) listed in the first-page header. Contact information must include a long-lived email address and optionally may include a postal address and/or telephone number.

Source: [RFC 7322 — RFC Style Guide](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7322), RFC 7322, Section 4.12.

Document kind: RFC (RFC Series document / Internet-Draft).

### rfc3552_threat_description — Writing Security Considerations Sections

> There should be a clear description of the kinds of threats on the described protocol or technology. This should be approached as an effort to perform "due diligence" in describing all known or foreseeable risks and threats to potential implementers and users.

Source: [RFC 3552 — Guidelines for Writing RFC Text on Security Considerations](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc3552), RFC 3552, Section 5 'Writing Security Considerations Sections'.

Document kind: Security Considerations section of an RFC.

### rfc3552_attack_scope — Writing Security Considerations Sections

> Authors MUST describe 1. which attacks are out of scope (and why!) 2. which attacks are in-scope 2.1 and the protocol is susceptible to 2.2 and the protocol protects against

Source: [RFC 3552 — Guidelines for Writing RFC Text on Security Considerations](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc3552), RFC 3552, Section 5 'Writing Security Considerations Sections'.

Document kind: Security Considerations section of an RFC.

### rfc3552_attack_forms — Writing Security Considerations Sections

> At least the following forms of attack MUST be considered: eavesdropping, replay, message insertion, deletion, modification, and man-in-the-middle. Potential denial of service attacks MUST be identified as well.

Source: [RFC 3552 — Guidelines for Writing RFC Text on Security Considerations](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc3552), RFC 3552, Section 5 'Writing Security Considerations Sections'.

Document kind: Security Considerations section of an RFC.

### rfc3552_cryptographic_protection — Writing Security Considerations Sections

> it should be clearly indicated which portions of the data are protected and what the protections are (i.e., integrity only, confidentiality, and/or endpoint authentication, etc.). Some indication should also be given to what sorts of attacks the cryptographic protection is susceptible. Data which should be held secret (keying material, random seeds, etc.) should be clearly labeled.

Source: [RFC 3552 — Guidelines for Writing RFC Text on Security Considerations](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc3552), RFC 3552, Section 5 'Writing Security Considerations Sections'.

Applies: If the protocol incorporates cryptographic protection mechanisms

Document kind: Security Considerations section of an RFC.

### rfc3552_authentication_assumptions — Writing Security Considerations Sections

> the security of the authentication method MUST be clearly specified. That is, authors MUST document the assumptions that the security of this authentication method is predicated upon.

Source: [RFC 3552 — Guidelines for Writing RFC Text on Security Considerations](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc3552), RFC 3552, Section 5 'Writing Security Considerations Sections'.

Applies: If the technology involves authentication, particularly user-host authentication

Document kind: Security Considerations section of an RFC.

### rfc3552_lower_layer_security — Writing Security Considerations Sections

> It is insufficient to simply state that one's protocol should be run over some lower layer security protocol. If a system relies upon lower layer security services for security, the protections those … services are expected to provide MUST be clearly specified. In addition, the resultant properties of the combined system need to be specified.

Source: [RFC 3552 — Guidelines for Writing RFC Text on Security Considerations](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc3552), RFC 3552, Section 5 'Writing Security Considerations Sections'.

Applies: If a system relies upon lower layer security services for security

Document kind: Security Considerations section of an RFC.

### rfc3552_threat_environment — Writing Security Considerations Sections

> The threat environment addressed by the Security Considerations section MUST at a minimum include deployment across the global Internet across multiple administrative boundaries without assuming that firewalls are in place, even if only to provide justification for why such consideration is out of scope for the protocol.

Source: [RFC 3552 — Guidelines for Writing RFC Text on Security Considerations](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc3552), RFC 3552, Section 5 'Writing Security Considerations Sections'.

Document kind: Security Considerations section of an RFC.

### rfc3552_residual_risk — Writing Security Considerations Sections

> There should be a clear description of the residual risk to the user or operator of that protocol after threat mitigation has been deployed.

Source: [RFC 3552 — Guidelines for Writing RFC Text on Security Considerations](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc3552), RFC 3552, Section 5 'Writing Security Considerations Sections'.

Document kind: Security Considerations section of an RFC.

### rfc3552_misapplication — Writing Security Considerations Sections

> There should also be some discussion of potential security risks arising from potential misapplications of the protocol or technology described in the RFC.

Source: [RFC 3552 — Guidelines for Writing RFC Text on Security Considerations](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc3552), RFC 3552, Section 5 'Writing Security Considerations Sections'.

Document kind: Security Considerations section of an RFC.

### oxide_rfd_timely_not_polished — Requests for Discussion (RFC 3 spirit)

> Notes are encouraged to be timely rather than polished. Philosophical positions without examples or other specifics, specific suggestions or implementation techniques without introductory or background explication, and explicit questions without any attempted answers are all acceptable.

Source: [Oxide RFD 1 — Requests for Discussion](https://rfd.shared.oxide.computer/rfd/0001), Oxide RFD 1, introduction (quoting RFC 3).

Applies: Depending on their state, RFDs may be quickly iterated on in a branch, discussed actively as part of a pull request to be merged, or commented upon after having been published.

Document kind: Oxide Request for Discussion (RFD).

### oxide_rfd_viable_options — Insights to include in RFDs

> Document the viable options (or a subset of options if we can easily rule some out) and the benefits and drawbacks of each option.

Source: [Oxide RFD 1 — Requests for Discussion](https://rfd.shared.oxide.computer/rfd/0001), Oxide RFD 1, 'Insights to include in RFDs'.

Applies: When writing RFDs for technical and core product determination

Document kind: Oxide Request for Discussion (RFD).

### oxide_rfd_reasoning — Insights to include in RFDs

> Document our reasoning including data and references wherever possible; making it easy for our future selves (and those who join in the future) to understand the decisions we’ve made and why.

Source: [Oxide RFD 1 — Requests for Discussion](https://rfd.shared.oxide.computer/rfd/0001), Oxide RFD 1, 'Insights to include in RFDs'.

Applies: When writing RFDs for technical and core product determination

Document kind: Oxide Request for Discussion (RFD).

### oxide_rfd_determination — Insights to include in RFDs

> Document the determination, see [rfd5] for a definition of determination.

Source: [Oxide RFD 1 — Requests for Discussion](https://rfd.shared.oxide.computer/rfd/0001), Oxide RFD 1, 'Insights to include in RFDs'.

Applies: When writing RFDs for technical and core product determination

Document kind: Oxide Request for Discussion (RFD).

### oxide_rfd_economic — Economic

> What is the relative cost of this idea (and/or alternative ideas) in terms of time and money? … What are the implications that this will have on the company over time, both short-term and long-term?

Source: [Oxide RFD 1 — Requests for Discussion](https://rfd.shared.oxide.computer/rfd/0001), Oxide RFD 1, 'Insights to include in RFDs' > business and customer considerations.

Applies: While not applicable in every case

Document kind: Oxide Request for Discussion (RFD).

### oxide_rfd_customer_outcomes — Customer outcomes and impact

> What does this idea look like in terms of how it impacts a customer? … What are the outcomes for customers if we implement this idea versus alternatives?

Source: [Oxide RFD 1 — Requests for Discussion](https://rfd.shared.oxide.computer/rfd/0001), Oxide RFD 1, 'Insights to include in RFDs' > business and customer considerations.

Applies: While not applicable in every case

Document kind: Oxide Request for Discussion (RFD).

### oxide_rfd_performance — Performance

> How do the trade-offs of this determination impact performace? Is it visible to customers? Is it a regression? Are there any benchmarks that helped lead to the determination?

Source: [Oxide RFD 1 — Requests for Discussion](https://rfd.shared.oxide.computer/rfd/0001), Oxide RFD 1, 'Insights to include in RFDs' > business and customer considerations.

Applies: While not applicable in every case

Document kind: Oxide Request for Discussion (RFD).

### oxide_rfd_security — Security

> How to the trade-offs of this determination impact the security of the product?

Source: [Oxide RFD 1 — Requests for Discussion](https://rfd.shared.oxide.computer/rfd/0001), Oxide RFD 1, 'Insights to include in RFDs' > business and customer considerations.

Applies: While not applicable in every case

Document kind: Oxide Request for Discussion (RFD).

### oxide_rfd_depth_judgement — Insights to include in RFDs

> Not every RFD is equal. Some have more sense of urgency and therefore might not be as rigorous as others. Some are a documentation of our knowledge thus far on a given topic. All things being said, use your best judgement when it comes to the level of depth to devote to an RFD.

Source: [Oxide RFD 1 — Requests for Discussion](https://rfd.shared.oxide.computer/rfd/0001), Oxide RFD 1, 'Insights to include in RFDs'.

Document kind: Oxide Request for Discussion (RFD).

### oxide_rfd_metadata — RFD Metadata and State

> At the start of every RFD document, we’d like to include a brief amount of metadata.

Source: [Oxide RFD 1 — Requests for Discussion](https://rfd.shared.oxide.computer/rfd/0001), Oxide RFD 1, 'RFD Metadata and State'.

Document kind: Oxide Request for Discussion (RFD).

### oxide_rfd_ideation_scope — RFD Metadata and State (ideation)

> A document in the ideation state contains only a description of the topic that the RFD will cover, providing an indication of the scope of the eventual RFD. … It is critical that RFDs in the ideation state are clear and narrowly defined.

Source: [Oxide RFD 1 — Requests for Discussion](https://rfd.shared.oxide.computer/rfd/0001), Oxide RFD 1, 'RFD Metadata and State'.

Applies: RFDs in the ideation state

Document kind: Oxide Request for Discussion (RFD).

### shapeup_pitch_five_ingredients — Write the Pitch

> There are five ingredients that we always want to include in a pitch:

Source: [Shape Up, Chapter 6: Write the Pitch (R. Singer, Basecamp)](https://basecamp.com/shapeup/1.5-chapter-06), Shape Up ch. 6 'Write the Pitch', introduction.

Document kind: Shape Up pitch.

### shapeup_pitch_problem — Ingredient 1. Problem

> Problem — The raw idea, a use case, or something we’ve seen that motivates us to work on this … It’s critical to always present both a problem and a solution together. … The best problem definition consists of a single specific story that shows why the status quo doesn’t work.

Source: [Shape Up, Chapter 6: Write the Pitch (R. Singer, Basecamp)](https://basecamp.com/shapeup/1.5-chapter-06), Shape Up ch. 6, 'Ingredient 1. Problem'.

Applies: How far you have to go to spell out the problem will depend on how much context you share with the people reading the write-up.

Document kind: Shape Up pitch.

### shapeup_pitch_appetite — Ingredient 2. Appetite

> Appetite — How much time we want to spend and how that constrains the solution … Stating the appetite in the pitch prevents unproductive conversations.

Source: [Shape Up, Chapter 6: Write the Pitch (R. Singer, Basecamp)](https://basecamp.com/shapeup/1.5-chapter-06), Shape Up ch. 6, 'Ingredient 2. Appetite'.

Document kind: Shape Up pitch.

### shapeup_pitch_solution — Ingredient 3. Solution

> Solution — The core elements we came up with, presented in a form that’s easy for people to immediately understand … A problem without a solution is unshaped work.

Source: [Shape Up, Chapter 6: Write the Pitch (R. Singer, Basecamp)](https://basecamp.com/shapeup/1.5-chapter-06), Shape Up ch. 6, 'Ingredient 3. Solution'.

Document kind: Shape Up pitch.

### shapeup_pitch_help_them_see_it — Help them see it

> We need to stay high level, but add a little more concreteness than when we worked alone or with a partner. People who read the pitch and look at the drawings without much context need to “get” the idea. We need more concreteness, but we don’t want to over-specify the design with wireframes or high-fidelity mocks.

Source: [Shape Up, Chapter 6: Write the Pitch (R. Singer, Basecamp)](https://basecamp.com/shapeup/1.5-chapter-06), Shape Up ch. 6, 'Help them see it'.

Document kind: Shape Up pitch.

### shapeup_pitch_rabbit_holes — Ingredient 4. Rabbit Holes

> Rabbit holes — Details about the solution worth calling out to avoid problems … Sometimes addressing a rabbit hole just requires a few lines of text.

Source: [Shape Up, Chapter 6: Write the Pitch (R. Singer, Basecamp)](https://basecamp.com/shapeup/1.5-chapter-06), Shape Up ch. 6, ingredient list and 'Ingredient 4. Rabbit holes'.

Document kind: Shape Up pitch.

### shapeup_pitch_no_gos — Ingredient 5. No Gos

> No-gos — Anything specifically excluded from the concept: functionality or use cases we intentionally aren’t covering to fit the appetite or make the problem tractable … Lastly if there’s anything we’re not doing in this concept, it’s good to mention it here.

Source: [Shape Up, Chapter 6: Write the Pitch (R. Singer, Basecamp)](https://basecamp.com/shapeup/1.5-chapter-06), Shape Up ch. 6, ingredient list and 'Ingredient 5. No Gos'.

Document kind: Shape Up pitch.

### shapeup_right_level_of_abstraction — Principles of Shaping

> When we shape the work, we need to do it at the right level of abstraction: not too vague and not too concrete.

Source: [Shape Up, Principles of Shaping (R. Singer, Basecamp)](https://basecamp.com/shapeup/1.1-chapter-02), Shape Up ch. 2 'Principles of Shaping', introduction.

Document kind: Shape Up pitch.

### shapeup_wireframes_too_concrete — Wireframes are too concrete

> When design leaders go straight to wireframes or high-fidelity mockups, they define too much detail too early. This leaves designers no room for creativity.

Source: [Shape Up, Principles of Shaping (R. Singer, Basecamp)](https://basecamp.com/shapeup/1.1-chapter-02), Shape Up ch. 2, 'Wireframes are too concrete'.

Document kind: Shape Up pitch.

### shapeup_words_too_abstract — Words are too abstract

> On the other end of the spectrum, projects that are too vague don’t work either. When a project is defined in a few words, nobody knows what it means. … Team members don’t have enough information to make trade-offs. They don’t know what to include or leave out.

Source: [Shape Up, Principles of Shaping (R. Singer, Basecamp)](https://basecamp.com/shapeup/1.1-chapter-02), Shape Up ch. 2, 'Words are too abstract'.

Document kind: Shape Up pitch.

### shapeup_rough — Property 1: It’s rough

> Work in the shaping stage is rough. Everyone can tell by looking at it that it’s unfinished. They can see the open spaces where their contributions will go. Work that’s too fine, too early commits everyone to the wrong details.

Source: [Shape Up, Principles of Shaping (R. Singer, Basecamp)](https://basecamp.com/shapeup/1.1-chapter-02), Shape Up ch. 2, 'Property 1: It’s rough'.

Document kind: Shape Up pitch.

### shapeup_solved — Property 2: It’s solved

> Despite being rough and unfinished, shaped work has been thought through. All the main elements of the solution are there at the macro level and they connect together. The work isn’t specified down to individual tasks, but the overall solution is spelled out. … Any open questions or rabbit holes we could see up front have been removed to reduce the project’s risk.

Source: [Shape Up, Principles of Shaping (R. Singer, Basecamp)](https://basecamp.com/shapeup/1.1-chapter-02), Shape Up ch. 2, 'Property 2: It’s solved'.

Document kind: Shape Up pitch.

### shapeup_bounded — Property 3: It’s bounded

> Lastly, shaped work indicates what not to do. It tells the team where to stop. There’s a specific appetite—the amount of time the team is allowed to spend on the project.

Source: [Shape Up, Principles of Shaping (R. Singer, Basecamp)](https://basecamp.com/shapeup/1.1-chapter-02), Shape Up ch. 2, 'Property 3: It’s bounded'.

Document kind: Shape Up pitch.

Contributes rather than criteria:

- [PEP 1 — PEP Purpose and Guidelines](https://peps.python.org/pep-0001/) (methods): PEP workflow, status transitions and the role of PEP editors/sponsors describe the review process, not the document's content.
- [Go proposal process (golang/proposal README)](https://github.com/golang/proposal/blob/master/README.md) (methods): The proposal process (brief issue first, design doc only when asked, weekly review meetings, minutes) is a triage method; it implies the condition that most proposals need no design doc at all.
- [Oxide RFD 1 — Requests for Discussion](https://rfd.shared.oxide.computer/rfd/0001) (methods): The RFD life-cycle (states prediscussion/ideation/discussion/published/committed/abandoned, branches, PRs) is process; only the state-dependent expectations were kept as conditions.
- [Shape Up, Chapter 6: Write the Pitch (R. Singer, Basecamp)](https://basecamp.com/shapeup/1.5-chapter-06) (methods): 'Ready to present' and 'How we do it in Basecamp' (post pitches asynchronously, comment to poke holes) describe the betting process.
- [Kubernetes KEP template (kubernetes/enhancements NNNN-kep-template)](https://github.com/kubernetes/enhancements/blob/master/keps/NNNN-kep-template/README.md) (methods): The Release Signoff Checklist and kep.yaml metadata are process gates checked by tooling, not prose criteria.
- [python/peps pull request 4050: PEP 661, add missing sections](https://github.com/python/peps/pull/4050) (examples): Example b17: PEP 661 adding the PEP 1 sections it lacked.
- [kubernetes/enhancements pull request 4439: KEP-127 user namespaces, graduate to beta](https://github.com/kubernetes/enhancements/pull/4439) (examples): Examples b3 and b18: PRR reviewer asked for a measurable rollback signal and a filled Upgrade/Downgrade section.
- [rust-lang/rfcs pull request 2539: cfg_attr expanding to multiple attributes](https://github.com/rust-lang/rfcs/pull/2539) (examples): Example c0: alternative added to 'Rationale and alternatives' after a reviewer named one.
- [rust-lang/rfcs pull request 3013: checking conditional compilation at compile time](https://github.com/rust-lang/rfcs/pull/3013) (examples): Examples c2 and c3: 'Drawbacks' revised from 'no known drawbacks' to named costs after review.
- [python/peps pull request 2997: PEP 705 TypedMapping review](https://github.com/python/peps/pull/2997) (examples): Example b9 (implementation detail cut from a PEP) is not tied by its own material to any PEP 1/PEP 12 section text, so it was not attached.

Gaps: Templates were fetched from their master branches on 2026-10-24. PEP 1 is the copy saved earlier by this project (ext/b/raw/gh/pep-0001.rst, copied into f1), and templates change over time. Examples attached: b17 to the four PEP 1 items PEP 661 added (its brief_source names PEP 1 items 6, 7, 8 and 11); b3 to the KEP PRR rollback-metrics question; b18 to Upgrade / Downgrade Strategy; b4 to Integration tests (its brief_source is that template comment, though no reviewer named the fault); c0 and c1 to rust_rfc_rationale_and_alternatives; c2, c3 and c4 to rust_rfc_drawbacks; c5 to rust_rfc_unresolved_questions. In 2017 the Rust template heading for c1 was 'Alternatives'; it is now 'Rationale and alternatives'. c4 is the author's own revision ('Add actual drawbacks') with no reviewer prompt. Not attached, because their own material does not tie them to a template criterion: b9 (PEP 705), where the reviewer's 'too much implementation detail' is not PEP 1 Backwards Compatibility text; b10 (Rust RFC 2947), where the reviewer's 'implementation detail' objection runs against the template's 'It is reasonably clear how the feature would be implemented'; c14, c21 and c22 (IETF drafts), whose reviewers do not invoke RFC 7322 or RFC 3552. Omitted: RFC 7322 parts the RFC Editor supplies (4.1 first-page header, 4.4 notes, 4.5 status, 4.6 copyright) and the 4.8.6.x reference-format subsections; the Go 2 language-change questionnaire (go2-language-changes.md, not fetched); the KEP Release Signoff Checklist and kep.yaml; Shape Up ch. 5 'Risks and Rabbit Holes' (not fetched; ch. 6 says little about rabbit holes beyond the ingredient line). Shape Up ch. 2 (existing reference shapeup-principles) is included alongside ch. 6. Section descriptions that are only bracketed prompts (PEP 12, Go TEMPLATE.md) are quoted as written, brackets included. KEP PRR criteria take the question heading as their name and quote, with the template comment appended after ' … ' where there is one. No levels: KEP alpha/beta/GA are feature-maturity stages, not quality levels, and none of these sources scores documents.

## Analytic writing rubrics

Sources: [AAC&U VALUE Rubric: Written Communication](https://www.aacu.org/initiatives/value-initiative/value-rubrics/value-rubrics-written-communication); [6+1 Trait Writing model (Education Northwest)](https://educationnorthwest.org/traits).

Rubric id `analytic_writing_rubrics`: Analytic writing rubrics: AAC&U Written Communication VALUE rubric and 6+1 Trait Writing rubric (grades 3–12), as published.

### aacu_context_purpose — Context of and Purpose for Writing

> Includes considerations of audience, purpose, and the circumstances surrounding the writing task(s).

Source: [AAC&U VALUE Rubric: Written Communication](https://www.aacu.org/initiatives/value-initiative/value-rubrics/value-rubrics-written-communication), Written Communication VALUE Rubric, p. 2 rubric table, row "Context of and Purpose for Writing"; quote from table row description; PDF archived at https://web.archive.org/web/20210918161231/https://www.aacu.org/sites/default/files/files/VALUE/WrittenCommunication.pdf.

Published levels (1 to 4) are not reproduced here because of the source's licence; a local copy in restricted/levels.json is used when present.

### aacu_content_development — Content Development

> The ways in which the text explores and represents its topic in relation to its audience and purpose.

Source: [AAC&U VALUE Rubric: Written Communication](https://www.aacu.org/initiatives/value-initiative/value-rubrics/value-rubrics-written-communication), Written Communication VALUE Rubric, p. 2 rubric table, row "Content Development"; quote from Glossary, "Content Development" (the table row has no description); PDF archived at https://web.archive.org/web/20210918161231/https://www.aacu.org/sites/default/files/files/VALUE/WrittenCommunication.pdf.

Published levels (1 to 4) are not reproduced here because of the source's licence; a local copy in restricted/levels.json is used when present.

### aacu_genre_conventions — Genre and Disciplinary Conventions

> Formal and informal rules inherent in the expectations for writing in particular forms and/or academic fields (please see glossary).

Source: [AAC&U VALUE Rubric: Written Communication](https://www.aacu.org/initiatives/value-initiative/value-rubrics/value-rubrics-written-communication), Written Communication VALUE Rubric, p. 2 rubric table, row "Genre and Disciplinary Conventions"; quote from table row description; PDF archived at https://web.archive.org/web/20210918161231/https://www.aacu.org/sites/default/files/files/VALUE/WrittenCommunication.pdf.

Published levels (1 to 4) are not reproduced here because of the source's licence; a local copy in restricted/levels.json is used when present.

### aacu_sources_evidence — Sources and Evidence

> Evidence: Source material that is used to extend, in purposeful ways, writers' ideas in a text. [...] Sources: Texts (written, oral, behavioral, visual, or other) that writers draw on as they work for a variety of purposes -- to extend, argue with, develop, define, or shape their ideas, for example.

Source: [AAC&U VALUE Rubric: Written Communication](https://www.aacu.org/initiatives/value-initiative/value-rubrics/value-rubrics-written-communication), Written Communication VALUE Rubric, p. 2 rubric table, row "Sources and Evidence"; quote from Glossary, "Evidence" and "Sources" (the table row has no description); PDF archived at https://web.archive.org/web/20210918161231/https://www.aacu.org/sites/default/files/files/VALUE/WrittenCommunication.pdf.

Published levels (1 to 4) are not reproduced here because of the source's licence; a local copy in restricted/levels.json is used when present.

### aacu_syntax_mechanics — Control of Syntax and Mechanics

The source states no definition; it publishes levels only.

Source: [AAC&U VALUE Rubric: Written Communication](https://www.aacu.org/initiatives/value-initiative/value-rubrics/value-rubrics-written-communication), Written Communication VALUE Rubric, p. 2 rubric table, row "Control of Syntax and Mechanics"; quote from table row has no description and the glossary has no entry; see levels; PDF archived at https://web.archive.org/web/20210918161231/https://www.aacu.org/sites/default/files/files/VALUE/WrittenCommunication.pdf.

Published levels (1 to 4) are not reproduced here because of the source's licence; a local copy in restricted/levels.json is used when present.

### six_traits_ideas — Ideas

> Does the writer engage the reader with fresh information or perspective on a focused topic?

Source: [6+1 Trait Writing model (Education Northwest)](https://educationnorthwest.org/traits), 6+1 Trait Writing Rubrics: Grades 3–12 (© 2021 Education Northwest; "refined rubrics" released 2018), trait IDEAS, pp. 2–3, quote is the trait's "KEY QUESTION", levels are the unlabeled first row; https://educationnorthwest.org/sites/default/files/resources/traits-rubrics-3-12.pdf.

Published levels (1 to 6) are not reproduced here because of the source's licence; a local copy in restricted/levels.json is used when present.

### six_traits_organization — Organization

> Does the organizational structure enhance the ideas and make them easier to understand?

Source: [6+1 Trait Writing model (Education Northwest)](https://educationnorthwest.org/traits), 6+1 Trait Writing Rubrics: Grades 3–12 (© 2021 Education Northwest; "refined rubrics" released 2018), trait ORGANIZATION, pp. 4–5, quote is the trait's "KEY QUESTION", levels are the unlabeled first row; https://educationnorthwest.org/sites/default/files/resources/traits-rubrics-3-12.pdf.

Published levels (1 to 6) are not reproduced here because of the source's licence; a local copy in restricted/levels.json is used when present.

### six_traits_voice — Voice

> Is the voice appropriate to the task, purpose, and audience?

Source: [6+1 Trait Writing model (Education Northwest)](https://educationnorthwest.org/traits), 6+1 Trait Writing Rubrics: Grades 3–12 (© 2021 Education Northwest; "refined rubrics" released 2018), trait VOICE, pp. 6, quote is the trait's "KEY QUESTION", levels are the unlabeled first row; https://educationnorthwest.org/sites/default/files/resources/traits-rubrics-3-12.pdf.

### six_traits_word_choice — Word Choice

> Does the language convey precise and compelling meaning and/or create a vivid picture for the reader?

Source: [6+1 Trait Writing model (Education Northwest)](https://educationnorthwest.org/traits), 6+1 Trait Writing Rubrics: Grades 3–12 (© 2021 Education Northwest; "refined rubrics" released 2018), trait WORD CHOICE, pp. 7–8, quote is the trait's "KEY QUESTION", levels are the unlabeled first row; https://educationnorthwest.org/sites/default/files/resources/traits-rubrics-3-12.pdf.

Published levels (1 to 6) are not reproduced here because of the source's licence; a local copy in restricted/levels.json is used when present.

### six_traits_sentence_fluency — Sentence Fluency

> Does the writer control sentences so the text flows smoothly when read aloud?

Source: [6+1 Trait Writing model (Education Northwest)](https://educationnorthwest.org/traits), 6+1 Trait Writing Rubrics: Grades 3–12 (© 2021 Education Northwest; "refined rubrics" released 2018), trait SENTENCE FLUENCY, pp. 9–10, quote is the trait's "KEY QUESTION", levels are the unlabeled first row; https://educationnorthwest.org/sites/default/files/resources/traits-rubrics-3-12.pdf.

Published levels (1 to 6) are not reproduced here because of the source's licence; a local copy in restricted/levels.json is used when present.

### six_traits_conventions — Conventions

> How well does the text illustrate the writer’s proficiency with grade-appropriate conventions?

Source: [6+1 Trait Writing model (Education Northwest)](https://educationnorthwest.org/traits), 6+1 Trait Writing Rubrics: Grades 3–12 (© 2021 Education Northwest; "refined rubrics" released 2018), trait CONVENTIONS, pp. 11–12, quote is the trait's "KEY QUESTION", levels are the unlabeled first row; https://educationnorthwest.org/sites/default/files/resources/traits-rubrics-3-12.pdf.

Applies: (Note: For the trait of conventions, grade level matters. Expectations should be based on grade level and include only skills that have been taught.)

Published levels (1 to 6) are not reproduced here because of the source's licence; a local copy in restricted/levels.json is used when present.

### six_traits_presentation — Presentation

> Is the finished piece easy to read, polished in presentation, and pleasing to the eye?

Source: [6+1 Trait Writing model (Education Northwest)](https://educationnorthwest.org/traits), 6+1 Trait Writing Rubrics: Grades 3–12 (© 2021 Education Northwest; "refined rubrics" released 2018), trait PRESENTATION, pp. 13–14, quote is the trait's "KEY QUESTION", levels are the unlabeled first row; https://educationnorthwest.org/sites/default/files/resources/traits-rubrics-3-12.pdf.

Published levels (1 to 6) are not reproduced here because of the source's licence; a local copy in restricted/levels.json is used when present.

Gaps: AAC&U: aacu.org now serves the rubric only through a store checkout, so the PDF was taken from the Wayback Machine copy of the official file (sites/default/files/files/VALUE/WrittenCommunication.pdf, captured 2021-09-18; it says "all 15 of the VALUE rubrics", the live page says 16). The PDF carries a large "Preview" watermark, whose letters were dropped by font height; cell text was rebuilt from pdftotext word boxes (aacu_parsed.json). The rubric gives no description or glossary entry for "Control of Syntax and Mechanics", so that criterion has quote null and only its levels. Level 0 is not a published level; the rubric only encourages a zero below Benchmark (scale_note). 6+1 Traits: Education Northwest publishes no prose trait definitions in the rubric; each trait's "KEY QUESTION" is used as the quote, the unlabeled first row as the trait levels, and the lettered rows (A–F) are kept as sub_criteria with their own verbatim levels. Cells were rebuilt from pdftotext word boxes (six_traits_2021_parsed.json) because the six-column layout interleaves in plain text; line-final hyphens and slashes were joined (e.g. "domain- specific" -> "domain-specific"). The 2021 PDF has no sub-row E under Conventions (A–D, then F) and a Conventions level-3 cell that ends with a stray editorial note ("” please include grade level specific considerations"); both are kept verbatim. The rubric is written for grades 3–12 student writing; its handwriting, title, font and white-space rows rarely apply to plan documents, but nothing was removed. Older Education Northwest rubrics (2010 5-point, 2013 6-point) were also downloaded (raw/) but not used; the 2021 file is the one the official page links. No examples attached: none of the verified examples comes from either rubric.

## Plain language and style

Sources: [Diátaxis: a systematic approach to technical documentation authoring (D. Procida)](https://diataxis.fr/); [Federal Plain Language Guidelines (PLAIN, plainlanguage.gov)](https://www.plainlanguage.gov/guidelines/); [Google developer documentation style guide](https://developers.google.com/style); [Google for Developers. Technical Writing One](https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one); [Google for Developers. Technical Writing Two: Organizing large documents](https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/two/large-docs); [GOV.UK content and publishing guidance: Writing guidelines (Writing to GOV.UK standards)](https://guidance.publishing.service.gov.uk/writing-to-gov-uk-standards/writing-guidelines/); [Microsoft Writing Style Guide](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/style-guide/welcome/); [Narratology. Plain language ISO 24495 (quotes ISO 24495-1:2023 clause 3.1 and the four governing principles)](https://narratology.co.uk/plain-language-iso-24495-narratology/); [U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission. A Plain English Handbook: How to create clear SEC disclosure documents](https://www.sec.gov/pdf/handbook.pdf); [Write the Docs: Documentation principles](https://www.writethedocs.org/guide/writing/docs-principles/).

Rubric id `plain_language_and_style`: Plain-language and style guidance, item by item: ISO 24495-1, Federal Plain Language Guidelines, SEC Plain English Handbook, Google Technical Writing One/Two, GOV.UK, Microsoft, Write the Docs, Diátaxis.

### iso24495_relevant — Principle 1: relevant

> Principle 1: Readers get what they need (relevant).

Source: [Narratology. Plain language ISO 24495 (quotes ISO 24495-1:2023 clause 3.1 and the four governing principles)](https://narratology.co.uk/plain-language-iso-24495-narratology/), ISO 24495-1:2023 governing principles, as quoted on narratology.co.uk "Plain language ISO 24495" (section "Plain language governing principles"); ISO OBP preview not used (requires accepting ISO terms of use; scripted fetch returned 403); quote_source_ref narratology-iso24495.

### iso24495_findable — Principle 2: findable

> Principle 2: Readers can easily find what they need (findable).

Source: [Narratology. Plain language ISO 24495 (quotes ISO 24495-1:2023 clause 3.1 and the four governing principles)](https://narratology.co.uk/plain-language-iso-24495-narratology/), ISO 24495-1:2023 governing principles, as quoted on narratology.co.uk "Plain language ISO 24495" (section "Plain language governing principles"); ISO OBP preview not used (requires accepting ISO terms of use; scripted fetch returned 403); quote_source_ref narratology-iso24495.

### iso24495_understandable — Principle 3: understandable

> Principle 3: Readers can easily understand what they find (understandable).

Source: [Narratology. Plain language ISO 24495 (quotes ISO 24495-1:2023 clause 3.1 and the four governing principles)](https://narratology.co.uk/plain-language-iso-24495-narratology/), ISO 24495-1:2023 governing principles, as quoted on narratology.co.uk "Plain language ISO 24495" (section "Plain language governing principles"); ISO OBP preview not used (requires accepting ISO terms of use; scripted fetch returned 403); quote_source_ref narratology-iso24495.

### iso24495_usable — Principle 4: usable

> Principle 4: Readers can easily use the information (usable).

Source: [Narratology. Plain language ISO 24495 (quotes ISO 24495-1:2023 clause 3.1 and the four governing principles)](https://narratology.co.uk/plain-language-iso-24495-narratology/), ISO 24495-1:2023 governing principles, as quoted on narratology.co.uk "Plain language ISO 24495" (section "Plain language governing principles"); ISO OBP preview not used (requires accepting ISO terms of use; scripted fetch returned 403); quote_source_ref narratology-iso24495.

### fplg_audience — Identify and write for your audience

> Identifying your audience will do more than ensure that you write clearly. It will also help you focus on the audience’s needs.

Source: [Federal Plain Language Guidelines (PLAIN, plainlanguage.gov)](https://www.plainlanguage.gov/guidelines/), Federal Plain Language Guidelines (March 2011, Rev. 1 May 2011), I. Think about your audience > a. Identify and write for your audience, printed p. 2 (PDF p. 8).

### fplg_separate_audiences — Address separate audiences separately

> Documents that mix material intended for different audiences may confuse readers. By addressing different audiences in the same place, you make it harder for each audience to find the material that applies to them.

Source: [Federal Plain Language Guidelines (PLAIN, plainlanguage.gov)](https://www.plainlanguage.gov/guidelines/), Federal Plain Language Guidelines (March 2011, Rev. 1 May 2011), I. Think about your audience > b. Address separate audiences separately, printed p. 3 (PDF p. 9).

Applies: Many documents address more than one audience.

### fplg_organize_needs — Organize to meet your readers’ needs

> Think through the questions your audience is likely to ask and then organize your material in the order they’d ask them.

Source: [Federal Plain Language Guidelines (PLAIN, plainlanguage.gov)](https://www.plainlanguage.gov/guidelines/), Federal Plain Language Guidelines (March 2011, Rev. 1 May 2011), II. Organize > a. Organize to meet your readers’ needs, printed p. 6 (PDF p. 12).

### fplg_one_person — Address one person, not a group

> Remember that even though your document may affect a thousand or a million people, you are speaking to the one person who is reading it.

Source: [Federal Plain Language Guidelines (PLAIN, plainlanguage.gov)](https://www.plainlanguage.gov/guidelines/), Federal Plain Language Guidelines (March 2011, Rev. 1 May 2011), II. Organize > b. Address one person, not a group, printed p. 10 (PDF p. 16).

Examples: `fplg-1` ([Federal Plain Language Guidelines (PLAIN, plainlanguage.gov)](https://www.plainlanguage.gov/guidelines/)).

### fplg_headings — Use lots of useful headings

> The best-organized document will still be difficult for users to follow if they can’t see how it’s organized. An effective way to reveal your document’s organization is to use lots of useful headings.

Source: [Federal Plain Language Guidelines (PLAIN, plainlanguage.gov)](https://www.plainlanguage.gov/guidelines/), Federal Plain Language Guidelines (March 2011, Rev. 1 May 2011), II. Organize > c. Use lots of useful headings, printed p. 11 (PDF p. 17).

### fplg_short_sections — Write short sections

> Short sections break up material so it appears easier to comprehend. Long, dense sections with no white space are visually unappealing, and give the impression your document is difficult to understand.

Source: [Federal Plain Language Guidelines (PLAIN, plainlanguage.gov)](https://www.plainlanguage.gov/guidelines/), Federal Plain Language Guidelines (March 2011, Rev. 1 May 2011), II. Organize > d. Write short sections, printed p. 15 (PDF p. 21).

### fplg_active_voice — Use active voice

> Active voice makes it clear who is supposed to do what. It eliminates ambiguity about responsibilities.

Source: [Federal Plain Language Guidelines (PLAIN, plainlanguage.gov)](https://www.plainlanguage.gov/guidelines/), Federal Plain Language Guidelines (March 2011, Rev. 1 May 2011), III. Write your document > a. Words > 1. Verbs > i. Use active voice, printed p. 20 (PDF p. 26).

### fplg_simple_words — Use short, simple words

> In making your word choices, pick the familiar or frequently used word over the unusual or obscure.

Source: [Federal Plain Language Guidelines (PLAIN, plainlanguage.gov)](https://www.plainlanguage.gov/guidelines/), Federal Plain Language Guidelines (March 2011, Rev. 1 May 2011), III. Write your document > a. Words > 3. Other word issues > i. Use short, simple words, printed p. 36 (PDF p. 42).

Examples: `fplg-2` ([Federal Plain Language Guidelines (PLAIN, plainlanguage.gov)](https://www.plainlanguage.gov/guidelines/)).

### fplg_omit_words — Omit unnecessary words

> To address the problem, writers must become more critical of their own writing. They must consider whether they need every word.

Source: [Federal Plain Language Guidelines (PLAIN, plainlanguage.gov)](https://www.plainlanguage.gov/guidelines/), Federal Plain Language Guidelines (March 2011, Rev. 1 May 2011), III. Write your document > a. Words > 3. Other word issues > ii. Omit unnecessary words, printed p. 38 (PDF p. 44).

### fplg_definitions — Dealing with definitions

> We have ONE rule for dealing with definitions: use them rarely.

Source: [Federal Plain Language Guidelines (PLAIN, plainlanguage.gov)](https://www.plainlanguage.gov/guidelines/), Federal Plain Language Guidelines (March 2011, Rev. 1 May 2011), III. Write your document > a. Words > 3. Other word issues > iii. Dealing with definitions, printed p. 41 (PDF p. 47).

### fplg_same_term — Use the same term consistently for a specific thought or object

> You will confuse your audience if you use different terms for the same concept.

Source: [Federal Plain Language Guidelines (PLAIN, plainlanguage.gov)](https://www.plainlanguage.gov/guidelines/), Federal Plain Language Guidelines (March 2011, Rev. 1 May 2011), III. Write your document > a. Words > 3. Other word issues > iv. Use the same term consistently for a specific thought or object, printed p. 45 (PDF p. 51).

### fplg_jargon — Avoid legal, foreign, and technical jargon

> Jargon is unnecessarily complicated, technical language used to impress, rather than to inform, your audience.

Source: [Federal Plain Language Guidelines (PLAIN, plainlanguage.gov)](https://www.plainlanguage.gov/guidelines/), Federal Plain Language Guidelines (March 2011, Rev. 1 May 2011), III. Write your document > a. Words > 3. Other word issues > v. Avoid legal, foreign, and technical jargon, printed p. 46 (PDF p. 52).

Applies: When we say not to use jargon, we’re not advocating leaving out necessary technical terms; we are saying to make sure your other language is as clear as possible.

### fplg_short_sentences — Write short sentences

> Express only one idea in each sentence. Long, complicated sentences often mean that you aren’t sure about what you want to say.

Source: [Federal Plain Language Guidelines (PLAIN, plainlanguage.gov)](https://www.plainlanguage.gov/guidelines/), Federal Plain Language Guidelines (March 2011, Rev. 1 May 2011), III. Write your document > b. Sentences > 1. Write short sentences, printed p. 50 (PDF p. 56).

### fplg_main_idea_first — Place the main idea before exceptions and conditions

> Material is much easier to follow if you start with the main idea and then cover exceptions and conditions.

Source: [Federal Plain Language Guidelines (PLAIN, plainlanguage.gov)](https://www.plainlanguage.gov/guidelines/), Federal Plain Language Guidelines (March 2011, Rev. 1 May 2011), III. Write your document > b. Sentences > 4. Place the main idea before exceptions and conditions, printed p. 56 (PDF p. 62).

### fplg_topic_sentence — Have a topic sentence

> Establish a context for your audience before you provide them with the details.

Source: [Federal Plain Language Guidelines (PLAIN, plainlanguage.gov)](https://www.plainlanguage.gov/guidelines/), Federal Plain Language Guidelines (March 2011, Rev. 1 May 2011), III. Write your document > c. Paragraphs > 1. Have a topic sentence, printed p. 63 (PDF p. 69).

### fplg_transitions — Use transition words

> But a transition word or phrase (usually in the topic sentence) clearly tells the audience whether the paragraph expands on the paragraph before, contrasts with it, or takes a completely different direction.

Source: [Federal Plain Language Guidelines (PLAIN, plainlanguage.gov)](https://www.plainlanguage.gov/guidelines/), Federal Plain Language Guidelines (March 2011, Rev. 1 May 2011), III. Write your document > c. Paragraphs > 2. Use transition words, printed p. 64 (PDF p. 70).

### fplg_short_paragraphs — Write short paragraphs

> Long paragraphs discourage your audience from even trying to understand your material. Short paragraphs are easier to read and understand.

Source: [Federal Plain Language Guidelines (PLAIN, plainlanguage.gov)](https://www.plainlanguage.gov/guidelines/), Federal Plain Language Guidelines (March 2011, Rev. 1 May 2011), III. Write your document > c. Paragraphs > 3. Write short paragraphs, printed p. 66 (PDF p. 72).

### fplg_one_topic — Cover only one topic in each paragraph

> Limit each paragraph or section to one topic to make it easier for your audience to understand your information.

Source: [Federal Plain Language Guidelines (PLAIN, plainlanguage.gov)](https://www.plainlanguage.gov/guidelines/), Federal Plain Language Guidelines (March 2011, Rev. 1 May 2011), III. Write your document > c. Paragraphs > 4. Cover only one topic in each paragraph, printed p. 68 (PDF p. 74).

### fplg_examples — Use examples

> Examples help you clarify complex concepts, even in regulations. They are an ideal way to help your readers.

Source: [Federal Plain Language Guidelines (PLAIN, plainlanguage.gov)](https://www.plainlanguage.gov/guidelines/), Federal Plain Language Guidelines (March 2011, Rev. 1 May 2011), III. Write your document > d. Other aids to clarity > 1. Use examples, printed p. 70 (PDF p. 76).

Applies: The more complex the concept you are writing about, the more you should consider using an example.

### fplg_lists — Use lists

> Vertical lists highlight a series of requirements or other information in a visually clear way.

Source: [Federal Plain Language Guidelines (PLAIN, plainlanguage.gov)](https://www.plainlanguage.gov/guidelines/), Federal Plain Language Guidelines (March 2011, Rev. 1 May 2011), III. Write your document > d. Other aids to clarity > 2. Use lists, printed p. 71 (PDF p. 77).

### fplg_tables — Use tables to make complex material easier to understand

> Tables help your audience see relationships that are often times hidden in dense text.

Source: [Federal Plain Language Guidelines (PLAIN, plainlanguage.gov)](https://www.plainlanguage.gov/guidelines/), Federal Plain Language Guidelines (March 2011, Rev. 1 May 2011), III. Write your document > d. Other aids to clarity > 3. Use tables to make complex material easier to understand, printed p. 74 (PDF p. 80).

### fplg_illustrations — Consider using illustrations

> However, even in these documents, you can use illustrations to good effect.

Source: [Federal Plain Language Guidelines (PLAIN, plainlanguage.gov)](https://www.plainlanguage.gov/guidelines/), Federal Plain Language Guidelines (March 2011, Rev. 1 May 2011), III. Write your document > d. Other aids to clarity > 4. Consider using illustrations, printed p. 77 (PDF p. 83).

### fplg_emphasis — Use emphasis to highlight important concepts

> Use bold and italics to make important concepts stand out. While it is difficult to use these techniques in regulations, emphasis helps bring out important points in other documents. Limit emphasis to important information, otherwise you’ll dilute its impact.

Source: [Federal Plain Language Guidelines (PLAIN, plainlanguage.gov)](https://www.plainlanguage.gov/guidelines/), Federal Plain Language Guidelines (March 2011, Rev. 1 May 2011), III. Write your document > d. Other aids to clarity > 5. Use emphasis to highlight important concepts, printed p. 82 (PDF p. 88).

### fplg_cross_refs — Minimize cross-references

> Numerous cross-references can confuse users and make them less attentive to your message.

Source: [Federal Plain Language Guidelines (PLAIN, plainlanguage.gov)](https://www.plainlanguage.gov/guidelines/), Federal Plain Language Guidelines (March 2011, Rev. 1 May 2011), III. Write your document > d. Other aids to clarity > 6. Minimize cross-references, printed p. 83 (PDF p. 89).

### fplg_design — Design your document for easy reading

> Documents that are easy on the eye are far easier to understand than more traditional styles.

Source: [Federal Plain Language Guidelines (PLAIN, plainlanguage.gov)](https://www.plainlanguage.gov/guidelines/), Federal Plain Language Guidelines (March 2011, Rev. 1 May 2011), III. Write your document > d. Other aids to clarity > 7. Design your document for easy reading, printed p. 88 (PDF p. 94).

### sec_active_voice — Use the active voice with strong verbs

> The plodding verbosity of most disclosure documents makes readers yearn for clear words and short sentences. The quickest fix lies in using the active voice with strong verbs.

Source: [U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission. A Plain English Handbook: How to create clear SEC disclosure documents](https://www.sec.gov/pdf/handbook.pdf), A Plain English Handbook (SEC, 1998), Chapter 6 Writing in Plain English > Use the active voice with strong verbs, printed p. 19 (PDF p. 25).

Examples: `sec-plain-english-1` ([U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission. A Plain English Handbook: How to create clear SEC disclosure documents](https://www.sec.gov/pdf/handbook.pdf)).

### sec_personal_pronouns — Try personal pronouns

> No matter how sophisticated your audience is, if you use personal pronouns the clarity of your writing will dramatically improve.

Source: [U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission. A Plain English Handbook: How to create clear SEC disclosure documents](https://www.sec.gov/pdf/handbook.pdf), A Plain English Handbook (SEC, 1998), Chapter 6 Writing in Plain English > Try personal pronouns, printed p. 22 (PDF p. 28).

Examples: `sec-plain-english-2` ([U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission. A Plain English Handbook: How to create clear SEC disclosure documents](https://www.sec.gov/pdf/handbook.pdf)).

### sec_abstractions — Bring abstractions down to earth

> And yet, it’s far easier to comprehend a concept or a situation when your mind can form images.

Source: [U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission. A Plain English Handbook: How to create clear SEC disclosure documents](https://www.sec.gov/pdf/handbook.pdf), A Plain English Handbook (SEC, 1998), Chapter 6 Writing in Plain English > Bring abstractions down to earth, printed p. 23 (PDF p. 29).

Examples: `sec-plain-english-3` ([U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission. A Plain English Handbook: How to create clear SEC disclosure documents](https://www.sec.gov/pdf/handbook.pdf)).

### sec_superfluous — Omit superfluous words

> Words are superfluous when they can be replaced with fewer words that mean the same thing.

Source: [U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission. A Plain English Handbook: How to create clear SEC disclosure documents](https://www.sec.gov/pdf/handbook.pdf), A Plain English Handbook (SEC, 1998), Chapter 6 Writing in Plain English > Omit superfluous words, printed p. 25 (PDF p. 31).

Examples: `sec-plain-english-4` ([U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission. A Plain English Handbook: How to create clear SEC disclosure documents](https://www.sec.gov/pdf/handbook.pdf)).

### sec_positive — Write in the “positive”

> Positive sentences are shorter and easier to understand than their negative counterparts.

Source: [U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission. A Plain English Handbook: How to create clear SEC disclosure documents](https://www.sec.gov/pdf/handbook.pdf), A Plain English Handbook (SEC, 1998), Chapter 6 Writing in Plain English > Write in the “positive”, printed p. 27 (PDF p. 33).

### sec_short_sentences — Use short sentences

> The longer and more complex a sentence, the harder it is for readers to understand any single portion of it.

Source: [U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission. A Plain English Handbook: How to create clear SEC disclosure documents](https://www.sec.gov/pdf/handbook.pdf), A Plain English Handbook (SEC, 1998), Chapter 6 Writing in Plain English > Use short sentences, printed p. 28 (PDF p. 34).

Examples: `sec-plain-english-5` ([U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission. A Plain English Handbook: How to create clear SEC disclosure documents](https://www.sec.gov/pdf/handbook.pdf)), `sec-plain-english-6` ([U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission. A Plain English Handbook: How to create clear SEC disclosure documents](https://www.sec.gov/pdf/handbook.pdf)).

### sec_jargon — Replace jargon and legalese with short, common words

> Ruthlessly eliminate jargon and legalese. Instead, use short, common words to get your points across.

Source: [U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission. A Plain English Handbook: How to create clear SEC disclosure documents](https://www.sec.gov/pdf/handbook.pdf), A Plain English Handbook (SEC, 1998), Chapter 6 Writing in Plain English > Replace jargon and legalese with short, common words, printed p. 30 (PDF p. 36).

Applies: In those instances where there is no plain English alternative, explain what the term means when you first use it.

Examples: `sec-plain-english-7` ([U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission. A Plain English Handbook: How to create clear SEC disclosure documents](https://www.sec.gov/pdf/handbook.pdf)).

### sec_svo — Keep the subject, verb, and object close together

> The natural word order of English speakers is subject-verb-object. Your sentences will be clearer if you follow this order as closely as possible.

Source: [U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission. A Plain English Handbook: How to create clear SEC disclosure documents](https://www.sec.gov/pdf/handbook.pdf), A Plain English Handbook (SEC, 1998), Chapter 6 Writing in Plain English > Keep the subject, verb, and object close together, printed p. 32 (PDF p. 38).

### sec_if_then — Write using “if-then” conditionals
