> Conditional statements are very common in disclosure documents— although they are rarely written that way. When we rewrote the last example as a conditional, we followed the natural English word order very closely.

Source: [U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission. A Plain English Handbook: How to create clear SEC disclosure documents](https://www.sec.gov/pdf/handbook.pdf), A Plain English Handbook (SEC, 1998), Chapter 6 Writing in Plain English > Write using “if-then” conditionals, printed p. 33 (PDF p. 39).

### sec_parallel — Keep your sentence structure parallel

> A long sentence often fails without a parallel structure. Parallelism simply means ensuring a list or series of items is presented using parallel parts of speech, such as nouns or verbs.

Source: [U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission. A Plain English Handbook: How to create clear SEC disclosure documents](https://www.sec.gov/pdf/handbook.pdf), A Plain English Handbook (SEC, 1998), Chapter 6 Writing in Plain English > Keep your sentence structure parallel, printed p. 34 (PDF p. 40).

### sec_respectively — Steer clear of “respectively”

> Whenever you use “respectively,” you force your reader to go back and match up what belongs to what.

Source: [U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission. A Plain English Handbook: How to create clear SEC disclosure documents](https://www.sec.gov/pdf/handbook.pdf), A Plain English Handbook (SEC, 1998), Chapter 6 Writing in Plain English > Steer clear of “respectively”, printed p. 35 (PDF p. 41).

### sec_hierarchy — Hierarchy

> Much like an outline, a document’s hierarchy shows how you’ve organized the information and helps the reader to understand the relationship between different levels of information.

Source: [U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission. A Plain English Handbook: How to create clear SEC disclosure documents](https://www.sec.gov/pdf/handbook.pdf), A Plain English Handbook (SEC, 1998), Chapter 7 Designing the Document > Hierarchy (first of the chapter's five basic design elements), printed p. 39 (PDF p. 45).

### sec_emphasis — Emphasizing text

> Whatever method you choose to highlight information, use it consistently throughout your document so your readers can recognize how you flag important information.

Source: [U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission. A Plain English Handbook: How to create clear SEC disclosure documents](https://www.sec.gov/pdf/handbook.pdf), A Plain English Handbook (SEC, 1998), Chapter 7 Designing the Document > Typography > Emphasizing text, printed p. 43 (PDF p. 49).

Examples: `sec-plain-english-8` ([U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission. A Plain English Handbook: How to create clear SEC disclosure documents](https://www.sec.gov/pdf/handbook.pdf)).

### sec_paragraph_length — Keep paragraph length relatively short

> To reduce dense text, keep paragraphs as short as possible.

Source: [U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission. A Plain English Handbook: How to create clear SEC disclosure documents](https://www.sec.gov/pdf/handbook.pdf), A Plain English Handbook (SEC, 1998), Chapter 7 Designing the Document > Layout > Keep paragraph length relatively short, printed p. 48 (PDF p. 54).

### sec_tables — Use tables to increase clarity

> Use tables to increase clarity and cut down text. Tables often convey information more quickly and clearly than text.

Source: [U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission. A Plain English Handbook: How to create clear SEC disclosure documents](https://www.sec.gov/pdf/handbook.pdf), A Plain English Handbook (SEC, 1998), Chapter 7 Designing the Document > Layout > Use tables to increase clarity, printed p. 48 (PDF p. 54).

### gtw_scope — State your document's scope

> A good document begins by defining its scope. [...] A better document additionally defines its non-scope—the topics not covered that the target audience might reasonably expect your document to cover.

Source: [Google for Developers. Technical Writing One](https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one), Technical Writing One > Documents > State your document's scope (https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one/documents).

### gtw_state_audience — State your audience

> A good document explicitly specifies its audience. [...] Beyond the audience's role, a good audience declaration might also specify any prerequisite knowledge or experience.

Source: [Google for Developers. Technical Writing One](https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one), Technical Writing One > Documents > State your audience (https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one/documents).

### gtw_key_points_first — Summarize key points at the start

> Imagine that your peers might only read the first paragraph of your document. Therefore, ensure that the start of your document answers your readers' essential questions.

Source: [Google for Developers. Technical Writing One](https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one), Technical Writing One > Documents > Summarize key points at the start (https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one/documents).

Examples: `google-tw1-1` ([Google for Developers. Technical Writing One](https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one)).

### gtw_write_for_audience — Write for your audience

> After defining the audience's needs, organize the document to help readers get the information they need.

Source: [Google for Developers. Technical Writing One](https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one), Technical Writing One > Documents > Write for your audience (https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one/documents).

### gtw_define_audience — Define your audience

> Begin by identifying your audience's role(s).

Source: [Google for Developers. Technical Writing One](https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one), Technical Writing One > Audience > Define your audience (https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one/audience).

### gtw_needs_to_learn — Determine what your audience needs to learn

> Write down a list of everything your target audience needs to learn to accomplish goals.

Source: [Google for Developers. Technical Writing One](https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one), Technical Writing One > Audience > Determine what your audience needs to learn (https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one/audience).

### gtw_fit_audience — Fit documentation to your audience

> Writing to meet your audience's needs requires unselfish empathy. You must create explanations that satisfy your audience's curiosity rather than your own.

Source: [Google for Developers. Technical Writing One](https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one), Technical Writing One > Audience > Fit documentation to your audience (https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one/audience).

Examples: `google-tw1-2` ([Google for Developers. Technical Writing One](https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one)).

### gtw_define_terms — Define new or unfamiliar terms

> When writing or editing, learn to recognize terms that might be unfamiliar to some or all of your target audience. [...] If your document is introducing the term, define the term.

Source: [Google for Developers. Technical Writing One](https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one), Technical Writing One > Words > Define new or unfamiliar terms (https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one/words).

### gtw_terms_consistently — Use terms consistently

> The moral: apply the same unambiguous word or term consistently throughout your document.

Source: [Google for Developers. Technical Writing One](https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one), Technical Writing One > Words > Use terms consistently (https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one/words).

### gtw_ambiguous_pronouns — Recognize ambiguous pronouns

> In many cases, you should simply avoid the pronoun and just reuse the noun. However, the utility of a pronoun sometimes outweighs its risk (as in this sentence).

Source: [Google for Developers. Technical Writing One](https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one), Technical Writing One > Words > Recognize ambiguous pronouns (https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one/words).

Examples: `google-tw1-3` ([Google for Developers. Technical Writing One](https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one)).

### gtw_active_voice — Prefer active voice to passive voice

> Use the active voice most of the time. Use the passive voice sparingly.

Source: [Google for Developers. Technical Writing One](https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one), Technical Writing One > Active voice vs. passive voice > Prefer active voice to passive voice (https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one/active-voice).

### gtw_strong_verbs — Choose strong verbs

> To engage and educate readers, choose precise, strong, specific verbs.

Source: [Google for Developers. Technical Writing One](https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one), Technical Writing One > Clear sentences > Choose strong verbs (https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one/clear-sentences).

### gtw_adjectives_adverbs — Minimize certain adjectives and adverbs (optional)

> That's because adjectives and adverbs tend to be too loosely defined and subjective for technical readers. Worse, adjectives and adverbs can make technical documentation sound dangerously like marketing material.

Source: [Google for Developers. Technical Writing One](https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one), Technical Writing One > Clear sentences > Minimize certain adjectives and adverbs (optional) (https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one/clear-sentences).

Examples: `google-tw1-4` ([Google for Developers. Technical Writing One](https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one)).

### gtw_single_idea — Focus each sentence on a single idea

> Focus each sentence on a single idea, thought, or concept.

Source: [Google for Developers. Technical Writing One](https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one), Technical Writing One > Short sentences > Focus each sentence on a single idea (https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one/short-sentences).

### gtw_sentences_to_lists — Convert some long sentences to lists

> Inside many long technical sentences is a list yearning to break free.

Source: [Google for Developers. Technical Writing One](https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one), Technical Writing One > Short sentences > Convert some long sentences to lists (https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one/short-sentences).

### gtw_extraneous_words — Eliminate or reduce extraneous words

> Many sentences contain filler—textual junk food that consumes space without nourishing the reader.

Source: [Google for Developers. Technical Writing One](https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one), Technical Writing One > Short sentences > Eliminate or reduce extraneous words (https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one/short-sentences).

Examples: `google-tw1-5` ([Google for Developers. Technical Writing One](https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one)).

### gtw_list_type — Choose the correct type of list

> Use a bulleted list for unordered items; use a numbered list for ordered items.

Source: [Google for Developers. Technical Writing One](https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one), Technical Writing One > Lists and tables > Choose the correct type of list (https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one/lists-and-tables).

### gtw_parallel_lists — Keep list items parallel

> Effective lists are parallel; defective lists tend to be nonparallel.

Source: [Google for Developers. Technical Writing One](https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one), Technical Writing One > Lists and tables > Keep list items parallel (https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one/lists-and-tables).

### gtw_useful_tables — Create useful tables

> Label each column with a meaningful header. Don't make readers guess what each column holds. Avoid putting too much text into a table cell.

Source: [Google for Developers. Technical Writing One](https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one), Technical Writing One > Lists and tables > Create useful tables (https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one/lists-and-tables).

### gtw_introduce_lists — Introduce each list and table

> We recommend introducing each list and table with a sentence that tells readers what the list or table represents. In other words, give the list or table context.

Source: [Google for Developers. Technical Writing One](https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one), Technical Writing One > Lists and tables > Introduce each list and table (https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one/lists-and-tables).

### gtw_opening_sentence — Write a great opening sentence

> The opening sentence is the most important sentence of any paragraph. [...] Good opening sentences establish the paragraph's central point.

Source: [Google for Developers. Technical Writing One](https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one), Technical Writing One > Paragraphs > Write a great opening sentence (https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one/paragraphs).

Examples: `google-tw1-6` ([Google for Developers. Technical Writing One](https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one)).

### gtw_single_topic — Focus each paragraph on a single topic

> A paragraph should represent an independent unit of logic. Restrict each paragraph to the current topic.

Source: [Google for Developers. Technical Writing One](https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one), Technical Writing One > Paragraphs > Focus each paragraph on a single topic (https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one/paragraphs).

Examples: `google-tw1-7` ([Google for Developers. Technical Writing One](https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one)).

### gtw_paragraph_length — Don't make paragraphs too long or too short

> Readers generally welcome paragraphs containing three to five sentences, but will avoid paragraphs containing more than about seven sentences. [...] Conversely, don't make paragraphs too short.

Source: [Google for Developers. Technical Writing One](https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one), Technical Writing One > Paragraphs > Don't make paragraphs too long or too short (https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one/paragraphs).

### gtw_what_why_how — Answer what, why, and how

> Good paragraphs answer the following three questions: What are you trying to tell your reader? Why is it important for the reader to know this? How should the reader use this knowledge? Alternatively, how should the reader know your point to be true?

Source: [Google for Developers. Technical Writing One](https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one), Technical Writing One > Paragraphs > Answer what, why, and how (https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one/paragraphs).

Examples: `google-tw1-8` ([Google for Developers. Technical Writing One](https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one)).

### gtw_outline — Outline a document

> Starting with a structured, high-level outline can help you group topics and determine where more detail is needed.

Source: [Google for Developers. Technical Writing Two: Organizing large documents](https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/two/large-docs), Technical Writing Two > Organizing large documents > Organize a document > Outline a document (https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/two/large-docs).

Examples: `google-tw2-1` ([Google for Developers. Technical Writing Two: Organizing large documents](https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/two/large-docs)).

### gtw_introduce — Introduce a document

> If readers of your documentation can't find relevance in the subject, they are likely to ignore it. To set the ground rules for your users, we recommend providing an introduction that includes the following information: What the document covers. What prior knowledge you expect readers to have. What the document doesn't cover.

Source: [Google for Developers. Technical Writing Two: Organizing large documents](https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/two/large-docs), Technical Writing Two > Organizing large documents > Organize a document > Introduce a document (https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/two/large-docs).

Examples: `google-tw2-2` ([Google for Developers. Technical Writing Two: Organizing large documents](https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/two/large-docs)), `google-tw2-3` ([Google for Developers. Technical Writing Two: Organizing large documents](https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/two/large-docs)).

### gtw_navigation — Add navigation

> Providing navigation and signposting for your readers ensures they can find what they are looking for and the information they need to get unstuck.

Source: [Google for Developers. Technical Writing Two: Organizing large documents](https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/two/large-docs), Technical Writing Two > Organizing large documents > Add navigation (https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/two/large-docs).

### gtw_task_headings — Prefer task-based headings

> Choose a heading that describes the task your reader is working on. Avoid headings that rely on unfamiliar terminology or tools.

Source: [Google for Developers. Technical Writing Two: Organizing large documents](https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/two/large-docs), Technical Writing Two > Organizing large documents > Add navigation > Prefer task-based headings (https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/two/large-docs).

Applies: Unless your readers are already very experienced with the terminology and concepts for this topic, a more familiar heading might be preferable

### gtw_text_under_heading — Provide text under each heading

> Most readers appreciate at least a brief introduction under each heading to provide some context.

Source: [Google for Developers. Technical Writing Two: Organizing large documents](https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/two/large-docs), Technical Writing Two > Organizing large documents > Add navigation > Provide text under each heading (https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/two/large-docs).

Examples: `google-tw2-4` ([Google for Developers. Technical Writing Two: Organizing large documents](https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/two/large-docs)).

### gtw_progressive — Disclose information progressively

> Readers are more likely to be receptive to longer documents that progressively disclose new information to them when they need it.

Source: [Google for Developers. Technical Writing Two: Organizing large documents](https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/two/large-docs), Technical Writing Two > Organizing large documents > Disclose information progressively (https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/two/large-docs).

### govuk_user_needs — Write content to meet user needs

> Publish only what’s needed to meet that user need and nothing more.

Source: [GOV.UK content and publishing guidance: Writing guidelines (Writing to GOV.UK standards)](https://guidance.publishing.service.gov.uk/writing-to-gov-uk-standards/writing-guidelines/), GOV.UK content and publishing guidance, Writing guidelines, https://guidance.publishing.service.gov.uk/writing-to-gov-uk-standards/writing-guidelines/meet-user-needs/ (page).

### govuk_frontload — Frontload your content

> Put the most important information first. The quicker you get to the point, the greater the chance your users will see the information you want them to.

Source: [GOV.UK content and publishing guidance: Writing guidelines (Writing to GOV.UK standards)](https://guidance.publishing.service.gov.uk/writing-to-gov-uk-standards/writing-guidelines/), GOV.UK content and publishing guidance, Writing guidelines, https://guidance.publishing.service.gov.uk/writing-to-gov-uk-standards/writing-guidelines/clear-structure/ > Frontload your content.

### govuk_headings — Break up the text with headings

> Write headings that are: descriptive – avoid generic headings like ‘Introduction’ frontloaded – put the most important information first [...] removable – the content should still make sense with the headings removed

Source: [GOV.UK content and publishing guidance: Writing guidelines (Writing to GOV.UK standards)](https://guidance.publishing.service.gov.uk/writing-to-gov-uk-standards/writing-guidelines/), GOV.UK content and publishing guidance, Writing guidelines, https://guidance.publishing.service.gov.uk/writing-to-gov-uk-standards/writing-guidelines/clear-structure/ > Break up the text with headings.

### govuk_lists_steps — Use bullet point lists and steps

> Bullet point lists and steps can: reduce duplication make lists easier to read

Source: [GOV.UK content and publishing guidance: Writing guidelines (Writing to GOV.UK standards)](https://guidance.publishing.service.gov.uk/writing-to-gov-uk-standards/writing-guidelines/), GOV.UK content and publishing guidance, Writing guidelines, https://guidance.publishing.service.gov.uk/writing-to-gov-uk-standards/writing-guidelines/clear-structure/ > Use bullet point lists and steps.

### govuk_specialists_too — Write clearly for specialists too

> Even when writing for a specialist audience, you still need to make sure everyone can understand what the content is about.

Source: [GOV.UK content and publishing guidance: Writing guidelines (Writing to GOV.UK standards)](https://guidance.publishing.service.gov.uk/writing-to-gov-uk-standards/writing-guidelines/), GOV.UK content and publishing guidance, Writing guidelines, https://guidance.publishing.service.gov.uk/writing-to-gov-uk-standards/writing-guidelines/clear-language/ > Write clearly for specialists too.

### govuk_words_to_avoid — Know about words to avoid

> Do not use formal or long words when easy or short ones will do. [...] Avoid government ‘buzzwords’ and jargon. Often, these words are too vague and can lead to misinterpretation or empty, meaningless text.

Source: [GOV.UK content and publishing guidance: Writing guidelines (Writing to GOV.UK standards)](https://guidance.publishing.service.gov.uk/writing-to-gov-uk-standards/writing-guidelines/), GOV.UK content and publishing guidance, Writing guidelines, https://guidance.publishing.service.gov.uk/writing-to-gov-uk-standards/writing-guidelines/clear-language/ > Know about words to avoid.

### govuk_specialist_language — Use specialist language if needed

> They’re not always jargon. You just need to explain what they mean the first time you use them.

Source: [GOV.UK content and publishing guidance: Writing guidelines (Writing to GOV.UK standards)](https://guidance.publishing.service.gov.uk/writing-to-gov-uk-standards/writing-guidelines/), GOV.UK content and publishing guidance, Writing guidelines, https://guidance.publishing.service.gov.uk/writing-to-gov-uk-standards/writing-guidelines/clear-language/ > Use specialist language if needed.

Applies: Where you need to use specialist terms, you can.

### govuk_active_voice — Use the active voice

> Use the active voice rather than the passive voice.

Source: [GOV.UK content and publishing guidance: Writing guidelines (Writing to GOV.UK standards)](https://guidance.publishing.service.gov.uk/writing-to-gov-uk-standards/writing-guidelines/), GOV.UK content and publishing guidance, Writing guidelines, https://guidance.publishing.service.gov.uk/writing-to-gov-uk-standards/writing-guidelines/clear-language/ > Use the active voice.

Applies: when the outcome is more important than the agent of the action

### govuk_short_chunks — Keep paragraphs and sentences short

> Keep chunks of text short. Paragraphs should have no more than 5 sentences each. Try to split up sentences that are over 25 words long.

Source: [GOV.UK content and publishing guidance: Writing guidelines (Writing to GOV.UK standards)](https://guidance.publishing.service.gov.uk/writing-to-gov-uk-standards/writing-guidelines/), GOV.UK content and publishing guidance, Writing guidelines, https://guidance.publishing.service.gov.uk/writing-to-gov-uk-standards/writing-guidelines/clear-language/ > Keep paragraphs and sentences short.

Applies: There’s no maximum or minimum overall page length.

### govuk_tone — Use the right tone

> The ‘tone of voice’ for GOV.UK content is: specific informative clear and concise brisk, but not terse incisive (friendliness can lead to a lack of precision and unnecessary words) – but remain human (not a faceless machine) serious but not pompous emotionless – adjectives can be subjective and make the text sound more emotive and like spin

Source: [GOV.UK content and publishing guidance: Writing guidelines (Writing to GOV.UK standards)](https://guidance.publishing.service.gov.uk/writing-to-gov-uk-standards/writing-guidelines/), GOV.UK content and publishing guidance, Writing guidelines, https://guidance.publishing.service.gov.uk/writing-to-gov-uk-standards/writing-guidelines/right-tone/ (page opening).

### ms_bigger_ideas — Use bigger ideas, fewer words

> Our modern design hinges on crisp minimalism. Shorter is always better.

Source: [Microsoft Writing Style Guide](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/style-guide/welcome/), Microsoft Writing Style Guide, Top 10 tips for Microsoft style and voice > Use bigger ideas, fewer words (https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/style-guide/top-10-tips-style-voice).

### ms_write_like_speak — Write like you speak

> Write like you speak. Read your text aloud. Avoid jargon and overly complex or technical language. It should sound like a friendly conversation.

Source: [Microsoft Writing Style Guide](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/style-guide/welcome/), Microsoft Writing Style Guide, Top 10 tips > Write like you speak.

### ms_point_fast — Get to the point fast

> Lead with what's most important. Front-load keywords for scanning. Make customer choices and next steps obvious.

Source: [Microsoft Writing Style Guide](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/style-guide/welcome/), Microsoft Writing Style Guide, Top 10 tips > Get to the point fast.

### ms_be_brief — Be brief

> Give customers just enough information to make decisions confidently. Prune every excess word.

Source: [Microsoft Writing Style Guide](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/style-guide/welcome/), Microsoft Writing Style Guide, Top 10 tips > Be brief.

### ms_crisp_clear — Crisp and clear

> We’re to the point. We write for scanning first, reading second. We make it simple above all.

Source: [Microsoft Writing Style Guide](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/style-guide/welcome/), Microsoft Writing Style Guide, Microsoft's brand voice: Above all, simple and human > Crisp and clear (https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/style-guide/brand-voice-above-all-simple-human).

### ms_lend_a_hand — Ready to lend a hand

> We show customers we’re on their side. We anticipate their real needs and offer great information at just the right time.

Source: [Microsoft Writing Style Guide](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/style-guide/welcome/), Microsoft Writing Style Guide, Microsoft's brand voice > Ready to lend a hand.

### wtd_arid — ARID

> Accept (some) Repetition In Documentation. [...] It means: try to keep things as DRY as possible but also recognize that you’ll inevitably need some amount of “moisture” to produce documentation.

Source: [Write the Docs: Documentation principles](https://www.writethedocs.org/guide/writing/docs-principles/), Write the Docs, Documentation principles, Principles for great content > ARID (https://www.writethedocs.org/guide/writing/docs-principles/).

### wtd_skimmable — Skimmable

> Structure content to help readers identify and skip over concepts which they already understand or are not relevant to their immediate questions.

Source: [Write the Docs: Documentation principles](https://www.writethedocs.org/guide/writing/docs-principles/), Write the Docs, Documentation principles, Principles for great content > Skimmable (https://www.writethedocs.org/guide/writing/docs-principles/).

### wtd_exemplary — Exemplary

> Include (some) examples and tutorials in content. [...] Try to write examples for the most common use cases, but not for everything.

Source: [Write the Docs: Documentation principles](https://www.writethedocs.org/guide/writing/docs-principles/), Write the Docs, Documentation principles, Principles for great content > Exemplary (https://www.writethedocs.org/guide/writing/docs-principles/).

### wtd_consistent — Consistent

> Use consistent language and formatting in content.

Source: [Write the Docs: Documentation principles](https://www.writethedocs.org/guide/writing/docs-principles/), Write the Docs, Documentation principles, Principles for great content > Consistent (https://www.writethedocs.org/guide/writing/docs-principles/).

### wtd_current — Current

> Consider incorrect documentation to be worse than missing documentation.

Source: [Write the Docs: Documentation principles](https://www.writethedocs.org/guide/writing/docs-principles/), Write the Docs, Documentation principles, Principles for great content > Current (https://www.writethedocs.org/guide/writing/docs-principles/).

### wtd_cumulative — Cumulative

> Content should be ordered to cover prerequisite concepts first.

Source: [Write the Docs: Documentation principles](https://www.writethedocs.org/guide/writing/docs-principles/), Write the Docs, Documentation principles, Principles for publications > Cumulative (https://www.writethedocs.org/guide/writing/docs-principles/).

### wtd_complete — Complete

> Within each publication, cover concepts in full, or not at all.

Source: [Write the Docs: Documentation principles](https://www.writethedocs.org/guide/writing/docs-principles/), Write the Docs, Documentation principles, Principles for publications > Complete (https://www.writethedocs.org/guide/writing/docs-principles/).

Applies: Publishing partially completed documentation must be done cautiously. To avoid misleading readers, make every effort to clearly state, up front, that a particular concept is only covered partially.

### wtd_beautiful — Beautiful

> Visual style should be intentional and aesthetically pleasing.

Source: [Write the Docs: Documentation principles](https://www.writethedocs.org/guide/writing/docs-principles/), Write the Docs, Documentation principles, Principles for publications > Beautiful (https://www.writethedocs.org/guide/writing/docs-principles/).

### diataxis_functional_quality — Functional quality

> We need documentation to meet standards of accuracy, completeness, consistency, usefulness, precision and so on. We can call these aspects of its functional quality. Documentation that fails to meet any one of them is failing to perform one of its key functions.

Source: [Diátaxis: a systematic approach to technical documentation authoring (D. Procida)](https://diataxis.fr/), Diátaxis, Quality > Functional quality (https://diataxis.fr/quality/).

### diataxis_deep_quality — Deep quality

> Think of characteristics such as: feeling good to use having flow fitting to human needs being beautiful anticipating the user Unlike the characteristics of functional quality, they cannot be checked or measured, but they can still be clearly identified.

Source: [Diátaxis: a systematic approach to technical documentation authoring (D. Procida)](https://diataxis.fr/), Diátaxis, Quality > Deep quality (https://diataxis.fr/quality/).

### diataxis_expectations_guidance — Expectations and guidance

> Each piece of content is of a kind that not only has one particular job to do, that job is also clearly distinguished from and contrasted with the other functions of documentation.

Source: [Diátaxis: a systematic approach to technical documentation authoring (D. Procida)](https://diataxis.fr/), Diátaxis, The map > Expectations and guidance (https://diataxis.fr/map/).

Applies: The user whose needs Diátaxis serves is the practitioner in a domain of skill

### diataxis_blur — Blur

> When these distinctions are allowed to blur, the different kinds of documentation bleed into each other. Writing style and content make their way into inappropriate places.

Source: [Diátaxis: a systematic approach to technical documentation authoring (D. Procida)](https://diataxis.fr/), Diátaxis, The map > Blur (https://diataxis.fr/map/).

Applies: The user whose needs Diátaxis serves is the practitioner in a domain of skill

Contributes rather than criteria:

- [Google for Developers. Technical Writing Two: Organizing large documents](https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/two/large-docs) (methods): Technical Writing Two "Self-editing" (adopt a style guide, think like your audience, read it out loud, come back to it later, change the context, find a peer editor) gives revision practices, not properties of the finished document.
- [Federal Plain Language Guidelines (PLAIN, plainlanguage.gov)](https://www.plainlanguage.gov/guidelines/) (methods): FPLG section V "Test" (paraphrase testing, usability testing, controlled comparative studies) describes how to test a document with readers; it is the evaluation method behind ISO principle 4 (usable) rather than a criterion a reader can score from the text.
- [Federal Plain Language Guidelines (PLAIN, plainlanguage.gov)](https://www.plainlanguage.gov/guidelines/) (examples): The plainlanguage.gov before/after pages (examples a3, a10, a33) are separate example pages, not printed under an FPLG guideline heading, so they are not attached to FPLG criteria.
- [Google developer documentation style guide](https://developers.google.com/style) (methods): The Google developer documentation style guide "Highlights" page lists sentence- and markup-level rules (second person, active voice, sentence case, code font, date formats, link text); it states no document-level quality criteria, so none were taken from it.

Gaps: ISO 24495-1: the official text was not read. The ISO OBP preview requires accepting ISO's terms of use (not done without the user's consent) and iso.org returns 403 to scripts; the principle wording is quoted from narratology.co.uk, a consultancy page, and matches the paraphrase on sitecockpit.com (iso24495_sitecockpit.txt). Treat these four quotes as secondary. Selection within sources: only items a reader could score on a whole document were kept. Left out as micro-rules: FPLG "Use the simplest form of a verb", "Avoid hidden verbs", "Use must to indicate requirements", "Use contractions when appropriate", "Don't turn verbs into nouns", "Use pronouns to speak directly to readers", "Minimize abbreviations", "Don't use slashes", "Keep subject, verb, and object close together", "Avoid double negatives and exceptions to exceptions", "Place words carefully", all of IV "Write for the web"; Google TW "Use acronyms properly", "Reduce there is / there are", "Reduce subordinate clauses (optional)", "Distinguish that from which", "Start numbered list items with imperative verbs", "Punctuate items appropriately", the optional Punctuation and Markdown units; GOV.UK "Avoid footnotes", "Do not repeat yourself" (about page summaries), "Be careful with contractions", "Make requirements clear", "Think about your users' choice of words", the "Write clear summaries/titles/links/change notes" pages; Microsoft "Project friendliness", capitalization, punctuation, serial comma and spacing tips, "Revise weak writing", brand-voice "Warm and relaxed"; Write the Docs "Precursory", "Participatory", "Nearby", "Unique", "Discoverable", "Addressable", "Comprehensive" (process, source-storage, publication-system or whole-body properties). SEC: all eleven chapter 6 principle headings kept (the user asked for them) even where sentence-level; from chapter 7 only Hierarchy and the text-applicable subheadings Emphasizing text, Keep paragraph length relatively short and Use tables to increase clarity were kept; typeface, type size, justification, line spacing, line length, graphics and color items were left out as print design. Google TW criteria are taken at h2 level, except in "Organizing large documents" where the h2 "Organize a document" is only an umbrella and its h3s (Outline, Introduce) carry the rules. Example a29 sits in the "Compare and contrast" subsection of "Summarize key points at the start" and its answer is about what belongs in the opening paragraph, so it is attached to that h2; a15 is the "Outline exercise" that follows "Outline a document"; a36 is the "Curse of knowledge" exercise inside "Fit documentation to your audience". Example a8's locator says "III. Write clearly" but the FPLG heading is "III. Write your document"; the subheading "i. Use short, simple words" matches. ISO, GOV.UK, Microsoft, Write the Docs and Diátaxis publish no levels; FPLG, SEC and Google TW publish none either. Diátaxis "deep quality" is explicitly not measurable ("cannot be checked or measured"), which the quote keeps. Some sources give numeric thresholds (GOV.UK: no more than 5 sentences per paragraph, split sentences over 25 words; Google TW: 3–5 sentences per paragraph) which are kept verbatim in the quotes, not turned into levels.

## Generated-text evaluation

Sources: [Graesser, A. C., McNamara, D. S., Louwerse, M. M. and Cai, Z. Coh-Metrix: Analysis of text on cohesion and language. Behavior Research Methods 36:193–202](https://doi.org/10.3758/BF03195564); [Kryściński, W. et al. Evaluating the Factual Consistency of Abstractive Text Summarization. EMNLP](https://arxiv.org/abs/1910.12840); [Min, S. et al. FActScore: Fine-grained Atomic Evaluation of Factual Precision in Long Form Text Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.14251); [Liu, Y. et al. G-Eval: NLG Evaluation using GPT-4 with Better Human Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2303.16634); [Kim, S. et al. Prometheus: Inducing Fine-grained Evaluation Capability in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.08491); [Wang, A., Cho, K. and Lewis, M. Asking and Answering Questions to Evaluate the Factual Consistency of Summaries. ACL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2004.04228); [Laban, P. et al. SummaC: Re-Visiting NLI-based Models for Inconsistency Detection in Summarization. TACL 10](https://arxiv.org/abs/2111.09525); [Fabbri, A. R. et al. SummEval: Re-evaluating Summarization Evaluation. TACL 9](https://arxiv.org/abs/2007.12626); [Maynez, J., Narayan, S., Bohnet, B. and McDonald, R. On Faithfulness and Factuality in Abstractive Summarization (XSum hallucination annotations)](https://github.com/google-research-datasets/xsum_hallucination_annotations).

Rubric id `generated_text_evaluation`: SummEval human-evaluation dimensions (coherence, consistency, fluency, relevance; 1–5).

### summeval_coherence — Coherence

> Coherence - the collective quality of all sentences. We align this dimension with the DUC quality question (Dang, 2005) of structure and coherence whereby "the summary should be well-structured and well-organized. The summary should not just be a heap of related information, but should build from sentence to sentence to a coherent body of information about a topic."

Source: [Fabbri, A. R. et al. SummEval: Re-evaluating Summarization Evaluation. TACL 9](https://arxiv.org/abs/2007.12626), Fabbri et al. 2021 (arXiv 2007.12626v5), Section 4.3 Human Annotations; annotator-facing definition from Appendix Figure 3 (data collection interface, image; transcribed by OCR and checked against the image).

Published levels (1 to 5): 1: worst; 5: best

### summeval_consistency — Consistency

> Consistency - the factual alignment between the summary and the summarized source. A factually consistent summary contains only statements that are entailed by the source document. Annotators were also asked to penalize summaries that contained hallucinated facts.

Source: [Fabbri, A. R. et al. SummEval: Re-evaluating Summarization Evaluation. TACL 9](https://arxiv.org/abs/2007.12626), Fabbri et al. 2021 (arXiv 2007.12626v5), Section 4.3 Human Annotations; annotator-facing definition from Appendix Figure 3 (data collection interface, image; transcribed by OCR and checked against the image).

Published levels (1 to 5): 1: worst; 5: best

### summeval_fluency — Fluency

> Fluency - the quality of individual sentences. Drawing again from the DUC quality guidelines, sentences in the summary "should have no formatting problems, capitalization errors or obviously ungrammatical sentences (e.g., fragments, missing components) that make the text difficult to read."

Source: [Fabbri, A. R. et al. SummEval: Re-evaluating Summarization Evaluation. TACL 9](https://arxiv.org/abs/2007.12626), Fabbri et al. 2021 (arXiv 2007.12626v5), Section 4.3 Human Annotations; annotator-facing definition from Appendix Figure 3 (data collection interface, image; transcribed by OCR and checked against the image).

Published levels (1 to 5): 1: worst; 5: best

### summeval_relevance — Relevance

> Relevance - selection of important content from the source. The summary should include only important information from the source document. Annotators were instructed to penalize summaries which contained redundancies and excess information.

Source: [Fabbri, A. R. et al. SummEval: Re-evaluating Summarization Evaluation. TACL 9](https://arxiv.org/abs/2007.12626), Fabbri et al. 2021 (arXiv 2007.12626v5), Section 4.3 Human Annotations; annotator-facing definition from Appendix Figure 3 (data collection interface, image; transcribed by OCR and checked against the image).

Published levels (1 to 5): 1: worst; 5: best

Contributes rather than criteria:

- [Liu, Y. et al. G-Eval: NLG Evaluation using GPT-4 with Better Human Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2303.16634) (methods): G-Eval prompts an LLM with a task description and one criterion, has it generate chain-of-thought evaluation steps, and weights the 1–5 score by token probabilities; on summarisation it scores the SummEval dimensions rather than defining its own.
- [Kim, S. et al. Prometheus: Inducing Fine-grained Evaluation Capability in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.08491) (methods): Prometheus is an open evaluator model trained to grade a response against a user-supplied score rubric (criterion plus five level descriptions) and reference answer, writing feedback before the score; the criteria come from the user.
- [Min, S. et al. FActScore: Fine-grained Atomic Evaluation of Factual Precision in Long Form Text Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.14251) (methods): FActScore splits long-form generations into atomic facts and reports the fraction supported by a knowledge source.
- [Laban, P. et al. SummaC: Re-Visiting NLI-based Models for Inconsistency Detection in Summarization. TACL 10](https://arxiv.org/abs/2111.09525) (methods): SummaC runs an NLI model on sentence pairs between source and summary and aggregates the entailment matrix to detect inconsistency.
- [Kryściński, W. et al. Evaluating the Factual Consistency of Abstractive Text Summarization. EMNLP](https://arxiv.org/abs/1910.12840) (methods): FactCC is a weakly supervised classifier trained on rule-transformed summary sentences that labels a claim consistent or inconsistent with its source document.
- [Wang, A., Cho, K. and Lewis, M. Asking and Answering Questions to Evaluate the Factual Consistency of Summaries. ACL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2004.04228) (methods): QAGS generates questions from a summary, answers them against both the summary and the source, and scores consistency by answer agreement.
- [Graesser, A. C., McNamara, D. S., Louwerse, M. M. and Cai, Z. Coh-Metrix: Analysis of text on cohesion and language. Behavior Research Methods 36:193–202](https://doi.org/10.3758/BF03195564) (methods): Coh-Metrix computes automatic text indices of cohesion, language and readability (for example referential overlap, connectives, LSA similarity).
- [Maynez, J., Narayan, S., Bohnet, B. and McDonald, R. On Faithfulness and Factuality in Abstractive Summarization (XSum hallucination annotations)](https://github.com/google-research-datasets/xsum_hallucination_annotations) (examples): Examples b12 and b13 come from the XSum hallucination annotations (Maynez et al.) and FRANK factuality labels, not from SummEval (which annotated CNN/DailyMail summaries); SummEval does not place them under its consistency dimension, so they are not attached.

Gaps: SummEval publishes only the scale end points (1 = worst, 5 = best); there are no per-level descriptors, so levels holds the two anchors. The paper says summaries were shown "in sets of 5", while the Figure 3 interface says "A-F (6 in total)"; both are quoted as found. The annotator-facing definitions exist only inside the Figure 3 image; they were OCR-transcribed (summeval_fig3_ocr.txt, raw/summeval_fig3-000.png) and checked by eye against the image, including the source's own wording "to the fit together and sound naturally". The not_criteria notes are one-sentence summaries written from the cited papers' known content; they are not quotes and were not re-verified against the papers in this pass. No examples attached: no verified example comes from SummEval.

## Text organization research

Sources: [Barzilay, R. and Lapata, M. Modeling Local Coherence: An Entity-Based Approach. Computational Linguistics 34(1):1–34](https://aclanthology.org/J08-1001/); [Garnock-Jones, T. Conversational Concurrency. PhD thesis, Northeastern University (arXiv 2409.04055), Figure 1 'Grice's Conversational Maxims (Grice 1975)'](https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.04055); [Gopen, G. D. and Swan, J. A. The Science of Scientific Writing. American Scientist 78(6):550–558](https://www.americanscientist.org/blog/the-long-view/the-science-of-scientific-writing); [Grice, H. P. Logic and Conversation. In Cole and Morgan (eds.), Syntax and Semantics 3: Speech Acts, 41–58](https://doi.org/10.1163/9789004368811_003); [Halliday, M. A. K. and Hasan, R. Cohesion in English. Longman](https://doi.org/10.4324/9781315836010); [Minto, B. The Pyramid Principle: Logic in Writing and Thinking (3rd ed.). Pearson](https://www.barbaraminto.com/); [Manager 138. Pyramid Principle: Why a Pyramid Structure (reading notes quoting Minto [1996] 2018 with page numbers)](https://www.manager138.com/resources/minto_pyramid_principle/ch1_why_pyramid.html); [Millerd, P. The Pyramid Principle: Book Summary & Review (Part 1: Logic In Writing). StrategyU](https://strategyu.co/pyramid-principle-partone/); [MIT OpenCourseWare 24.903 Language and its Structure III (Spring 2005), reading 'Grice's System' (grice_maxims.pdf)](https://web.archive.org/web/2010id_/http://ocw.mit.edu/courses/linguistics-and-philosophy/24-903-language-and-its-structure-iii-semantics-and-pragmatics-spring-2005/readings/grice_maxims.pdf); [Grandy, R. E. and Warner, R. Paul Grice. Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (substantive revision 19 June 2026)](https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/grice/); [Tanswell, F. H.P. Grice and the Great Pragmatics Predicament. Aporia (University of St Andrews)](https://ojs.st-andrews.ac.uk/index.php/aporia/article/download/2049/1515/7210).

Rubric id `text_organization`: Text organization: reader expectations, conversational maxims and the pyramid rules.

### gs_1 — Follow a grammatical subject as soon as possible with its verb.

> 1. Follow a grammatical subject as soon as possible with its verb.

Source: [Gopen, G. D. and Swan, J. A. The Science of Scientific Writing. American Scientist 78(6):550–558](https://www.americanscientist.org/blog/the-long-view/the-science-of-scientific-writing), American Scientist 78(6), 1990, final section 'Writing and the Scientific Process', numbered list of structural principles, item 1 (Wayback 2021-01-15 copy of the americanscientist.org page).

Applies: None of these reader-expectation principles should be considered "rules." [...] any reader expectation can be violated to good effect.

Examples: `gopen-swan-1` ([Gopen, G. D. and Swan, J. A. The Science of Scientific Writing. American Scientist 78(6):550–558](https://www.americanscientist.org/blog/the-long-view/the-science-of-scientific-writing)).

### gs_2 — Place in the stress position the "new information" you want the reader to emphasize.

> 2. Place in the stress position the "new information" you want the reader to emphasize.

Source: [Gopen, G. D. and Swan, J. A. The Science of Scientific Writing. American Scientist 78(6):550–558](https://www.americanscientist.org/blog/the-long-view/the-science-of-scientific-writing), American Scientist 78(6), 1990, final section 'Writing and the Scientific Process', numbered list of structural principles, item 2 (Wayback 2021-01-15 copy of the americanscientist.org page).

Applies: None of these reader-expectation principles should be considered "rules." [...] any reader expectation can be violated to good effect.

Examples: `gopen-swan-2` ([Gopen, G. D. and Swan, J. A. The Science of Scientific Writing. American Scientist 78(6):550–558](https://www.americanscientist.org/blog/the-long-view/the-science-of-scientific-writing)), `gopen-swan-1` ([Gopen, G. D. and Swan, J. A. The Science of Scientific Writing. American Scientist 78(6):550–558](https://www.americanscientist.org/blog/the-long-view/the-science-of-scientific-writing)).

### gs_3 — Place the person or thing whose "story" a sentence is telling at the beginning of the sentence, in the topic position.

> 3. Place the person or thing whose "story" a sentence is telling at the beginning of the sentence, in the topic position.

Source: [Gopen, G. D. and Swan, J. A. The Science of Scientific Writing. American Scientist 78(6):550–558](https://www.americanscientist.org/blog/the-long-view/the-science-of-scientific-writing), American Scientist 78(6), 1990, final section 'Writing and the Scientific Process', numbered list of structural principles, item 3 (Wayback 2021-01-15 copy of the americanscientist.org page).

Applies: None of these reader-expectation principles should be considered "rules." [...] any reader expectation can be violated to good effect.

Examples: `gopen-swan-2` ([Gopen, G. D. and Swan, J. A. The Science of Scientific Writing. American Scientist 78(6):550–558](https://www.americanscientist.org/blog/the-long-view/the-science-of-scientific-writing)), `gopen-swan-1` ([Gopen, G. D. and Swan, J. A. The Science of Scientific Writing. American Scientist 78(6):550–558](https://www.americanscientist.org/blog/the-long-view/the-science-of-scientific-writing)).

### gs_4 — Place appropriate "old information" (material already stated in the discourse) in the topic position for linkage backward and contextualization forward.

> 4. Place appropriate "old information" (material already stated in the discourse) in the topic position for linkage backward and contextualization forward.

Source: [Gopen, G. D. and Swan, J. A. The Science of Scientific Writing. American Scientist 78(6):550–558](https://www.americanscientist.org/blog/the-long-view/the-science-of-scientific-writing), American Scientist 78(6), 1990, final section 'Writing and the Scientific Process', numbered list of structural principles, item 4 (Wayback 2021-01-15 copy of the americanscientist.org page).

Applies: None of these reader-expectation principles should be considered "rules." [...] any reader expectation can be violated to good effect.

Examples: `gopen-swan-2` ([Gopen, G. D. and Swan, J. A. The Science of Scientific Writing. American Scientist 78(6):550–558](https://www.americanscientist.org/blog/the-long-view/the-science-of-scientific-writing)), `gopen-swan-1` ([Gopen, G. D. and Swan, J. A. The Science of Scientific Writing. American Scientist 78(6):550–558](https://www.americanscientist.org/blog/the-long-view/the-science-of-scientific-writing)).

### gs_5 — Articulate the action of every clause or sentence in its verb.

> 5. Articulate the action of every clause or sentence in its verb.

Source: [Gopen, G. D. and Swan, J. A. The Science of Scientific Writing. American Scientist 78(6):550–558](https://www.americanscientist.org/blog/the-long-view/the-science-of-scientific-writing), American Scientist 78(6), 1990, final section 'Writing and the Scientific Process', numbered list of structural principles, item 5 (Wayback 2021-01-15 copy of the americanscientist.org page).

Applies: None of these reader-expectation principles should be considered "rules." [...] any reader expectation can be violated to good effect.

### gs_6 — In general, provide context for your reader before asking that reader to consider anything new.

> 6. In general, provide context for your reader before asking that reader to consider anything new.

Source: [Gopen, G. D. and Swan, J. A. The Science of Scientific Writing. American Scientist 78(6):550–558](https://www.americanscientist.org/blog/the-long-view/the-science-of-scientific-writing), American Scientist 78(6), 1990, final section 'Writing and the Scientific Process', numbered list of structural principles, item 6 (Wayback 2021-01-15 copy of the americanscientist.org page).

Applies: None of these reader-expectation principles should be considered "rules." [...] any reader expectation can be violated to good effect.

### gs_7 — In general, try to ensure that the relative emphases of the substance coincide with the relative expectations for emphasis raised by the structure.

> 7. In general, try to ensure that the relative emphases of the substance coincide with the relative expectations for emphasis raised by the structure.

Source: [Gopen, G. D. and Swan, J. A. The Science of Scientific Writing. American Scientist 78(6):550–558](https://www.americanscientist.org/blog/the-long-view/the-science-of-scientific-writing), American Scientist 78(6), 1990, final section 'Writing and the Scientific Process', numbered list of structural principles, item 7 (Wayback 2021-01-15 copy of the americanscientist.org page).

Applies: None of these reader-expectation principles should be considered "rules." [...] any reader expectation can be violated to good effect.

### gs_8 — every unit of discourse, no matter the size, should serve a single function or make a single point

> We now have three rhetorical principles based on reader expectations: [...] second, every unit of discourse, no matter the size, should serve a single function or make a single point;

Source: [Gopen, G. D. and Swan, J. A. The Science of Scientific Writing. American Scientist 78(6):550–558](https://www.americanscientist.org/blog/the-long-view/the-science-of-scientific-writing), Section 'The Stress Position', summary of the three rhetorical principles (the second is not repeated in the final numbered list).

Applies: None of these reader-expectation principles should be considered "rules." [...] any reader expectation can be violated to good effect.

### grice_cp — The Cooperative Principle

> Make your conversational contribution such as is required, at the stage at which it occurs, by the accepted purpose or direction of the talk exchange in which you are engaged.

Source: [Grice, H. P. Logic and Conversation. In Cole and Morgan (eds.), Syntax and Semantics 3: Speech Acts, 41–58](https://doi.org/10.1163/9789004368811_003), Grice 1975 'Logic and Conversation' (Grice 1989, pp. 26-27 as cited by SEP); original not seen. Wording verified against: SEP 'Paul Grice' §3 'Conversational implicature' and the MIT OCW 24.903 handout 'Grice's System'; quoted as checked against [MIT OpenCourseWare 24.903 Language and its Structure III (Spring 2005), reading 'Grice's System' (grice_maxims.pdf)](https://web.archive.org/web/2010id_/http://ocw.mit.edu/courses/linguistics-and-philosophy/24-903-language-and-its-structure-iii-semantics-and-pragmatics-spring-2005/readings/grice_maxims.pdf); [Grandy, R. E. and Warner, R. Paul Grice. Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (substantive revision 19 June 2026)](https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/grice/).

Applies: at the stage at which it occurs, by the accepted purpose or direction of the talk exchange in which you are engaged

### grice_quantity_1 — Quantity 1

> Make your contribution as informative as is required [...] (for the current purposes of the exchange).

Source: [Grice, H. P. Logic and Conversation. In Cole and Morgan (eds.), Syntax and Semantics 3: Speech Acts, 41–58](https://doi.org/10.1163/9789004368811_003), Grice 1975 'Logic and Conversation' (Grice 1989, pp. 26-27 as cited by SEP); original not seen. Wording verified against: MIT OCW handout (maxim text); the parenthetical is absent from that handout and is verified against Tanswell (Aporia, St Andrews) and Garnock-Jones (2017 thesis, arXiv 2409.04055), both quoting Grice 1975; quoted as checked against [Garnock-Jones, T. Conversational Concurrency. PhD thesis, Northeastern University (arXiv 2409.04055), Figure 1 'Grice's Conversational Maxims (Grice 1975)'](https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.04055); [MIT OpenCourseWare 24.903 Language and its Structure III (Spring 2005), reading 'Grice's System' (grice_maxims.pdf)](https://web.archive.org/web/2010id_/http://ocw.mit.edu/courses/linguistics-and-philosophy/24-903-language-and-its-structure-iii-semantics-and-pragmatics-spring-2005/readings/grice_maxims.pdf); [Tanswell, F. H.P. Grice and the Great Pragmatics Predicament. Aporia (University of St Andrews)](https://ojs.st-andrews.ac.uk/index.php/aporia/article/download/2049/1515/7210).

Applies: (for the current purposes of the exchange)

### grice_quantity_2 — Quantity 2

> Do not make your contribution more informative than is required.

Source: [Grice, H. P. Logic and Conversation. In Cole and Morgan (eds.), Syntax and Semantics 3: Speech Acts, 41–58](https://doi.org/10.1163/9789004368811_003), Grice 1975 'Logic and Conversation' (Grice 1989, pp. 26-27 as cited by SEP); original not seen. Wording verified against: MIT OCW handout; quoted as checked against [MIT OpenCourseWare 24.903 Language and its Structure III (Spring 2005), reading 'Grice's System' (grice_maxims.pdf)](https://web.archive.org/web/2010id_/http://ocw.mit.edu/courses/linguistics-and-philosophy/24-903-language-and-its-structure-iii-semantics-and-pragmatics-spring-2005/readings/grice_maxims.pdf).

### grice_quality — Quality (supermaxim)

> Try to make your contribution one that is true

Source: [Grice, H. P. Logic and Conversation. In Cole and Morgan (eds.), Syntax and Semantics 3: Speech Acts, 41–58](https://doi.org/10.1163/9789004368811_003), Grice 1975 'Logic and Conversation' (Grice 1989, pp. 26-27 as cited by SEP); original not seen. Wording verified against: SEP 'Paul Grice' §3; quoted as checked against [Grandy, R. E. and Warner, R. Paul Grice. Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (substantive revision 19 June 2026)](https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/grice/).

### grice_quality_1 — Quality 1

> Do not say what you believe to be false.

Source: [Grice, H. P. Logic and Conversation. In Cole and Morgan (eds.), Syntax and Semantics 3: Speech Acts, 41–58](https://doi.org/10.1163/9789004368811_003), Grice 1975 'Logic and Conversation' (Grice 1989, pp. 26-27 as cited by SEP); original not seen. Wording verified against: MIT OCW handout; quoted as checked against [MIT OpenCourseWare 24.903 Language and its Structure III (Spring 2005), reading 'Grice's System' (grice_maxims.pdf)](https://web.archive.org/web/2010id_/http://ocw.mit.edu/courses/linguistics-and-philosophy/24-903-language-and-its-structure-iii-semantics-and-pragmatics-spring-2005/readings/grice_maxims.pdf).

### grice_quality_2 — Quality 2

> Do not say that for which you lack adequate evidence.

Source: [Grice, H. P. Logic and Conversation. In Cole and Morgan (eds.), Syntax and Semantics 3: Speech Acts, 41–58](https://doi.org/10.1163/9789004368811_003), Grice 1975 'Logic and Conversation' (Grice 1989, pp. 26-27 as cited by SEP); original not seen. Wording verified against: MIT OCW handout; quoted as checked against [MIT OpenCourseWare 24.903 Language and its Structure III (Spring 2005), reading 'Grice's System' (grice_maxims.pdf)](https://web.archive.org/web/2010id_/http://ocw.mit.edu/courses/linguistics-and-philosophy/24-903-language-and-its-structure-iii-semantics-and-pragmatics-spring-2005/readings/grice_maxims.pdf).

### grice_relation — Relation

> Be relevant.

Source: [Grice, H. P. Logic and Conversation. In Cole and Morgan (eds.), Syntax and Semantics 3: Speech Acts, 41–58](https://doi.org/10.1163/9789004368811_003), Grice 1975 'Logic and Conversation' (Grice 1989, pp. 26-27 as cited by SEP); original not seen. Wording verified against: SEP 'Paul Grice' §3 and MIT OCW handout; quoted as checked against [MIT OpenCourseWare 24.903 Language and its Structure III (Spring 2005), reading 'Grice's System' (grice_maxims.pdf)](https://web.archive.org/web/2010id_/http://ocw.mit.edu/courses/linguistics-and-philosophy/24-903-language-and-its-structure-iii-semantics-and-pragmatics-spring-2005/readings/grice_maxims.pdf); [Grandy, R. E. and Warner, R. Paul Grice. Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (substantive revision 19 June 2026)](https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/grice/).

### grice_manner — Manner (supermaxim)

> Be perspicuous

Source: [Grice, H. P. Logic and Conversation. In Cole and Morgan (eds.), Syntax and Semantics 3: Speech Acts, 41–58](https://doi.org/10.1163/9789004368811_003), Grice 1975 'Logic and Conversation' (Grice 1989, pp. 26-27 as cited by SEP); original not seen. Wording verified against: SEP 'Paul Grice' §3; quoted as checked against [Grandy, R. E. and Warner, R. Paul Grice. Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (substantive revision 19 June 2026)](https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/grice/).

### grice_manner_1 — Manner 1

> Avoid obscurity of expression.

Source: [Grice, H. P. Logic and Conversation. In Cole and Morgan (eds.), Syntax and Semantics 3: Speech Acts, 41–58](https://doi.org/10.1163/9789004368811_003), Grice 1975 'Logic and Conversation' (Grice 1989, pp. 26-27 as cited by SEP); original not seen. Wording verified against: MIT OCW handout; quoted as checked against [MIT OpenCourseWare 24.903 Language and its Structure III (Spring 2005), reading 'Grice's System' (grice_maxims.pdf)](https://web.archive.org/web/2010id_/http://ocw.mit.edu/courses/linguistics-and-philosophy/24-903-language-and-its-structure-iii-semantics-and-pragmatics-spring-2005/readings/grice_maxims.pdf).

### grice_manner_2 — Manner 2

> Avoid ambiguity.

Source: [Grice, H. P. Logic and Conversation. In Cole and Morgan (eds.), Syntax and Semantics 3: Speech Acts, 41–58](https://doi.org/10.1163/9789004368811_003), Grice 1975 'Logic and Conversation' (Grice 1989, pp. 26-27 as cited by SEP); original not seen. Wording verified against: MIT OCW handout; quoted as checked against [MIT OpenCourseWare 24.903 Language and its Structure III (Spring 2005), reading 'Grice's System' (grice_maxims.pdf)](https://web.archive.org/web/2010id_/http://ocw.mit.edu/courses/linguistics-and-philosophy/24-903-language-and-its-structure-iii-semantics-and-pragmatics-spring-2005/readings/grice_maxims.pdf).

### grice_manner_3 — Manner 3

> Be brief (avoid unnecessary prolixity).

Source: [Grice, H. P. Logic and Conversation. In Cole and Morgan (eds.), Syntax and Semantics 3: Speech Acts, 41–58](https://doi.org/10.1163/9789004368811_003), Grice 1975 'Logic and Conversation' (Grice 1989, pp. 26-27 as cited by SEP); original not seen. Wording verified against: MIT OCW handout ('Be brief.'); the parenthetical is verified against Tanswell (Aporia) and Garnock-Jones (arXiv 2409.04055); quoted as checked against [Garnock-Jones, T. Conversational Concurrency. PhD thesis, Northeastern University (arXiv 2409.04055), Figure 1 'Grice's Conversational Maxims (Grice 1975)'](https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.04055); [Tanswell, F. H.P. Grice and the Great Pragmatics Predicament. Aporia (University of St Andrews)](https://ojs.st-andrews.ac.uk/index.php/aporia/article/download/2049/1515/7210).

### grice_manner_4 — Manner 4

> Be orderly.

Source: [Grice, H. P. Logic and Conversation. In Cole and Morgan (eds.), Syntax and Semantics 3: Speech Acts, 41–58](https://doi.org/10.1163/9789004368811_003), Grice 1975 'Logic and Conversation' (Grice 1989, pp. 26-27 as cited by SEP); original not seen. Wording verified against: MIT OCW handout; quoted as checked against [MIT OpenCourseWare 24.903 Language and its Structure III (Spring 2005), reading 'Grice's System' (grice_maxims.pdf)](https://web.archive.org/web/2010id_/http://ocw.mit.edu/courses/linguistics-and-philosophy/24-903-language-and-its-structure-iii-semantics-and-pragmatics-spring-2005/readings/grice_maxims.pdf).

### minto_top_down — present their information from the top down

> the clearest written documents will be those that consistently present their information from the top down, in a pyramidal structure, even though the original thinking will have been done from the bottom up.

Source: [Minto, B. The Pyramid Principle: Logic in Writing and Thinking (3rd ed.). Pearson](https://www.barbaraminto.com/), Minto, The Minto Pyramid Principle ([1996] 2018), ch. 1, p. 7, as quoted with page reference by manager138.com notes; book not seen; quoted as checked against [Manager 138. Pyramid Principle: Why a Pyramid Structure (reading notes quoting Minto [1996] 2018 with page numbers)](https://www.manager138.com/resources/minto_pyramid_principle/ch1_why_pyramid.html).

### minto_rule_1 — Ideas at any level in the pyramid must always be summaries of the ideas grouped below them

> Ideas at any level in the pyramid must always be summaries of the ideas grouped below them.

Source: [Minto, B. The Pyramid Principle: Logic in Writing and Thinking (3rd ed.). Pearson](https://www.barbaraminto.com/), Minto, The Minto Pyramid Principle ([1996] 2018), ch. 1 'Pyramid rules', p. 9, as quoted with page reference by manager138.com notes; book not seen; same wording quoted by strategyu.co and ConsultingMethodology.com; quoted as checked against [Manager 138. Pyramid Principle: Why a Pyramid Structure (reading notes quoting Minto [1996] 2018 with page numbers)](https://www.manager138.com/resources/minto_pyramid_principle/ch1_why_pyramid.html); [Millerd, P. The Pyramid Principle: Book Summary & Review (Part 1: Logic In Writing). StrategyU](https://strategyu.co/pyramid-principle-partone/).

### minto_rule_2 — Ideas in each grouping must always be the same kind of idea

> Ideas in each grouping must always be the same kind of idea. [...] Be sure you can label the ideas with a plural noun.

Source: [Minto, B. The Pyramid Principle: Logic in Writing and Thinking (3rd ed.). Pearson](https://www.barbaraminto.com/), Minto, The Minto Pyramid Principle ([1996] 2018), ch. 1 'Pyramid rules', pp. 9-10, as quoted with page reference by manager138.com notes; book not seen; rule wording also quoted by strategyu.co; quoted as checked against [Manager 138. Pyramid Principle: Why a Pyramid Structure (reading notes quoting Minto [1996] 2018 with page numbers)](https://www.manager138.com/resources/minto_pyramid_principle/ch1_why_pyramid.html); [Millerd, P. The Pyramid Principle: Book Summary & Review (Part 1: Logic In Writing). StrategyU](https://strategyu.co/pyramid-principle-partone/).

### minto_rule_3 — Ideas in each grouping must always be logically ordered

> Ideas in each grouping must always be logically ordered. [...] Deductively (major premise, minor premise, conclusion) Chronologically (first, second, third) Structurally (Boston, New York, Washington) Comparatively (first most important, second most important, etc)

Source: [Minto, B. The Pyramid Principle: Logic in Writing and Thinking (3rd ed.). Pearson](https://www.barbaraminto.com/), Minto, The Minto Pyramid Principle ([1996] 2018), ch. 1 'Pyramid rules', pp. 9-11, as quoted with page reference by manager138.com notes; book not seen; rule wording also quoted by strategyu.co and ConsultingMethodology.com; quoted as checked against [Manager 138. Pyramid Principle: Why a Pyramid Structure (reading notes quoting Minto [1996] 2018 with page numbers)](https://www.manager138.com/resources/minto_pyramid_principle/ch1_why_pyramid.html); [Millerd, P. The Pyramid Principle: Book Summary & Review (Part 1: Logic In Writing). StrategyU](https://strategyu.co/pyramid-principle-partone/).

Contributes rather than criteria:

- [Halliday, M. A. K. and Hasan, R. Cohesion in English. Longman](https://doi.org/10.4324/9781315836010) (methods): Cohesion in English describes and classifies cohesive ties (reference, substitution, ellipsis, conjunction, lexical cohesion) as a linguistic analysis; it does not state them as writing criteria. Usable as an annotation method for continuity/cohesion checks.
- [Barzilay, R. and Lapata, M. Modeling Local Coherence: An Entity-Based Approach. Computational Linguistics 34(1):1–34](https://aclanthology.org/J08-1001/) (methods): The entity grid is a computational model of local coherence (entity role transitions between sentences) for ranking or scoring texts; it is a measurement method, not a writing criterion.
- [Grice, H. P. Logic and Conversation. In Cole and Morgan (eds.), Syntax and Semantics 3: Speech Acts, 41–58](https://doi.org/10.1163/9789004368811_003) (methods): Grice's maxims describe presumptions of cooperative conversation used to derive implicatures; Grice notes they can be flouted to communicate. They are listed here as criteria because they are phrased as injunctions, but they were not written as a document-quality rubric.

Gaps: Grice 1975 itself (paywalled) was not seen: sub-maxim wording is checked against a MIT OCW handout and two independent quotations, supermaxims and the Cooperative Principle against the SEP 'Paul Grice' entry; page numbers are SEP's (Grice 1989, 26-27). Sources disagree on 'as informative as is required' (OCW handout, Tanswell, Wikipedia) vs 'as informative as required' (Garnock-Jones); the majority wording is used. Minto's book was not seen and barbaraminto.com does not state the rules; the Minto quotes come from a personal reading-notes site (manager138.com) with page references and are corroborated by strategyu.co and a ConsultingMethodology.com PDF, which is weaker than a publisher source. Minto's SCQ (situation-complication-question) introduction and question/answer vertical relationship were not found quoted verbatim with a locator, so they are not criteria. Gopen and Swan text is the American Scientist web edition via a Wayback copy (2021-01-15), not the 1990 print PDF. No source in this family publishes levels.

## Analytic tradecraft

Sources: [Headquarters, Department of the Army. DA Pamphlet 600-67, Effective Writing for Army Leaders](https://armywriter.com/DAP600-67.pdf); [Intelligence Community Directive 203: Analytic Standards (ODNI)](https://www.dni.gov/files/documents/ICD/ICD-203.pdf); [Mastrandrea, M. D. et al. Guidance Note for Lead Authors of the IPCC Fifth Assessment Report on Consistent Treatment of Uncertainties](https://www.ipcc.ch/site/assets/uploads/2017/08/AR5_Uncertainty_Guidance_Note.pdf); [Kent, S. Words of Estimative Probability. Studies in Intelligence 8(4)](https://www.cia.gov/resources/csi/static/Words-of-Estimative-Probability.pdf); [U.S. Senate Select Committee on Intelligence. Report on the U.S. Intelligence Community's Prewar Intelligence Assessments on Iraq (S. Rept. 108-301), Section X](https://irp.fas.org/congress/2004_rpt/ssci_whitepaper.pdf).

Rubric id `analytic_tradecraft`: Analytic tradecraft: ICD 203 standards, the Army writing standard and calibrated uncertainty language.

### icd203_objective — Objective

> Objective: Analysts must perform their functions with objectivity and with awareness of their own assumptions and reasoning. They must employ reasoning techniques and practical mechanisms that reveal and mitigate bias. Analysts should be alert to influence by existing analytic positions or judgments and must consider alternative perspectives and contrary information. Analysis should not be unduly constrained by previous judgments when new developments indicate a modification is necessary.

Source: [Intelligence Community Directive 203: Analytic Standards (ODNI)](https://www.dni.gov/files/documents/ICD/ICD-203.pdf), ICD 203 Analytic Standards (signed 2 Jan 2015, as amended), Section D.6.a, printed p. 4.

Applies: IC Analytic Standards shall be applied in each analytic product in a manner appropriate to its purpose, the type and scope of its underlying source information, its production timeline, and its customers.

### icd203_independent — Independent of political consideration

> Independent of political consideration: Analytic assessments must not be distorted by, nor shaped for, advocacy of a particular audience, agenda, or policy viewpoint. Analytic judgments must not be influenced by the force of preference for a particular policy.

Source: [Intelligence Community Directive 203: Analytic Standards (ODNI)](https://www.dni.gov/files/documents/ICD/ICD-203.pdf), ICD 203 Analytic Standards (signed 2 Jan 2015, as amended), Section D.6.b, printed p. 4.

Applies: IC Analytic Standards shall be applied in each analytic product in a manner appropriate to its purpose, the type and scope of its underlying source information, its production timeline, and its customers.

### icd203_timely — Timely

> Timely: Analysis must be disseminated in time for it to be actionable by customers.

Source: [Intelligence Community Directive 203: Analytic Standards (ODNI)](https://www.dni.gov/files/documents/ICD/ICD-203.pdf), ICD 203 Analytic Standards (signed 2 Jan 2015, as amended), Section D.6.c, printed p. 4.

Applies: IC Analytic Standards shall be applied in each analytic product in a manner appropriate to its purpose, the type and scope of its underlying source information, its production timeline, and its customers.

### icd203_all_sources — Based on all available sources of intelligence information

> Based on all available sources of intelligence information: Analysis should be informed by all relevant information available. Analytic elements should identify and address critical information gaps and work with collection activities and data providers to develop access and collection strategies.

Source: [Intelligence Community Directive 203: Analytic Standards (ODNI)](https://www.dni.gov/files/documents/ICD/ICD-203.pdf), ICD 203 Analytic Standards (signed 2 Jan 2015, as amended), Section D.6.d, printed p. 4.

Applies: IC Analytic Standards shall be applied in each analytic product in a manner appropriate to its purpose, the type and scope of its underlying source information, its production timeline, and its customers.

### icd203_t1 — Properly describes quality and credibility of underlying sources, data, and methodologies

> Analytic products should identify underlying sources and methodologies upon which judgments are based, and use source descriptors in accordance with ICD 206, Sourcing Requirements for Disseminated Analytic Products, to describe factors affecting source quality and credibility. [...] Source summary statements, described in ICD 206, are strongly encouraged and should be used to provide a holistic assessment of the strengths or weaknesses in the source base and explain which sources are most important to key analytic judgments.

Source: [Intelligence Community Directive 203: Analytic Standards (ODNI)](https://www.dni.gov/files/documents/ICD/ICD-203.pdf), ICD 203 Analytic Standards (signed 2 Jan 2015, as amended), Section D.6.e.(1), printed p. 4-5.

Applies: IC Analytic Standards shall be applied in each analytic product in a manner appropriate to its purpose, the type and scope of its underlying source information, its production timeline, and its customers.

### icd203_t2 — Properly expresses and explains uncertainties associated with major analytic judgments

> Analytic products should indicate and explain the basis for the uncertainties associated with major analytic judgments, specifically the likelihood of occurrence of an event or development, and the analyst's confidence in the basis for this judgment. [...] Analytic products should note causes of uncertainty (e.g., type, currency, and amount of information, knowledge gaps, and the nature of the issue) and explain how uncertainties affect analysis (e.g., to what degree and how a judgment depends on assumptions). [...] Consistency in the terms used and the supporting information and logic advanced is critical to success in expressing uncertainty

Source: [Intelligence Community Directive 203: Analytic Standards (ODNI)](https://www.dni.gov/files/documents/ICD/ICD-203.pdf), ICD 203 Analytic Standards (signed 2 Jan 2015, as amended), Section D.6.e.(2), printed p. 5.

Applies: IC Analytic Standards shall be applied in each analytic product in a manner appropriate to its purpose, the type and scope of its underlying source information, its production timeline, and its customers. [...] As appropriate, products should identify indicators that would alter the levels of uncertainty for major analytic judgments.

### icd203_t2a — (a) For expressions of likelihood or probability

> (a) For expressions of likelihood or probability, an analytic product must use one of the following sets of terms: [...] Analysts are strongly encouraged not to mix terms from different rows. Products that do mix terms must include a disclaimer clearly noting the terms indicate the same assessment of probability.

Source: [Intelligence Community Directive 203: Analytic Standards (ODNI)](https://www.dni.gov/files/documents/ICD/ICD-203.pdf), ICD 203 Analytic Standards (signed 2 Jan 2015, as amended), Section D.6.e.(2)(a), printed p. 5.

Applies: IC Analytic Standards shall be applied in each analytic product in a manner appropriate to its purpose, the type and scope of its underlying source information, its production timeline, and its customers.

### icd203_t2b — (b) confidence level and degree of likelihood not combined

> (b) To avoid confusion, products that express an analyst's confidence in an assessment or judgment using a "confidence level" (e.g., "high confidence") must not combine a confidence level and a degree of likelihood, which refers to an event or development, in the same sentence.

Source: [Intelligence Community Directive 203: Analytic Standards (ODNI)](https://www.dni.gov/files/documents/ICD/ICD-203.pdf), ICD 203 Analytic Standards (signed 2 Jan 2015, as amended), Section D.6.e.(2)(b), printed p. 5.

Applies: IC Analytic Standards shall be applied in each analytic product in a manner appropriate to its purpose, the type and scope of its underlying source information, its production timeline, and its customers.

### icd203_t3 — Properly distinguishes between underlying intelligence information and analysts' assumptions and judgments

> Analytic products should clearly distinguish statements that convey underlying intelligence information used in analysis from statements that convey assumptions or judgments. [...] Products should state assumptions explicitly when they serve as the linchpin of an argument or when they bridge key information gaps. Products should explain the implications for judgments if assumptions prove to be incorrect.

Source: [Intelligence Community Directive 203: Analytic Standards (ODNI)](https://www.dni.gov/files/documents/ICD/ICD-203.pdf), ICD 203 Analytic Standards (signed 2 Jan 2015, as amended), Section D.6.e.(3), printed p. 5.

Applies: IC Analytic Standards shall be applied in each analytic product in a manner appropriate to its purpose, the type and scope of its underlying source information, its production timeline, and its customers. [...] when they serve as the linchpin of an argument or when they bridge key information gaps. [...] Products also should, as appropriate, identify indicators that, if detected, would alter judgments.

### icd203_t4 — Incorporates analysis of alternatives

> Analytic products should identify and assess plausible alternative hypotheses. This is particularly important when major judgments must contend with significant uncertainties, or complexity (e.g., forecasting future trends), or when low probability events could produce high-impact results.
