In discussing alternatives, products should address factors such as associated assumptions, likelihood, or implications related to U.S. interests.

Source: [Intelligence Community Directive 203: Analytic Standards (ODNI)](https://www.dni.gov/files/documents/ICD/ICD-203.pdf), ICD 203 Analytic Standards (signed 2 Jan 2015, as amended), Section D.6.e.(4), printed p. 6.

Applies: IC Analytic Standards shall be applied in each analytic product in a manner appropriate to its purpose, the type and scope of its underlying source information, its production timeline, and its customers. [...] This is particularly important when major judgments must contend with significant uncertainties, or complexity (e.g., forecasting future trends), or when low probability events could produce high-impact results.

### icd203_t5 — Demonstrates customer relevance and addresses implications

> Analytic products should provide information and insight on issues relevant to the customers of U.S. intelligence and address the implications of the information and analysis they provide. Products should add value by addressing prospects, context, threats, or factors affecting opportunities for action.

Source: [Intelligence Community Directive 203: Analytic Standards (ODNI)](https://www.dni.gov/files/documents/ICD/ICD-203.pdf), ICD 203 Analytic Standards (signed 2 Jan 2015, as amended), Section D.6.e.(5), printed p. 6.

Applies: IC Analytic Standards shall be applied in each analytic product in a manner appropriate to its purpose, the type and scope of its underlying source information, its production timeline, and its customers.

### icd203_t6 — Uses clear and logical argumentation

> Analytic products should present a clear main analytic message up front. Products containing multiple judgments should have a main analytic message that is drawn collectively from those judgments. All analytic judgments should be effectively supported by relevant intelligence information and coherent reasoning. Language and syntax should convey meaning unambiguously. Products should be internally consistent and acknowledge significant supporting and contrary information affecting judgments.

Source: [Intelligence Community Directive 203: Analytic Standards (ODNI)](https://www.dni.gov/files/documents/ICD/ICD-203.pdf), ICD 203 Analytic Standards (signed 2 Jan 2015, as amended), Section D.6.e.(6), printed p. 6.

Applies: IC Analytic Standards shall be applied in each analytic product in a manner appropriate to its purpose, the type and scope of its underlying source information, its production timeline, and its customers.

### icd203_t7 — Explains change to or consistency of analytic judgments

> Analytic products should state how their major judgments on a topic are consistent with or represent a change from those in previously published analysis, or represent initial coverage of a topic. Products need not be lengthy or detailed in explaining change or consistency. They should avoid using boilerplate language, however, and should make clear how new information or different reasoning led to the judgments expressed in them.

Source: [Intelligence Community Directive 203: Analytic Standards (ODNI)](https://www.dni.gov/files/documents/ICD/ICD-203.pdf), ICD 203 Analytic Standards (signed 2 Jan 2015, as amended), Section D.6.e.(7), printed p. 6.

Applies: IC Analytic Standards shall be applied in each analytic product in a manner appropriate to its purpose, the type and scope of its underlying source information, its production timeline, and its customers. [...] Recurrent products such as daily crisis reports should note any changes in judgments; absent changes, recurrent products need not confirm consistency with previous editions.

### icd203_t8 — Makes accurate judgments and assessments

> Analytic products should apply expertise and logic to make the most accurate judgments and assessments possible, based on the information available and known information gaps. In doing so, analytic products should present all judgments that would be useful to customers, and should not avoid difficult judgments in order to minimize the risk of being wrong. [...] analytic products should express judgments as clearly and precisely as possible, reducing ambiguity by addressing the likelihood, timing, and nature of the outcome or development.

Source: [Intelligence Community Directive 203: Analytic Standards (ODNI)](https://www.dni.gov/files/documents/ICD/ICD-203.pdf), ICD 203 Analytic Standards (signed 2 Jan 2015, as amended), Section D.6.e.(8), printed p. 6.

Applies: IC Analytic Standards shall be applied in each analytic product in a manner appropriate to its purpose, the type and scope of its underlying source information, its production timeline, and its customers.

### icd203_t9 — Incorporates effective visual information where appropriate

> Analytic products should incorporate visual information to clarify an analytic message and to complement or enhance the presentation of data and analysis. In particular, visual presentations should be used when information or concepts (e.g., spatial or temporal relationships) can be conveyed better in graphic form (e.g., tables, flow charts, images) than in written text. [...] Visual information should always be clear and pertinent to the product's subject.

Source: [Intelligence Community Directive 203: Analytic Standards (ODNI)](https://www.dni.gov/files/documents/ICD/ICD-203.pdf), ICD 203 Analytic Standards (signed 2 Jan 2015, as amended), Section D.6.e.(9), printed p. 6-7.

Applies: IC Analytic Standards shall be applied in each analytic product in a manner appropriate to its purpose, the type and scope of its underlying source information, its production timeline, and its customers. [...] Incorporates effective visual information where appropriate

### dapam_1_4a — the standard for Army writing

> According to AR 600–70, the standard for Army writing is writing you can understand in a single rapid reading, and is generally free of errors in grammar, mechanics, and usage.

Source: [Headquarters, Department of the Army. DA Pamphlet 600-67, Effective Writing for Army Leaders](https://armywriter.com/DAP600-67.pdf), DA PAM 600-67 (2 June 1986), para 1-4a 'Defining the standard', printed p. 1.

### dapam_1_4b — Good Army writing

> Good Army writing is clear, concise, organized, and right to the point.

Source: [Headquarters, Department of the Army. DA Pamphlet 600-67, Effective Writing for Army Leaders](https://armywriter.com/DAP600-67.pdf), DA PAM 600-67 (2 June 1986), para 1-4b 'Defining the standard', printed p. 1.

### dapam_3_1a — bottom line

> Put the recommendation, conclusion, or reason for writing— the “ bottom line ”—in the first or second paragraph, not at the end.

Source: [Headquarters, Department of the Army. DA Pamphlet 600-67, Effective Writing for Army Leaders](https://armywriter.com/DAP600-67.pdf), DA PAM 600-67 (2 June 1986), para 3-1a 'Style Rules', printed p. 1.

Examples: `da-pam-600-67-1` ([Headquarters, Department of the Army. DA Pamphlet 600-67, Effective Writing for Army Leaders](https://armywriter.com/DAP600-67.pdf)).

### dapam_3_1b — Use the active voice

> Use the active voice.

Source: [Headquarters, Department of the Army. DA Pamphlet 600-67, Effective Writing for Army Leaders](https://armywriter.com/DAP600-67.pdf), DA PAM 600-67 (2 June 1986), para 3-1b 'Style Rules', printed p. 1.

Applies: Although sometimes the passive is appropriate, most of the time it is not.

Examples: `da-pam-600-67-2` ([Headquarters, Department of the Army. DA Pamphlet 600-67, Effective Writing for Army Leaders](https://armywriter.com/DAP600-67.pdf)).

### dapam_3_1c — Use short sentences

> Use short sentences (an average of 15 or fewer words).

Source: [Headquarters, Department of the Army. DA Pamphlet 600-67, Effective Writing for Army Leaders](https://armywriter.com/DAP600-67.pdf), DA PAM 600-67 (2 June 1986), para 3-1c 'Style Rules', printed p. 1.

### dapam_3_1d — Use short words

> Use short words (three syllables or fewer). (See the clarity index in paragraph 4–3.)

Source: [Headquarters, Department of the Army. DA Pamphlet 600-67, Effective Writing for Army Leaders](https://armywriter.com/DAP600-67.pdf), DA PAM 600-67 (2 June 1986), para 3-1d 'Style Rules', printed p. 1.

### dapam_3_1e — paragraphs no more than 1 inch deep

> Write paragraphs that, with few exceptions, are no more than 1 inch deep.

Source: [Headquarters, Department of the Army. DA Pamphlet 600-67, Effective Writing for Army Leaders](https://armywriter.com/DAP600-67.pdf), DA PAM 600-67 (2 June 1986), para 3-1e 'Style Rules', printed p. 1.

Applies: with few exceptions

Examples: `da-pam-600-67-1` ([Headquarters, Department of the Army. DA Pamphlet 600-67, Effective Writing for Army Leaders](https://armywriter.com/DAP600-67.pdf)).

### dapam_3_1f — Use correct spelling, grammar, and punctuation

> Use correct spelling, grammar, and punctuation.

Source: [Headquarters, Department of the Army. DA Pamphlet 600-67, Effective Writing for Army Leaders](https://armywriter.com/DAP600-67.pdf), DA PAM 600-67 (2 June 1986), para 3-1f 'Style Rules', printed p. 1.

Examples: `da-pam-600-67-3` ([Headquarters, Department of the Army. DA Pamphlet 600-67, Effective Writing for Army Leaders](https://armywriter.com/DAP600-67.pdf)).

### dapam_3_1g — Use “I,” “you” and “we” as subjects

> Use “I,”“you” and “we” as subjects of sentences instead of “this office,”“this headquarters,”“all individuals,” and so forth, for most kinds of writing.

Source: [Headquarters, Department of the Army. DA Pamphlet 600-67, Effective Writing for Army Leaders](https://armywriter.com/DAP600-67.pdf), DA PAM 600-67 (2 June 1986), para 3-1g 'Style Rules', printed p. 1.

Applies: for most kinds of writing

### dapam_3_2a1 — Structure—main idea first

> Structure—main idea first. (1) Require all staff writing to begin with the main idea. [...] Have subordinates start with the information they would keep if they had to get rid of all the rest.

Source: [Headquarters, Department of the Army. DA Pamphlet 600-67, Effective Writing for Army Leaders](https://armywriter.com/DAP600-67.pdf), DA PAM 600-67 (2 June 1986), para 3-2a(1) 'Two essential changes', printed p. 1.

### dapam_3_2a2 — packaging

> (2) Require specific packaging of all writing. [...] To package– (a) Open with a short, clear purpose sentence. (b) Put the recommendation, conclusion, or most important information (the main point) next. (Some writing combines the purpose and the main point.) (c) Clearly separate each major section. Use paragraphs, headings, or section titles. (d) Use a specific format if one is appropriate.

Source: [Headquarters, Department of the Army. DA Pamphlet 600-67, Effective Writing for Army Leaders](https://armywriter.com/DAP600-67.pdf), DA PAM 600-67 (2 June 1986), para 3-2a(2) 'Two essential changes', printed p. 1.

Applies: (Some writing combines the purpose and the main point.) [...] (d) Use a specific format if one is appropriate.

Examples: `da-pam-600-67-4` ([Headquarters, Department of the Army. DA Pamphlet 600-67, Effective Writing for Army Leaders](https://armywriter.com/DAP600-67.pdf)), `da-pam-600-67-2` ([Headquarters, Department of the Army. DA Pamphlet 600-67, Effective Writing for Army Leaders](https://armywriter.com/DAP600-67.pdf)), `da-pam-600-67-1` ([Headquarters, Department of the Army. DA Pamphlet 600-67, Effective Writing for Army Leaders](https://armywriter.com/DAP600-67.pdf)).

### dapam_3_2b — Style – the active voice

> Style – the active voice. (1) The major style change that makes Army writing clear, direct communication is using the active voice rather than passive voice. [...] (6) When you see writing in the passive voice, have the writers change it to active voice.

Source: [Headquarters, Department of the Army. DA Pamphlet 600-67, Effective Writing for Army Leaders](https://armywriter.com/DAP600-67.pdf), DA PAM 600-67 (2 June 1986), para 3-2b 'Two essential changes', printed p. 1.

Applies: Although sometimes the passive is appropriate, most of the time it is not.

Examples: `da-pam-600-67-2` ([Headquarters, Department of the Army. DA Pamphlet 600-67, Effective Writing for Army Leaders](https://armywriter.com/DAP600-67.pdf)).

### dapam_4_2 — The quick–screen edit

> This editing tool identifies major violations of the Army standard for writing. [...] In a single, rapid reading highlight, circle, or underline– (1) The “bottom line,” the purpose of the piece of writing. (2) Any forms of the verb to be used with a past participle ( a verb ending in “–en” or “–ed”). This identifies the passive voice. (3) Any unnecessary long words or jargon. (4) Spelling, punctuation, or grammar mistakes.

Source: [Headquarters, Department of the Army. DA Pamphlet 600-67, Effective Writing for Army Leaders](https://armywriter.com/DAP600-67.pdf), DA PAM 600-67 (2 June 1986), para 4-2 'The quick-screen edit', printed p. 2.

### dapam_4_3 — The clarity index

> The clarity index is based on word and sentence length. [...] (3) Divide the number of words by the number of sentences to get the average sentence length. (The target average is 15 words per sentence. (4) Count the number of words that have three syllables or more. (5) Divide the number of long words by the total of words to determine the percentage of long words. (The target is 15 percent.) (6) Add the average sentence length to the percentage of long words. (7) The sum is the clarity index. (The target is 30.)

Source: [Headquarters, Department of the Army. DA Pamphlet 600-67, Effective Writing for Army Leaders](https://armywriter.com/DAP600-67.pdf), DA PAM 600-67 (2 June 1986), para 4-3 'The clarity index', printed p. 2.

From the source: Below 20, writing is too abrupt. Over 40, writing is difficult to understand. Aim for an index of 30.

Examples: `da-pam-600-67-2` ([Headquarters, Department of the Army. DA Pamphlet 600-67, Effective Writing for Army Leaders](https://armywriter.com/DAP600-67.pdf)).

### kent_1 — make clear to the reader what is certain knowledge and what is reasoned judgment

> It should set forth the community's findings in such a way as to make clear to the reader what is certain knowledge and what is reasoned judgment, and within this large realm of judgment what varying degrees of certitude lie behind each key statement.

Source: [Kent, S. Words of Estimative Probability. Studies in Intelligence 8(4)](https://www.cia.gov/resources/csi/static/Words-of-Estimative-Probability.pdf), Kent, 'Words of Estimative Probability', Studies in Intelligence 8(4), 1964; CIA CSI web edition (archived 2015 copy), section 'Estimative Uncertainty', paragraph on the NIE.

Examples: `kent-wep-1` ([Kent, S. Words of Estimative Probability. Studies in Intelligence 8(4)](https://www.cia.gov/resources/csi/static/Words-of-Estimative-Probability.pdf)).

### kent_2 — The first cardinal rule

> The first cardinal rule to emerge was thus, "The word `possible' (and its cognates (6) ) must not be modified." The urge to drop into ordinary usage and write "just possible," "barely possible," "a distinct [or good] possibility," and so on must be suppressed.

Source: [Kent, S. Words of Estimative Probability. Studies in Intelligence 8(4)](https://www.cia.gov/resources/csi/static/Words-of-Estimative-Probability.pdf), Kent, 'Words of Estimative Probability', Studies in Intelligence 8(4), 1964; CIA CSI web edition (archived 2015 copy), section 'Early Brush with Ambiguity', end.

Examples: `kent-wep-2` ([Kent, S. Words of Estimative Probability. Studies in Intelligence 8(4)](https://www.cia.gov/resources/csi/static/Words-of-Estimative-Probability.pdf)).

### kent_3 — meet these key estimates head on

> Let us meet these key estimates head on. Let us isolate and seize upon exactly the thing that needs estimating. Let us endeavor to make clear to the reader that the passage in question is of critical importance--the gut estimate, as we call it among ourselves.

Source: [Kent, S. Words of Estimative Probability. Studies in Intelligence 8(4)](https://www.cia.gov/resources/csi/static/Words-of-Estimative-Probability.pdf), Kent, 'Words of Estimative Probability', Studies in Intelligence 8(4), 1964; CIA CSI web edition (archived 2015 copy), closing section, paragraph addressed to 'the brotherhood of the NIE'.

### kent_4 — assign it a word or phrase chosen from one of the five rough categories of likelihood

> Let us talk of it in terms of odds or chances, and when we have made our best judgment let us assign it a word or phrase that is chosen from one of the five rough categories of likelihood on the chart. Let the judgment be unmistakable and let it be unmistakably ours.

Source: [Kent, S. Words of Estimative Probability. Studies in Intelligence 8(4)](https://www.cia.gov/resources/csi/static/Words-of-Estimative-Probability.pdf), Kent, 'Words of Estimative Probability', Studies in Intelligence 8(4), 1964; CIA CSI web edition (archived 2015 copy), closing section (rule) and section 'An Odds Table' (chart).

Examples: `kent-wep-3` ([Kent, S. Words of Estimative Probability. Studies in Intelligence 8(4)](https://www.cia.gov/resources/csi/static/Words-of-Estimative-Probability.pdf)).

### kent_5 — use the word "possible" ... with no modifier

> If the matter is important and cannot be assigned an order of likelihood, but is plainly something which is neither certain to come about nor impossible, let us use the word "possible" or one of its stand-ins--and with no modifier.

Source: [Kent, S. Words of Estimative Probability. Studies in Intelligence 8(4)](https://www.cia.gov/resources/csi/static/Words-of-Estimative-Probability.pdf), Kent, 'Words of Estimative Probability', Studies in Intelligence 8(4), 1964; CIA CSI web edition (archived 2015 copy), closing section.

Applies: If the matter is important and cannot be assigned an order of likelihood, but is plainly something which is neither certain to come about nor impossible

### kent_6 — use them sparingly

> If use them we must in NIEs, let us try to use them sparingly and in places where they are least likely to obscure the thrust of our key estimative passages.

Source: [Kent, S. Words of Estimative Probability. Studies in Intelligence 8(4)](https://www.cia.gov/resources/csi/static/Words-of-Estimative-Probability.pdf), Kent, 'Words of Estimative Probability', Studies in Intelligence 8(4), 1964; CIA CSI web edition (archived 2015 copy), section on 'apparently', 'seemingly', 'fear', 'hope' and similar expressions.

### ipcc_general — findings that are general enough

> It is important for author teams to develop findings that are general enough to reflect the underlying evidence but not so general that they lose substantive meaning.

Source: [Mastrandrea, M. D. et al. Guidance Note for Lead Authors of the IPCC Fifth Assessment Report on Consistent Treatment of Uncertainties](https://www.ipcc.ch/site/assets/uploads/2017/08/AR5_Uncertainty_Guidance_Note.pdf), IPCC AR5 Guidance Note on Consistent Treatment of Uncertainties (Mastrandrea et al. 2010), introduction, p. 1.

### ipcc_conditional — findings (effects) that are conditional on other findings (causes)

> For findings (effects) that are conditional on other findings (causes), consider independently evaluating the degrees of certainty in both causes and effects, with the understanding that the degree of certainty in the causes may be low.

Source: [Mastrandrea, M. D. et al. Guidance Note for Lead Authors of the IPCC Fifth Assessment Report on Consistent Treatment of Uncertainties](https://www.ipcc.ch/site/assets/uploads/2017/08/AR5_Uncertainty_Guidance_Note.pdf), IPCC AR5 Guidance Note on Consistent Treatment of Uncertainties (Mastrandrea et al. 2010), introduction, p. 1.

Applies: For findings (effects) that are conditional on other findings (causes)

### ipcc_tails — information on the tails of distributions

> Author teams are therefore encouraged to provide information on the tails of distributions of key variables, reporting quantitative estimates when possible and supplying qualitative assessments and evaluations when appropriate.

Source: [Mastrandrea, M. D. et al. Guidance Note for Lead Authors of the IPCC Fifth Assessment Report on Consistent Treatment of Uncertainties](https://www.ipcc.ch/site/assets/uploads/2017/08/AR5_Uncertainty_Guidance_Note.pdf), IPCC AR5 Guidance Note on Consistent Treatment of Uncertainties (Mastrandrea et al. 2010), introduction, p. 1.

Applies: reporting quantitative estimates when possible and supplying qualitative assessments and evaluations when appropriate

### ipcc_2 — traceable account

> 2) Be prepared to make expert judgments in developing key findings, and to explain those judgments by providing a traceable account: a description in the chapter text of your evaluation of the type, amount, quality, and consistency of evidence and the degree of agreement, which together form the basis for a given key finding.

Source: [Mastrandrea, M. D. et al. Guidance Note for Lead Authors of the IPCC Fifth Assessment Report on Consistent Treatment of Uncertainties](https://www.ipcc.ch/site/assets/uploads/2017/08/AR5_Uncertainty_Guidance_Note.pdf), IPCC AR5 Guidance Note on Consistent Treatment of Uncertainties (Mastrandrea et al. 2010), paragraph 2 ('Review the information available'), p. 2.

### ipcc_4 — the way in which a statement is framed

> 4) Be aware that the way in which a statement is framed will have an effect on how it is interpreted (e.g., a 10% chance of dying is interpreted more negatively than a 90% chance of surviving). [...] Consider reciprocal statements to avoid value-laden interpretations (e.g., report chances both of dying and of surviving).

Source: [Mastrandrea, M. D. et al. Guidance Note for Lead Authors of the IPCC Fifth Assessment Report on Consistent Treatment of Uncertainties](https://www.ipcc.ch/site/assets/uploads/2017/08/AR5_Uncertainty_Guidance_Note.pdf), IPCC AR5 Guidance Note on Consistent Treatment of Uncertainties (Mastrandrea et al. 2010), paragraph 4, p. 2.

### ipcc_5 — statements of fact without using uncertainty qualifiers

> 5) Consider that, in some cases, it may be appropriate to describe findings for which evidence and understanding are overwhelming as statements of fact without using uncertainty qualifiers.

Source: [Mastrandrea, M. D. et al. Guidance Note for Lead Authors of the IPCC Fifth Assessment Report on Consistent Treatment of Uncertainties](https://www.ipcc.ch/site/assets/uploads/2017/08/AR5_Uncertainty_Guidance_Note.pdf), IPCC AR5 Guidance Note on Consistent Treatment of Uncertainties (Mastrandrea et al. 2010), paragraph 5, p. 2.

Applies: in some cases, it may be appropriate to describe findings for which evidence and understanding are overwhelming

### ipcc_8 — evidence and agreement

> 8) Use the following dimensions to evaluate the validity of a finding: the type, amount, quality, and consistency of evidence (summary terms: “limited,” “medium,” or “robust”), and the degree of agreement (summary terms: “low,” “medium,” or “high”). [...] Provide a traceable account describing your evaluation of evidence and agreement in the text of your chapter.

Source: [Mastrandrea, M. D. et al. Guidance Note for Lead Authors of the IPCC Fifth Assessment Report on Consistent Treatment of Uncertainties](https://www.ipcc.ch/site/assets/uploads/2017/08/AR5_Uncertainty_Guidance_Note.pdf), IPCC AR5 Guidance Note on Consistent Treatment of Uncertainties (Mastrandrea et al. 2010), paragraph 8 ('Evaluate and communicate at the appropriate level of precision'), p. 2.

### ipcc_8a — high agreement and robust evidence

> For findings with high agreement and robust evidence, present a level of confidence or a quantified measure of uncertainty.

Source: [Mastrandrea, M. D. et al. Guidance Note for Lead Authors of the IPCC Fifth Assessment Report on Consistent Treatment of Uncertainties](https://www.ipcc.ch/site/assets/uploads/2017/08/AR5_Uncertainty_Guidance_Note.pdf), IPCC AR5 Guidance Note on Consistent Treatment of Uncertainties (Mastrandrea et al. 2010), paragraph 8, first bullet, p. 2.

Applies: For findings with high agreement and robust evidence

### ipcc_8b — high agreement or robust evidence, but not both

> For findings with high agreement or robust evidence, but not both, assign confidence or quantify uncertainty when possible. Otherwise, assign the appropriate combination of summary terms for your evaluation of evidence and agreement (e.g., robust evidence, medium agreement).

Source: [Mastrandrea, M. D. et al. Guidance Note for Lead Authors of the IPCC Fifth Assessment Report on Consistent Treatment of Uncertainties](https://www.ipcc.ch/site/assets/uploads/2017/08/AR5_Uncertainty_Guidance_Note.pdf), IPCC AR5 Guidance Note on Consistent Treatment of Uncertainties (Mastrandrea et al. 2010), paragraph 8, second bullet, p. 2.

Applies: For findings with high agreement or robust evidence, but not both

### ipcc_8c — low agreement and limited evidence

> For findings with low agreement and limited evidence, assign summary terms for your evaluation of evidence and agreement.

Source: [Mastrandrea, M. D. et al. Guidance Note for Lead Authors of the IPCC Fifth Assessment Report on Consistent Treatment of Uncertainties](https://www.ipcc.ch/site/assets/uploads/2017/08/AR5_Uncertainty_Guidance_Note.pdf), IPCC AR5 Guidance Note on Consistent Treatment of Uncertainties (Mastrandrea et al. 2010), paragraph 8, third bullet, p. 3.

Applies: For findings with low agreement and limited evidence

### ipcc_8d — findings that are conditional on other findings

> In any of these cases, the degree of certainty in findings that are conditional on other findings should be evaluated and reported separately.

Source: [Mastrandrea, M. D. et al. Guidance Note for Lead Authors of the IPCC Fifth Assessment Report on Consistent Treatment of Uncertainties](https://www.ipcc.ch/site/assets/uploads/2017/08/AR5_Uncertainty_Guidance_Note.pdf), IPCC AR5 Guidance Note on Consistent Treatment of Uncertainties (Mastrandrea et al. 2010), paragraph 8, fourth bullet, p. 3.

Applies: findings that are conditional on other findings

### ipcc_9 — level of confidence

> 9) A level of confidence is expressed using five qualifiers: “very low,” “low,” “medium,” “high,” and “very high.” [...] Presentation of findings with “low” and “very low” confidence should be reserved for areas of major concern, and the reasons for their presentation should be carefully explained. Confidence should not be interpreted probabilistically, and it is distinct from “statistical confidence.”

Source: [Mastrandrea, M. D. et al. Guidance Note for Lead Authors of the IPCC Fifth Assessment Report on Consistent Treatment of Uncertainties](https://www.ipcc.ch/site/assets/uploads/2017/08/AR5_Uncertainty_Guidance_Note.pdf), IPCC AR5 Guidance Note on Consistent Treatment of Uncertainties (Mastrandrea et al. 2010), paragraph 9, p. 3.

Applies: a finding that includes a probabilistic measure of uncertainty does not require explicit mention of the level of confidence associated with that finding if the level of confidence is “high” or “very high.”

### ipcc_10 — Likelihood

> 10) Likelihood, as defined in Table 1, provides calibrated language for describing quantified uncertainty. [...] When there is sufficient information, it is preferable to specify the full probability distribution or a probability range (e.g., 90–95%) without using the terms in Table 1. “About as likely as not” should not be used to express a lack of knowledge (see Paragraph 8 for that situation).

Source: [Mastrandrea, M. D. et al. Guidance Note for Lead Authors of the IPCC Fifth Assessment Report on Consistent Treatment of Uncertainties](https://www.ipcc.ch/site/assets/uploads/2017/08/AR5_Uncertainty_Guidance_Note.pdf), IPCC AR5 Guidance Note on Consistent Treatment of Uncertainties (Mastrandrea et al. 2010), paragraph 10 and Table 1, p. 3.

Applies: When there is sufficient information

### ipcc_11 — Characterize key findings regarding a variable

> 11) Characterize key findings regarding a variable (e.g., a measured, simulated, or derived quantity or its change) using calibrated uncertainty language that conveys the most information to the reader, based on the criteria (A-F) below. [...] recognizing that in all cases it is important to include a traceable account of relevant evidence and agreement in your chapter text.

Source: [Mastrandrea, M. D. et al. Guidance Note for Lead Authors of the IPCC Fifth Assessment Report on Consistent Treatment of Uncertainties](https://www.ipcc.ch/site/assets/uploads/2017/08/AR5_Uncertainty_Guidance_Note.pdf), IPCC AR5 Guidance Note on Consistent Treatment of Uncertainties (Mastrandrea et al. 2010), paragraph 11, p. 3.

### ipcc_11a — A) variable ambiguous or processes poorly known

> Confidence should not be assigned; assign summary terms for evidence and agreement (see Paragraph 8). Explain the governing factors, key indicators, and [...] relationships. If a variable could be either positive or negative, describe the pre-conditions or evidence for each.

Source: [Mastrandrea, M. D. et al. Guidance Note for Lead Authors of the IPCC Fifth Assessment Report on Consistent Treatment of Uncertainties](https://www.ipcc.ch/site/assets/uploads/2017/08/AR5_Uncertainty_Guidance_Note.pdf), IPCC AR5 Guidance Note on Consistent Treatment of Uncertainties (Mastrandrea et al. 2010), paragraph 11 A, p. 3-4.

Applies: A) A variable is ambiguous, or the processes determining it are poorly known or not amenable to measurement:

### ipcc_11b — B) sign known, magnitude poorly known

> Assign confidence when possible; otherwise assign summary terms for evidence and agreement (see Paragraphs 8 and 9). Explain the basis for this confidence evaluation and the extent to which opposite changes would not be expected.

Source: [Mastrandrea, M. D. et al. Guidance Note for Lead Authors of the IPCC Fifth Assessment Report on Consistent Treatment of Uncertainties](https://www.ipcc.ch/site/assets/uploads/2017/08/AR5_Uncertainty_Guidance_Note.pdf), IPCC AR5 Guidance Note on Consistent Treatment of Uncertainties (Mastrandrea et al. 2010), paragraph 11 B, p. 4.

Applies: B) The sign of a variable can be identified but the magnitude is poorly known:

### ipcc_11c — C) order of magnitude

> Assign confidence when possible; otherwise assign summary terms for evidence and agreement (see Paragraphs 8 and 9). Explain the basis for estimates and confidence evaluations made, and indicate any assumptions. If the evaluation is particularly sensitive to specific assumptions, then also evaluate confidence in those assumptions.

Source: [Mastrandrea, M. D. et al. Guidance Note for Lead Authors of the IPCC Fifth Assessment Report on Consistent Treatment of Uncertainties](https://www.ipcc.ch/site/assets/uploads/2017/08/AR5_Uncertainty_Guidance_Note.pdf), IPCC AR5 Guidance Note on Consistent Treatment of Uncertainties (Mastrandrea et al. 2010), paragraph 11 C, p. 4.

Applies: C) An order of magnitude can be given for a variable:

### ipcc_11d — D) a range can be given

> Assign likelihood or probability for that range when possible; otherwise only assign confidence (see Paragraphs 8-10). Explain the basis for the range given, noting factors that determine the outer bounds. State any assumptions made and estimate the role of structural uncertainties. Report likelihood or probability for values or changes outside the range, if appropriate.

Source: [Mastrandrea, M. D. et al. Guidance Note for Lead Authors of the IPCC Fifth Assessment Report on Consistent Treatment of Uncertainties](https://www.ipcc.ch/site/assets/uploads/2017/08/AR5_Uncertainty_Guidance_Note.pdf), IPCC AR5 Guidance Note on Consistent Treatment of Uncertainties (Mastrandrea et al. 2010), paragraph 11 D, p. 4.

Applies: D) A range can be given for a variable, based on quantitative analysis or expert judgment:

### ipcc_11e — E) a likelihood or probability can be determined

> Assign a likelihood for the event or outcomes, for which confidence should be “high” or “very high” (see Paragraphs 8-10). In this case, the level of confidence need not be explicitly stated. State any assumptions made and estimate the role of structural uncertainties. Consider characterizing the likelihood or probability of other events or outcomes within the full set of alternatives, including those at the tails.

Source: [Mastrandrea, M. D. et al. Guidance Note for Lead Authors of the IPCC Fifth Assessment Report on Consistent Treatment of Uncertainties](https://www.ipcc.ch/site/assets/uploads/2017/08/AR5_Uncertainty_Guidance_Note.pdf), IPCC AR5 Guidance Note on Consistent Treatment of Uncertainties (Mastrandrea et al. 2010), paragraph 11 E, p. 4.

Applies: E) A likelihood or probability can be determined for a variable, for the occurrence of an event, or for a range of outcomes (e.g., based on multiple observations, model ensemble runs, or expert judgment):

### ipcc_11f — F) a probability distribution can be determined

> Present the probability distribution(s) graphically and/or provide a range of percentiles of the distribution(s), for which confidence should be “high” or “very high” (see Paragraphs 8-10). In this case, the level of confidence need not be explicitly stated. Explain the method used to produce the probability distribution(s) and any assumptions made, and estimate the role of structural uncertainties. Provide quantification of the tails of the distribution(s) to the extent possible.

Source: [Mastrandrea, M. D. et al. Guidance Note for Lead Authors of the IPCC Fifth Assessment Report on Consistent Treatment of Uncertainties](https://www.ipcc.ch/site/assets/uploads/2017/08/AR5_Uncertainty_Guidance_Note.pdf), IPCC AR5 Guidance Note on Consistent Treatment of Uncertainties (Mastrandrea et al. 2010), paragraph 11 F, p. 4.

Applies: F) A probability distribution or a set of distributions can be determined for the variable either through statistical analysis or through use of a formal quantitative survey of expert views:

Contributes rather than criteria:

- [Mastrandrea, M. D. et al. Guidance Note for Lead Authors of the IPCC Fifth Assessment Report on Consistent Treatment of Uncertainties](https://www.ipcc.ch/site/assets/uploads/2017/08/AR5_Uncertainty_Guidance_Note.pdf) (methods): Paragraphs 1 (agree a process for describing ranges of views), 3 (guard against group convergence and anchoring), 6 (consider all plausible sources of uncertainty) and 7 (assess uncertainty and risk; consider ranges and high-consequence outcomes) direct the author team's process rather than the text, so they are not scored. The closing summary restates paragraphs 2, 8 and 10.
- [Mastrandrea, M. D. et al. Guidance Note for Lead Authors of the IPCC Fifth Assessment Report on Consistent Treatment of Uncertainties](https://www.ipcc.ch/site/assets/uploads/2017/08/AR5_Uncertainty_Guidance_Note.pdf) (examples): Example c13 (AR5 WG1 Summary for Policymakers, D.3) uses the note's calibrated language, but the guidance note does not cite or discuss it, so it is not attached to any ipcc criterion.
- [Intelligence Community Directive 203: Analytic Standards (ODNI)](https://www.dni.gov/files/documents/ICD/ICD-203.pdf) (methods): Section E.1.b(1) refers to 'ODNI Analytic Tradecraft Standards Evaluation Rating Criteria' used in product reviews; ICD 203 does not publish them, so no levels are given for the ICD 203 criteria.
- [U.S. Senate Select Committee on Intelligence. Report on the U.S. Intelligence Community's Prewar Intelligence Assessments on Iraq (S. Rept. 108-301), Section X](https://irp.fas.org/congress/2004_rpt/ssci_whitepaper.pdf) (examples): Examples c6 and c7 (2002 NIE key judgments vs the CIA white paper) are left unattached. The critique (removing 'we judge'/'we assess' turned assessments into statements of fact; Conclusion 85) is the Senate committee's, written in 2004 against the pre-ICD-203 tradecraft. ICD 203 (2007, re-issued 2015) never cites them. They match icd203-t2 and icd203-t3 in substance, but attaching them would claim a tie that ICD 203 does not make.
- [Headquarters, Department of the Army. DA Pamphlet 600-67, Effective Writing for Army Leaders](https://armywriter.com/DAP600-67.pdf) (methods): The quick-screen edit (4-2) and clarity index (4-3) are the pamphlet's own enforcement tools; they are kept as criteria because the pamphlet says they identify violations of, and enforce, the standard (5-1b).

Gaps: ICD 203 text is the current dni.gov PDF (signed 2 Jan 2015 with later technical amendments, incl. 2022 ombuds changes), fetched via a Wayback copy of 2024-12-22; it is byte-identical to the earlier saved copy. Its PDF text layer drops some spaces ('oflikelihood', 'whichjudgments'), which the whitespace-insensitive check tolerates. The likelihood term table was rebuilt from the column layout and checked term by term, not as running text. Kent is quoted from the CIA CSI web edition (archived 2015 HTML), not the cia.gov PDF named in references.json. The IPCC PDF text layer drops the en dash in '90–95%'; dashes are ignored in the check. Only DA PAM 600-67 publishes anything like levels (the clarity index rules of thumb) and published scores for an attached example (a31: 42 vs 23). Figures 4-1/4-2 and 4-4/4-5 have no entry in the verified example sets, so they are not attached. No example is attached to ICD 203 or IPCC criteria.

## Judge reliability

Sources: [Burrows, J. 'Delta': a Measure of Stylistic Difference and a Guide to Likely Authorship. Literary and Linguistic Computing 17(3):267–287](https://doi.org/10.1093/llc/17.3.267); [Zhang, X. et al. From Lists to Emojis: How Format Bias Affects Model Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.11704); [Dubois, Y. et al. Length-Controlled AlpacaEval: A Simple Way to Debias Automatic Evaluators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.04475); [Huang, H. et al. An Empirical Study of LLM-as-a-Judge for LLM Evaluation: Fine-tuned Judge Model is not a General Substitute for GPT-4](https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.02839); [Zheng, L. et al. Judging LLM-as-a-Judge with MT-Bench and Chatbot Arena. NeurIPS Datasets and Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2306.05685); [Saito, K. et al. Verbosity Bias in Preference Labeling by Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.10076).

Contributes rather than criteria:

- [Zheng, L. et al. Judging LLM-as-a-Judge with MT-Bench and Chatbot Arena. NeurIPS Datasets and Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2306.05685) (bias_checks): Checks LLM-as-a-judge for position bias, verbosity bias, self-enhancement bias and limited reasoning ability, against expert and crowd human preferences (MT-Bench, Chatbot Arena).
- [Saito, K. et al. Verbosity Bias in Preference Labeling by Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.10076) (bias_checks): Checks whether an LLM judge prefers the more verbose of two answers of similar quality more often than humans do, with a proposed metric for that bias.
- [Dubois, Y. et al. Length-Controlled AlpacaEval: A Simple Way to Debias Automatic Evaluators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.04475) (bias_checks): Checks and removes length bias in auto-annotator win rates by regressing preferences on length difference and predicting the preference at zero length difference.
- [Zhang, X. et al. From Lists to Emojis: How Format Bias Affects Model Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.11704) (bias_checks): Checks whether judges and reward models favour format patterns (lists, bold, links, emojis, length) independent of content quality.
- [Huang, H. et al. An Empirical Study of LLM-as-a-Judge for LLM Evaluation: Fine-tuned Judge Model is not a General Substitute for GPT-4](https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.02839) (bias_checks): Checks whether fine-tuned judge models generalise beyond their training scheme (generalisability, fairness, adaptability); they behave as task-specific classifiers and underperform GPT-4 out of domain.
- [Burrows, J. 'Delta': a Measure of Stylistic Difference and a Guide to Likely Authorship. Literary and Linguistic Computing 17(3):267–287](https://doi.org/10.1093/llc/17.3.267) (bias_checks): Stylometric distance over z-scored frequencies of the most common words; used to measure how close a candidate's style is to a reference author so that style-match (self-/author-preference) bias in judge scores can be detected.

Gaps: Notes are our one-sentence summaries, not quotes. The five arXiv papers were checked against their abstracts (saved in f3/bias/). Burrows (2002) is paywalled (OUP returned 403; Crossref has no abstract), so its note rests on the title and general knowledge and was not checked against a saved file.

## General style guidance (classic texts on writing in general)

Sources: [Orwell, G. Politics and the English Language. Horizon 13(76), April 1946 (text as published by The Orwell Foundation)](https://www.orwellfoundation.com/the-orwell-foundation/orwell/essays-and-other-works/politics-and-the-english-language/); [Quiller-Couch, A. On the Art of Writing: Lectures delivered in the University of Cambridge, 1913–1914 (Project Gutenberg eBook #17470)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/17470); [Strunk, W., Jr. The Elements of Style. Ithaca, NY: privately printed (Project Gutenberg eBook #37134)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/37134); [Zinsser, W. Writing English as a Second Language. The American Scholar, 2010 (talk to Columbia Graduate School of Journalism international students)](https://theamericanscholar.org/writing-english-as-a-second-language/).

Rubric id `general_style_guidance`: Rules of general prose style from Strunk (1918), Quiller-Couch (1916), Orwell (1946) and Zinsser (2010).

### strunk_08_paragraph_unit — Make the paragraph the unit of composition: one paragraph to each topic.

> Ordinarily, however, a subject requires subdivision into topics, each of which should be made the subject of a paragraph. The object of treating each topic in a paragraph by itself is, of course, to aid the reader. The beginning of each paragraph is a signal to him that a new step in the development of the subject has been reached.

Source: [Strunk, W., Jr. The Elements of Style. Ithaca, NY: privately printed (Project Gutenberg eBook #37134)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/37134), The Elements of Style (1918), Chapter III Elementary Principles of Composition, Rule 8.

Applies: If the subject on which you are writing is of slight extent, or if you intend to treat it very briefly, there may be no need of subdividing it into topics. ... As a rule, single sentences should not be written or printed as paragraphs. An exception may be made of sentences of transition, indicating the relation between the parts of an exposition or argument. Frequent exceptions are also necessary in textbooks, guidebooks, and other works in which many topics are treated briefly.

### strunk_09_topic_sentence — As a rule, begin each paragraph with a topic sentence, end it in conformity with the beginning.

> the most generally useful kind of paragraph, particularly in exposition and argument, is that in which (a) the topic sentence comes at or near the beginning; (b) the succeeding sentences explain or establish or develop the statement made in the topic sentence; and (c) the final sentence either emphasizes the thought of the topic sentence or states some important consequence. Ending with a digression, or with an unimportant detail, is particularly to be avoided.

Source: [Strunk, W., Jr. The Elements of Style. Ithaca, NY: privately printed (Project Gutenberg eBook #37134)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/37134), The Elements of Style (1918), Chapter III Elementary Principles of Composition, Rule 9.

Applies: If the paragraph forms part of a larger composition, its relation to what precedes, or its function as a part of the whole, may need to be expressed. ... The brief paragraphs of animated narrative, however, are often without even this semblance of a topic sentence.

### strunk_10_active_voice — Use the active voice.

> The active voice is usually more direct and vigorous than the passive ... The habitual use of the active voice makes for forcible writing. This is true not only in narrative principally concerned with action, but in writing of any kind.

Source: [Strunk, W., Jr. The Elements of Style. Ithaca, NY: privately printed (Project Gutenberg eBook #37134)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/37134), The Elements of Style (1918), Chapter III Elementary Principles of Composition, Rule 10.

Applies: This rule does not, of course, mean that the writer should entirely discard the passive voice, which is frequently convenient and sometimes necessary. ... The need of making a particular word the subject of the sentence will often, as in these examples, determine which voice is to be used.

Examples: `f4-strunk-r10-boston` ([Strunk, W., Jr. The Elements of Style. Ithaca, NY: privately printed (Project Gutenberg eBook #37134)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/37134)), `f4-strunk-r10-survey` ([Strunk, W., Jr. The Elements of Style. Ithaca, NY: privately printed (Project Gutenberg eBook #37134)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/37134)), `f4-strunk-r10-leaves` ([Strunk, W., Jr. The Elements of Style. Ithaca, NY: privately printed (Project Gutenberg eBook #37134)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/37134)).

### strunk_11_positive_form — Put statements in positive form.

> Make definite assertions. Avoid tame, colorless, hesitating, non-committal language. Use the word not as a means of denial or in antithesis, never as a means of evasion. ... Consciously or unconsciously, the reader is dissatisfied with being told only what is not; he wishes to be told what is.

Source: [Strunk, W., Jr. The Elements of Style. Ithaca, NY: privately printed (Project Gutenberg eBook #37134)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/37134), The Elements of Style (1918), Chapter III Elementary Principles of Composition, Rule 11.

Examples: `f4-strunk-r11-late` ([Strunk, W., Jr. The Elements of Style. Ithaca, NY: privately printed (Project Gutenberg eBook #37134)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/37134)).

### strunk_12_definite_specific_concrete — Use definite, specific, concrete language.

> Prefer the specific to the general, the definite to the vague, the concrete to the abstract. ... In exposition and in argument, the writer must likewise never lose his hold upon the concrete, and even when he is dealing with general principles, he must give particular instances of their application.

Source: [Strunk, W., Jr. The Elements of Style. Ithaca, NY: privately printed (Project Gutenberg eBook #37134)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/37134), The Elements of Style (1918), Chapter III Elementary Principles of Composition, Rule 12.

Applies: It is not that every detail is given; that would be impossible, as well as to no purpose; but that all the significant details are given

Examples: `strunk-1918-1` ([Strunk, W., Jr. The Elements of Style. Ithaca, NY: privately printed (Project Gutenberg eBook #37134)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/37134)), `f4-strunk-r12-surf` ([Strunk, W., Jr. The Elements of Style. Ithaca, NY: privately printed (Project Gutenberg eBook #37134)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/37134)).

### strunk_13_omit_needless_words — Omit needless words.

> Vigorous writing is concise. A sentence should contain no unnecessary words, a paragraph no unnecessary sentences, for the same reason that a drawing should have no unnecessary lines and a machine no unnecessary parts. ... A common violation of conciseness is the presentation of a single complex idea, step by step, in a series of sentences or independent clauses which might to advantage be combined into one.

Source: [Strunk, W., Jr. The Elements of Style. Ithaca, NY: privately printed (Project Gutenberg eBook #37134)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/37134), The Elements of Style (1918), Chapter III Elementary Principles of Composition, Rule 13.

Applies: This requires not that the writer make all his sentences short, or that he avoid all detail and treat his subjects only in outline, but that he make every word tell.

Examples: `strunk-1918-2` ([Strunk, W., Jr. The Elements of Style. Ithaca, NY: privately printed (Project Gutenberg eBook #37134)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/37134)), `f4-strunk-r13-courses` ([Strunk, W., Jr. The Elements of Style. Ithaca, NY: privately printed (Project Gutenberg eBook #37134)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/37134)).

### strunk_14_loose_sentences — Avoid a succession of loose sentences

> This rule refers especially to loose sentences of a particular type, those consisting of two co-ordinate clauses, the second introduced by a conjunction or relative. ... he should recast enough of them to remove the monotony, replacing them by simple sentences, by sentences of two clauses joined by a semicolon, by periodic sentences of two clauses, by sentences, loose or periodic, of three clauses--whichever best represent the real relations of the thought.

Source: [Strunk, W., Jr. The Elements of Style. Ithaca, NY: privately printed (Project Gutenberg eBook #37134)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/37134), The Elements of Style (1918), Chapter III Elementary Principles of Composition, Rule 14.

Applies: Although single sentences of this type may be unexceptionable (see under Rule 4), a series soon becomes monotonous and tedious.

### strunk_15_parallel_form — Express co-ordinate ideas in similar form.

> This principle, that of parallel construction, requires that expressions of similar content and function should be outwardly similar. The likeness of form enables the reader to recognize more readily the likeness of content and function.

Source: [Strunk, W., Jr. The Elements of Style. Ithaca, NY: privately printed (Project Gutenberg eBook #37134)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/37134), The Elements of Style (1918), Chapter III Elementary Principles of Composition, Rule 15.

Applies: It is true that in repeating a statement in order to emphasize it he may have need to vary its form.

Examples: `f4-strunk-r15-science` ([Strunk, W., Jr. The Elements of Style. Ithaca, NY: privately printed (Project Gutenberg eBook #37134)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/37134)), `f4-strunk-r15-objections` ([Strunk, W., Jr. The Elements of Style. Ithaca, NY: privately printed (Project Gutenberg eBook #37134)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/37134)).

### strunk_16_related_words_together — Keep related words together.

> The position of the words in a sentence is the principal means of showing their relationship. The writer must therefore, so far as possible, bring together the words, and groups of words, that are related in thought, and keep apart those which are not so related.

Source: [Strunk, W., Jr. The Elements of Style. Ithaca, NY: privately printed (Project Gutenberg eBook #37134)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/37134), The Elements of Style (1918), Chapter III Elementary Principles of Composition, Rule 16.

Applies: Usually, however, this objection does not hold when the order is interrupted only by a relative clause or by an expression in apposition. Nor does it hold in periodic sentences in which the interruption is a deliberately used means of creating suspense (see examples under Rule 18).

Examples: `f4-strunk-r16-only` ([Strunk, W., Jr. The Elements of Style. Ithaca, NY: privately printed (Project Gutenberg eBook #37134)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/37134)), `f4-strunk-r16-lecture` ([Strunk, W., Jr. The Elements of Style. Ithaca, NY: privately printed (Project Gutenberg eBook #37134)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/37134)).

### strunk_17_summaries_one_tense — In summaries, keep to one tense.

> Apart from the exceptions noted, whichever tense the writer chooses, he should use throughout. Shifting from one tense to the other gives the appearance of uncertainty and irresolution (compare Rule 15). ... He should indicate clearly at the outset, once for all, that what follows is summary, and then waste no words in repeating the notification.

Source: [Strunk, W., Jr. The Elements of Style. Ithaca, NY: privately printed (Project Gutenberg eBook #37134)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/37134), The Elements of Style (1918), Chapter III Elementary Principles of Composition, Rule 17.

Applies: But whichever tense be used in the summary, a past tense in indirect discourse or in indirect question remains unchanged.

### strunk_18_emphatic_words_end — Place the emphatic words of a sentence at the end.

> The proper place in the sentence for the word, or group of words, which the writer desires to make most prominent is usually the end. ... The principle that the proper place for what is to be made most prominent is the end applies equally to the words of a sentence, to the sentences of a paragraph, and to the paragraphs of a composition.

Source: [Strunk, W., Jr. The Elements of Style. Ithaca, NY: privately printed (Project Gutenberg eBook #37134)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/37134), The Elements of Style (1918), Chapter III Elementary Principles of Composition, Rule 18.

Applies: The other prominent position in the sentence is the beginning. Any element in the sentence, other than the subject, may become emphatic when placed first.

Examples: `f4-strunk-r18-fortitude` ([Strunk, W., Jr. The Elements of Style. Ithaca, NY: privately printed (Project Gutenberg eBook #37134)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/37134)).

### strunk_v_ready_made_formula — Words and Expressions Commonly Misused

> If the writer will make it his purpose from the beginning to express accurately his own individual thought, and will refuse to be satisfied with a ready-made formula that saves him the trouble of doing so, this last set of expressions will cause him little trouble.

Source: [Strunk, W., Jr. The Elements of Style. Ithaca, NY: privately printed (Project Gutenberg eBook #37134)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/37134), The Elements of Style (1918), Chapter V, prefatory note.

Applies: still others, as case, factor, feature, interesting, one of the most, are good in their place, but are constantly obtruding themselves into places where they have no right to be.

### qc_appropriate — appropriate

> when you talk or write, you would wish to observe the occasion; to say what you have to say without impertinence or ill-timed excess. ... this desire of yours to be appropriate is really the root of the matter.

Source: [Quiller-Couch, A. On the Art of Writing: Lectures delivered in the University of Cambridge, 1913–1914 (Project Gutenberg eBook #17470)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/17470), On the Art of Writing (1916), Lecture II The Practice of Writing.

### qc_perspicuity — Perspicuity.

> Perspicuity.--I shall waste no words on the need of this: since the first aim of speech is to be understood. The more clearly you write the more easily and surely you will be understood.

Source: [Quiller-Couch, A. On the Art of Writing: Lectures delivered in the University of Cambridge, 1913–1914 (Project Gutenberg eBook #17470)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/17470), On the Art of Writing (1916), Lecture II The Practice of Writing.

### qc_accuracy — Accuracy.

> Accuracy.--Did I not remind myself in my first lecture, that Cambridge is the home of accurate scholarship? Surely no Cambridge man would willingly be a sloven in speech, oral or written? ... Does it not follow, then, that the more accurately we use words the closer definition we shall give to our thoughts?

Source: [Quiller-Couch, A. On the Art of Writing: Lectures delivered in the University of Cambridge, 1913–1914 (Project Gutenberg eBook #17470)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/17470), On the Art of Writing (1916), Lecture II The Practice of Writing.

### qc_persuasiveness — Persuasiveness

> Persuasiveness; of which you may say, indeed, that it embraces the whole--not only the qualities of propriety, perspicuity, accuracy, we have been considering, but many another, such as harmony, order, sublimity, beauty of diction; all in short that--writing being an art, not a science, and therefore so personal a thing--may be summed up under the word Charm.

Source: [Quiller-Couch, A. On the Art of Writing: Lectures delivered in the University of Cambridge, 1913–1914 (Project Gutenberg eBook #17470)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/17470), On the Art of Writing (1916), Lecture II The Practice of Writing.

### qc_jargon_circumlocution — the two main vices of Jargon (the first)

> Have you begun to detect the two main vices of Jargon? The first is that it uses circumlocution rather than short straight speech.

Source: [Quiller-Couch, A. On the Art of Writing: Lectures delivered in the University of Cambridge, 1913–1914 (Project Gutenberg eBook #17470)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/17470), On the Art of Writing (1916), Lecture V Interlude: On Jargon.

### qc_jargon_abstract_nouns — the two main vices of Jargon (the second)

> The second vice is that it habitually chooses vague woolly abstract nouns rather than concrete ones.

Source: [Quiller-Couch, A. On the Art of Writing: Lectures delivered in the University of Cambridge, 1913–1914 (Project Gutenberg eBook #17470)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/17470), On the Art of Writing (1916), Lecture V Interlude: On Jargon.

Examples: `f4-qc-abstract-times` ([Quiller-Couch, A. On the Art of Writing: Lectures delivered in the University of Cambridge, 1913–1914 (Project Gutenberg eBook #17470)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/17470)).

### qc_rule_case_words — The first is:--Whenever in your reading you come across one of these words, case, instance, character, nature, condition, persuasion, degree

> The first is:--Whenever in your reading you come across one of these words, case, instance, character, nature, condition, persuasion, degree--whenever in writing your pen betrays you to one or another of them--pull yourself up and take thought.

Source: [Quiller-Couch, A. On the Art of Writing: Lectures delivered in the University of Cambridge, 1913–1914 (Project Gutenberg eBook #17470)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/17470), On the Art of Writing (1916), Lecture V Interlude: On Jargon (first of "one or two extremely rough rules").

Examples: `f4-qc-jargon-degree` ([Quiller-Couch, A. On the Art of Writing: Lectures delivered in the University of Cambridge, 1913–1914 (Project Gutenberg eBook #17470)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/17470)), `f4-qc-jargon-condition` ([Quiller-Couch, A. On the Art of Writing: Lectures delivered in the University of Cambridge, 1913–1914 (Project Gutenberg eBook #17470)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/17470)), `f4-qc-jargon-aintree` ([Quiller-Couch, A. On the Art of Writing: Lectures delivered in the University of Cambridge, 1913–1914 (Project Gutenberg eBook #17470)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/17470)).

### qc_rule_as_regards — Train your suspicions to bristle up whenever you come upon 'as regards,' 'with regard to,' 'in respect of,' 'in connection with,' 'according as to whether,' and the like.

> Train your suspicions to bristle up whenever you come upon 'as regards,' 'with regard to,' 'in respect of,' 'in connection with,' 'according as to whether,' and the like. They are all dodges of Jargon, circumlocutions for evading this or that simple statement

Source: [Quiller-Couch, A. On the Art of Writing: Lectures delivered in the University of Cambridge, 1913–1914 (Project Gutenberg eBook #17470)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/17470), On the Art of Writing (1916), Lecture V Interlude: On Jargon ("another rule--just as rough and ready").

Examples: `f4-qc-asto-game` ([Quiller-Couch, A. On the Art of Writing: Lectures delivered in the University of Cambridge, 1913–1914 (Project Gutenberg eBook #17470)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/17470)).

### qc_elegant_variation — the trick of Elegant Variation

> Let us turn to another trick of Jargon: the trick of Elegant Variation ... In an essay on Byron, Byron is (or ought to be) mentioned many times. I expect, nay exact, that Bryon shall be mentioned again and again. ... The Gospel does not, like my young essayist, fear to repeat a word, if the word be good.

Source: [Quiller-Couch, A. On the Art of Writing: Lectures delivered in the University of Cambridge, 1913–1914 (Project Gutenberg eBook #17470)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/17470), On the Art of Writing (1916), Lecture V Interlude: On Jargon.

### qc_active_verb_concrete_noun — The first virtue, the touchstone of a masculine style

> The first virtue, the touchstone of a masculine style, is its use of the active verb and the concrete noun.

Source: [Quiller-Couch, A. On the Art of Writing: Lectures delivered in the University of Cambridge, 1913–1914 (Project Gutenberg eBook #17470)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/17470), On the Art of Writing (1916), Lecture V Interlude: On Jargon.

Examples: `f4-qc-active-teapot` ([Quiller-Couch, A. On the Art of Writing: Lectures delivered in the University of Cambridge, 1913–1914 (Project Gutenberg eBook #17470)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/17470)).

### qc_concrete_term_maxim — our maxim--to prefer the concrete term to the abstract, the particular to the general, the definite to the vague

> men who have written learnedly on the art agree in treating our maxim--to prefer the concrete term to the abstract, the particular to the general, the definite to the vague--as a canon of rhetoric.

Source: [Quiller-Couch, A. On the Art of Writing: Lectures delivered in the University of Cambridge, 1913–1914 (Project Gutenberg eBook #17470)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/17470), On the Art of Writing (1916), Lecture V Interlude: On Jargon.

Examples: `f4-qc-burke-brougham` ([Quiller-Couch, A. On the Art of Writing: Lectures delivered in the University of Cambridge, 1913–1914 (Project Gutenberg eBook #17470)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/17470)).

### qc_murder_your_darlings — Murder your darlings.

> Style, for example, is not--can never be--extraneous Ornament. ... 'Whenever you feel an impulse to perpetrate a piece of exceptionally fine writing, obey it--whole-heartedly--and delete it before sending your manuscript to press. Murder your darlings.'

Source: [Quiller-Couch, A. On the Art of Writing: Lectures delivered in the University of Cambridge, 1913–1914 (Project Gutenberg eBook #17470)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/17470), On the Art of Writing (1916), Lecture XII On Style.

### orwell_i_stale_figures — i. Never use a metaphor, simile or other figure of speech which you are used to seeing in print.

> one needs rules that one can rely on when instinct fails. I think the following rules will cover most cases: i. Never use a metaphor, simile or other figure of speech which you are used to seeing in print.

Source: [Orwell, G. Politics and the English Language. Horizon 13(76), April 1946 (text as published by The Orwell Foundation)](https://www.orwellfoundation.com/the-orwell-foundation/orwell/essays-and-other-works/politics-and-the-english-language/), 'Politics and the English Language' (1946), Orwell Foundation text, rule i.

Applies: Break any of these rules sooner than say anything outright barbarous. ... I have not here been considering the literary use of language, but merely language as an instrument for expressing and not for concealing or preventing thought.

### orwell_ii_short_word — ii. Never use a long word where a short one will do.

> ii. Never use a long word where a short one will do.

Source: [Orwell, G. Politics and the English Language. Horizon 13(76), April 1946 (text as published by The Orwell Foundation)](https://www.orwellfoundation.com/the-orwell-foundation/orwell/essays-and-other-works/politics-and-the-english-language/), 'Politics and the English Language' (1946), Orwell Foundation text, rule ii.

Applies: Break any of these rules sooner than say anything outright barbarous. ... I have not here been considering the literary use of language, but merely language as an instrument for expressing and not for concealing or preventing thought.

### orwell_iii_cut_word — iii. If it is possible to cut a word out, always cut it out.

> iii. If it is possible to cut a word out, always cut it out.

Source: [Orwell, G. Politics and the English Language. Horizon 13(76), April 1946 (text as published by The Orwell Foundation)](https://www.orwellfoundation.com/the-orwell-foundation/orwell/essays-and-other-works/politics-and-the-english-language/), 'Politics and the English Language' (1946), Orwell Foundation text, rule iii.

Applies: Break any of these rules sooner than say anything outright barbarous. ... I have not here been considering the literary use of language, but merely language as an instrument for expressing and not for concealing or preventing thought.

### orwell_iv_active — iv. Never use the passive where you can use the active.

> iv. Never use the passive where you can use the active.

Source: [Orwell, G. Politics and the English Language. Horizon 13(76), April 1946 (text as published by The Orwell Foundation)](https://www.orwellfoundation.com/the-orwell-foundation/orwell/essays-and-other-works/politics-and-the-english-language/), 'Politics and the English Language' (1946), Orwell Foundation text, rule iv.

Applies: Break any of these rules sooner than say anything outright barbarous. ... I have not here been considering the literary use of language, but merely language as an instrument for expressing and not for concealing or preventing thought.

### orwell_v_everyday_equivalent — v. Never use a foreign phrase, a scientific word or a jargon word if you can think of an everyday English equivalent.

> v. Never use a foreign phrase, a scientific word or a jargon word if you can think of an everyday English equivalent.

Source: [Orwell, G. Politics and the English Language. Horizon 13(76), April 1946 (text as published by The Orwell Foundation)](https://www.orwellfoundation.com/the-orwell-foundation/orwell/essays-and-other-works/politics-and-the-english-language/), 'Politics and the English Language' (1946), Orwell Foundation text, rule v.

Applies: Break any of these rules sooner than say anything outright barbarous. ... I have not here been considering the literary use of language, but merely language as an instrument for expressing and not for concealing or preventing thought.

### orwell_vi_break_rules — vi. Break any of these rules sooner than say anything outright barbarous.

> vi. Break any of these rules sooner than say anything outright barbarous. These rules sound elementary, and so they are, but they demand a deep change of attitude in anyone who has grown used to writing in the style now fashionable. One could keep all of them and still write bad English

Source: [Orwell, G. Politics and the English Language. Horizon 13(76), April 1946 (text as published by The Orwell Foundation)](https://www.orwellfoundation.com/the-orwell-foundation/orwell/essays-and-other-works/politics-and-the-english-language/), 'Politics and the English Language' (1946), Orwell Foundation text, rule vi and the paragraph after the rules.

### orwell_scrupulous_writer_questions — A scrupulous writer, in every sentence that he writes, will ask himself at least four questions

> A scrupulous writer, in every sentence that he writes, will ask himself at least four questions, thus: What am I trying to say? What words will express it? What image or idiom will make it clearer? Is this image fresh enough to have an effect? And he will probably ask himself two more: Could I put it more shortly? Have I said anything that is avoidably ugly?

Source: [Orwell, G. Politics and the English Language. Horizon 13(76), April 1946 (text as published by The Orwell Foundation)](https://www.orwellfoundation.com/the-orwell-foundation/orwell/essays-and-other-works/politics-and-the-english-language/), 'Politics and the English Language' (1946), Orwell Foundation text, paragraph beginning 'As I have tried to show'.

### orwell_meaning_chooses_word — let the meaning choose the word, and not the other way about

> it does imply using the fewest and shortest words that will cover one’s meaning. What is above all needed is to let the meaning choose the word, and not the other way about.

Source: [Orwell, G. Politics and the English Language. Horizon 13(76), April 1946 (text as published by The Orwell Foundation)](https://www.orwellfoundation.com/the-orwell-foundation/orwell/essays-and-other-works/politics-and-the-english-language/), 'Politics and the English Language' (1946), Orwell Foundation text, paragraph beginning 'To begin with it has nothing to do with archaism'.

Applies: It has nothing to do with correct grammar and syntax, which are of no importance so long as one makes one’s meaning clear or with the avoidance of Americanisms, or with having what is called a ‘good prose style’. On the other hand it is not concerned with fake simplicity and the attempt to make written English colloquial.

### orwell_dying_metaphors — Dying metaphors.

> But in between these two classes there is a huge dump of worn-out metaphors which have lost all evocative power and are merely used because they save people the trouble of inventing phrases for themselves. ... incompatible metaphors are frequently mixed, a sure sign that the writer is not interested in what he is saying.

Source: [Orwell, G. Politics and the English Language. Horizon 13(76), April 1946 (text as published by The Orwell Foundation)](https://www.orwellfoundation.com/the-orwell-foundation/orwell/essays-and-other-works/politics-and-the-english-language/), 'Politics and the English Language' (1946), Orwell Foundation text, catalogue of 'tricks', first item.

Applies: A newly invented metaphor assists thought by evoking a visual image, while on the other hand a metaphor which is technically ‘dead’ (e. g. iron resolution) has in effect reverted to being an ordinary word and can generally be used without loss of vividness.

### orwell_operators_false_limbs — Operators, or verbal false limbs.

> These save the trouble of picking out appropriate verbs and nouns, and at the same time pad each sentence with extra syllables which give it an appearance of symmetry. ... The keynote is the elimination of simple verbs. ... Simple conjunctions and prepositions are replaced by such phrases as with respect to, having regard to, the fact that, by dint of, in view of, in the interests of, on the hypothesis that

Source: [Orwell, G. Politics and the English Language. Horizon 13(76), April 1946 (text as published by The Orwell Foundation)](https://www.orwellfoundation.com/the-orwell-foundation/orwell/essays-and-other-works/politics-and-the-english-language/), 'Politics and the English Language' (1946), Orwell Foundation text, catalogue of 'tricks', second item.

### orwell_pretentious_diction — Pretentious diction.

> Words like phenomenon, element, individual (as noun), objective, categorical, effective, virtual, basic, primary, promote, constitute, exhibit, exploit, utilize, eliminate, liquidate, are used to dress up simple statements and give an air of scientific impartiality to biassed judgements. ... The result, in general, is an increase in slovenliness and vagueness.

Source: [Orwell, G. Politics and the English Language. Horizon 13(76), April 1946 (text as published by The Orwell Foundation)](https://www.orwellfoundation.com/the-orwell-foundation/orwell/essays-and-other-works/politics-and-the-english-language/), 'Politics and the English Language' (1946), Orwell Foundation text, catalogue of 'tricks', third item.

Applies: Except for the useful abbreviations i.e., e.g., and etc., there is no real need for any of the hundreds of foreign phrases now current in English.

### orwell_meaningless_words — Meaningless words.

> Words like romantic, plastic, values, human, dead, sentimental, natural, vitality, as used in art criticism, are strictly meaningless, in the sense that they not only do not point to any discoverable object, but are hardly even expected to do so by the reader. ... Words of this kind are often used in a consciously dishonest way.

Source: [Orwell, G. Politics and the English Language. Horizon 13(76), April 1946 (text as published by The Orwell Foundation)](https://www.orwellfoundation.com/the-orwell-foundation/orwell/essays-and-other-works/politics-and-the-english-language/), 'Politics and the English Language' (1946), Orwell Foundation text, catalogue of 'tricks', fourth item.

### zinsser_clarity — Clarity

> I have four principles of writing good English. They are Clarity, Simplicity, Brevity, and Humanity. First, Clarity. If it’s not clear you might as well not write it.

Source: [Zinsser, W. Writing English as a Second Language. The American Scholar, 2010 (talk to Columbia Graduate School of Journalism international students)](https://theamericanscholar.org/writing-english-as-a-second-language/), 'Writing English as a Second Language' (The American Scholar, 2010; Wayback snapshot 2023-12-09).

### zinsser_simplicity — Simplicity

> Two: Simplicity. Simple is good.

Source: [Zinsser, W. Writing English as a Second Language. The American Scholar, 2010 (talk to Columbia Graduate School of Journalism international students)](https://theamericanscholar.org/writing-english-as-a-second-language/), 'Writing English as a Second Language' (The American Scholar, 2010; Wayback snapshot 2023-12-09).

### zinsser_brevity — Brevity

> Principle number 3. Brevity. Short is always better than long. Short sentences are better than long sentences. Short words are better than long words. ... Don’t say anything in writing that you wouldn’t comfortably say in conversation.

Source: [Zinsser, W. Writing English as a Second Language. The American Scholar, 2010 (talk to Columbia Graduate School of Journalism international students)](https://theamericanscholar.org/writing-english-as-a-second-language/), 'Writing English as a Second Language' (The American Scholar, 2010; Wayback snapshot 2023-12-09).

### zinsser_humanity — Humanity

> Which brings me to my fourth principle: Humanity. Be yourself. Never try in your writing to be someone you’re not.

Source: [Zinsser, W. Writing English as a Second Language. The American Scholar, 2010 (talk to Columbia Graduate School of Journalism international students)](https://theamericanscholar.org/writing-english-as-a-second-language/), 'Writing English as a Second Language' (The American Scholar, 2010; Wayback snapshot 2023-12-09).

Contributes rather than criteria:

- [Strunk, W., Jr. The Elements of Style. Ithaca, NY: privately printed (Project Gutenberg eBook #37134)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/37134) (methods): Chapter I states a condition on all the rules: 'It is an old observation that the best writers sometimes disregard the rules of rhetoric. When they do so, however, the reader will usually find in the sentence some compensating merit, attained at the cost of the violation.' Also: 'it gives in Chapter III only those principles of the paragraph and the sentence which are of the widest application.' Chapter II (punctuation and usage), IV (form), VI (spelling) and the rest of V (word-by-word usage entries) are not scorable document criteria and were left out.
- [Strunk, W., Jr. The Elements of Style. Ithaca, NY: privately printed (Project Gutenberg eBook #37134)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/37134) (examples): Rule 9 prints two annotated model paragraphs (Stevenson, Walking Tours; Lecky, The Political Value of History) with sentence-by-sentence function labels; Rule 14 prints a weak-only paragraph of loose sentences. Neither is a before/after pair, so they were not added as examples.
- [Orwell, G. Politics and the English Language. Horizon 13(76), April 1946 (text as published by The Orwell Foundation)](https://www.orwellfoundation.com/the-orwell-foundation/orwell/essays-and-other-works/politics-and-the-english-language/) (examples): Ecclesiastes vs parody pair (new example f4-orwell-ecclesiastes) is printed after the 'catalogue of swindles and perversions' as an illustration of the whole catalogue, not under one rule, so it is not attached to a criterion. Orwell's five numbered specimens (Laski, Hogben, a psychology essay, a Communist pamphlet, a Tribune letter) are weak-only.

Gaps: No source in this family publishes score levels; every criterion has levels null and would need anchors from elsewhere. Strunk Chapter III starts at Rule 8 in the 1918 edition, so Rule 8 (paragraph as unit) is included alongside 9-18; Chapters II, IV, VI and most of V are usage/mechanics and were left out. Quiller-Couch's four qualities (Lecture II) are stated as aims, not tests, and his Jargon rules are 'extremely rough rules'. Orwell's essay is still in copyright in the US (Orwell Foundation notice), and Zinsser 2010 is copyrighted: both are quoted in short excerpts only. Zinsser's 'Short is always better than long' has no condition attached and conflicts with sources (Strunk Rule 13, Redish) that say length alone is not the fault. Pinker (classic style, curse of knowledge) and Williams were not added: no openly published verbatim text of Pinker was fetched, and Williams is already in references.json (williams-style) for the aggregate rubric. Overlaps are kept as the sources state them (e.g. Strunk 10 / Orwell iv / Quiller-Couch 'active verb'; Strunk 12 / Quiller-Couch maxim; Strunk 13 / Orwell iii). The Orwell Ecclesiastes pair is recorded as a new example but not attached to a criterion.

## Large-scale general writing assessment rubrics

Sources: [ACT. The ACT Writing Test Scoring Rubric](https://www.act.org/content/dam/act/unsecured/documents/Writing-Test-Scoring-Rubric.pdf); [ACT.
