ASAP++: Enriching the ASAP Automated Essay Grading Dataset with Essay Attribute Scores. LREC 2018, 1169–1173](https://lwsam.github.io/ASAP++/lrec2018.html); [Crossley, S. A. et al. The English Language Learner Insight, Proficiency and Skills Evaluation (ELLIPSE) Corpus. International Journal of Learner Corpus Research 9(2), 248–269](https://github.com/scrosseye/ELLIPSE-Corpus); [ETS. GRE General Test: Analytical Writing Measure scoring guide (Analyze an Issue; Analyze an Argument for tests before September 22, 2023)](https://www.ets.org/gre/test-takers/general-test/prepare/content/analytical-writing/scoring.html); [ETS. GRE Practice General Test #3: Analytical Writing Sample Essays and Commentaries (large print edition)](https://www.ets.org/content/dam/ets-org/pdfs/gre/gre-practice-test-3-writing-responses-18-point.pdf); [IELTS. Writing Band Descriptors (Task 1 and Task 2), updated May 2023](https://ielts.org/cdn/ielts-guides/ielts-writing-band-descriptors.pdf); [IELTS. Writing Key Assessment Criteria](https://ielts.org/cdn/Guides/ielts-writing-key-assessment-criteria.pdf); [National Assessment Governing Board. Writing Framework for the 2011 National Assessment of Educational Progress](https://www.nagb.gov/content/dam/nagb/en/documents/publications/frameworks/writing/2011-writing-framework.pdf); [National Assessment Governing Board. Writing Framework for the 2017 National Assessment of Educational Progress](https://www.nagb.gov/content/dam/nagb/en/documents/publications/frameworks/writing/2017-writing-framework.pdf); [Crossley, S. A. et al. A large-scale corpus for assessing written argumentation: PERSUADE 2.0. Assessing Writing 61](https://github.com/scrosseye/persuade_corpus_2.0).

Rubric id `writing_assessment_rubrics`: Published scoring guides of NAEP (To Explain), GRE Analytical Writing, IELTS Writing Task 2 and ACT Writing.

### naep_development_of_ideas — Development of Ideas

> Development of ideas is effective in relation to the writer’s purpose and audience. • The depth and complexity of ideas are effective in relation to the writer’s purpose and audience. • Approaches to thinking and writing (e.g., analyzing, synthesizing) are used effectively in relation to the writer’s purpose and audience. • The details and examples used to develop ideas are specific and effective in relation to the writer’s purpose and audience.

Source: [National Assessment Governing Board. Writing Framework for the 2011 National Assessment of Educational Progress](https://www.nagb.gov/content/dam/nagb/en/documents/publications/frameworks/writing/2011-writing-framework.pdf), NAEP 2011 Writing Framework (NAGB), Chapter 4, Exhibit 4.1 Criteria for Evaluating Responses.

Applies: Although not all forms of writing require the same kind or extent of development (e.g., a detailed analysis of a problem versus a brief business letter or memorandum), effective writing is dependent on writers’ ability to anticipate the questions that might arise in the reader’s mind. Effective writers judiciously address those questions, with the depth and extent of the development of ideas determined by the difficulty of the material to be explained and by the purpose and audience for the writing.

### naep_organization_of_ideas — Organization of Ideas

> Organization is logical in relation to the writer’s purpose and audience. • Text structure is logical and effective in relation to the writer’s purpose and to the approaches to thinking and writing that the writer has used. • Coherence is maintained within and between paragraphs. • Focus is maintained throughout the response.

Source: [National Assessment Governing Board. Writing Framework for the 2011 National Assessment of Educational Progress](https://www.nagb.gov/content/dam/nagb/en/documents/publications/frameworks/writing/2011-writing-framework.pdf), NAEP 2011 Writing Framework (NAGB), Chapter 4, Exhibit 4.1 Criteria for Evaluating Responses.

Applies: Students may respond to the topic, purpose, and/or audience in any number of different ways to best accomplish the demands of the writing task, leading to many kinds of effective text structures.

### naep_language_facility_conventions — Language Facility and Conventions

> Language facility and conventions support clarity of expression and the effectiveness of the writing in relation to the writer’s purpose and audience. • Sentence structure is well controlled and sentence variety is appropriate for the writer’s purpose and audience. • Precise and appropriate word choice supports clarity of expression and enhances the presentation of the writer’s ideas. • Voice and tone are effective in relation to the writer’s purpose and audience. • Grammar, usage, and mechanics (capitalization, punctuation, and spelling) support clarity of expression and enhance the presentation of the writer’s ideas.

Source: [National Assessment Governing Board. Writing Framework for the 2011 National Assessment of Educational Progress](https://www.nagb.gov/content/dam/nagb/en/documents/publications/frameworks/writing/2011-writing-framework.pdf), NAEP 2011 Writing Framework (NAGB), Chapter 4, Exhibit 4.1 Criteria for Evaluating Responses.

Applies: Effective writing also involves adapting word choice, voice, and tone depending on the purpose, audience, and/or topic of a writing task.

### naep_holistic_to_explain — Preliminary Holistic Scoring Guide for To Explain

> The holistic approach to scoring focuses on an evaluation of the whole response rather than on its individual parts (Myers, 1980). That is, a response will not be evaluated with a separate score for each writing feature, and an overall score will not be derived by adding together scores for each separate feature.

Source: [National Assessment Governing Board. Writing Framework for the 2011 National Assessment of Educational Progress](https://www.nagb.gov/content/dam/nagb/en/documents/publications/frameworks/writing/2011-writing-framework.pdf), NAEP 2011 Writing Framework, Appendix C2, printed pp. 68–71 (identical wording, modulo commas and "e.g."→"for example", in the 2017 framework Appendix B2).

Applies: The effectiveness of these features will be evaluated in relation to the purpose and audience specified in the writing task.

Published levels (0 to 6): 0: Unscorable: Response is too brief to score; not written in English; off topic; or illegible.; 1: Responses in this range demonstrate little or no skill in responding to the writing task. Elements are seldom controlled and provide almost no support for the writer’s purpose, audience, and form.
• The response may provide little or no explanation of the subject.
• If any details or examples are present, they are brief, general, undeveloped, or not relevant to the explanation.
• The response shows an attempt to organize thoughts by grouping ideas, but groupings are illogical and there is little or no evidence of relevant approaches to organization. Relationships among ideas are mostly unclear.
• Sentence structure is often incorrect; word choice is often unclear and inappropriate; and there is little or no control of appropriate voice and tone.
• Grammar, usage, and mechanics are sometimes correct but with frequent distracting errors that often impede understanding.
• The response may be too brief to support a sound judgment about the development of ideas, organization, or language facility and conventions.; 2: Responses in this range demonstrate marginal skill in responding to the writing task. Elements are sometimes controlled but provide weak support for the writer’s purpose, audience, and form.
• The response provides a little explanation of the subject.
• A balance between broad assertions and general and specific examples may not be evident. If details and examples are present, they are brief, general, or inadequately developed, and they may not be clearly relevant to the explanation. There may be minimal evidence of relevant approaches to the development of ideas.
• Some ideas may not be clearly focused on the topic. The response shows an attempt to organize thoughts by grouping ideas, and there may be minimal evidence of approaches to organization. However, relationships among ideas are often illogical or unclear.
• Sentence structure is sometimes correct, but there is little, if any, sentence variety. Word choice is rarely specific and does little to support the clarity of the explanation. Voice and tone show little understanding of what is appropriate for the writer’s purpose and audience.
• Grammar, usage, and mechanics are usually correct but with many distracting errors that impede understanding.; 3: Responses in this range demonstrate developing skill in responding to the writing task. Some elements are controlled and provide some support for the writer’s purpose, audience, and form.
• The response provides some explanation of the subject.
• The explanation may provide both broad assertions and general and specific examples, but the balance may be uneven and only somewhat controlled. Some relevant details and examples are used, but they are not developed enough to support the explanation, or they may be unevenly developed. Approaches to the development of ideas are evident, but they may not be clearly relevant to the explanation.
• Most ideas are focused on the topic. The response uses a simple organizational structure, and for the most part, ideas are logically grouped. There may be some evidence of approaches to organization, but they may not be clearly relevant, or they may be confusing. Relationships among ideas are sometimes unclear.
• Sentence structure is usually correct and there may be a little sentence variety to communicate relationships among ideas. Word choice is usually clear and sometimes specific, but at times it may not be appropriate for the writer’s purpose. Voice and tone show some understanding of what is appropriate for the writer’s purpose and audience.
• Grammar, usage, and mechanics are mostly correct but with some distracting errors that may occasionally impede understanding.; 4: Responses in this range demonstrate adequate skill in responding to the writing task. Most elements are controlled and support the writer’s purpose, audience, and form.
• The response provides an explanation by addressing most parts of the subject and may include an evaluation of the importance of some of these parts.
• The explanation maintains an adequate balance between broad assertions and specific examples and details. While details and examples adequately support the clarity of the explanation, their development may be somewhat uneven. Approaches to the development of ideas are adequate, but their relevance to the explanation may not always be clear.
• Ideas are usually focused on the topic and an organizational structure is evident. Ideas are logically grouped and adequately reflect the writer’s use of relevant approaches to organization. Relationships among ideas are mostly clear.
• Sentence structure is adequately controlled and somewhat varied to communicate relationships among ideas. Word choice is sometimes specific and adequately supports the clarity of the explanation. Voice and tone are mostly controlled and usually effective for the writer’s purpose and audience.
• Grammar, usage, and mechanics are mostly correct with some distracting errors, but meaning is clear.; 5: Responses in this range demonstrate competent skill in responding to the writing task. Elements are usually well controlled and clearly support the writer’s purpose, audience, and form.
• The response provides a clear explanation of the subject by examining the topic as a whole, identifying and discussing various parts of the subject, and/or by evaluating and discussing the importance of those parts.
• The explanation usually maintains an effective balance between broad assertions and general and specific details and examples to support the clarity of the explanation. Approaches to the development of ideas are usually used skillfully to support the clarity of the explanation.
• Ideas are usually focused on the topic. Organization is clear and may demonstrate a logical progression of ideas that supports the clarity of the explanation and is relevant to the writer’s approaches to organization. Transitions clearly convey relationships among ideas.
• Sentence structure is well controlled to communicate relationships among ideas and varied as appropriate for the writer’s purpose. Word choice is usually specific and precise, and it usually supports the clarity of the explanation. Voice and tone are usually controlled and effective for the writer’s purpose and audience.
• Grammar, usage, and mechanics are usually correct with a few distracting errors, but meaning is clear.; 6: Responses in this range demonstrate effective skill in responding to the writing task. All elements of the response are well controlled and effectively support the writer’s purpose, audience, and form.
• The response provides a thoughtful and insightful explanation of the subject by fully examining the topic as a whole, by identifying and fully discussing significant parts of the subject, and/or by evaluating and fully discussing the importance of the parts.
• The explanation maintains an effective balance between broad assertions and well chosen general and specific details and examples to fully support understanding. Approaches to the development of ideas (e.g., analyzing, evaluating, narrating, etc.) are used skillfully to support the clarity of the explanation.
• Ideas are clearly focused on the topic throughout the response. Organization demonstrates a logical, well-executed progression of ideas that supports the clarity of the explanation and is relevant to the writer’s approaches to organization (e.g., summarizing, narrating, etc.). Transitions effectively convey relationships among ideas.
• Sentence structure is well controlled and varied to communicate relationships among ideas. Word choice is specific, precise, and evaluative and supports the clarity of the explanation. Voice and tone are well controlled and effective for the writer’s purpose and audience.
• Though there may be a few minor errors in grammar, usage, and mechanics, meaning is clear throughout the response.

### ielts_t2_task_response — Task Response

> The TR criterion assesses: how fully the candidate responds to the task. how adequately the main ideas are extended and supported. how relevant the candidate’s ideas are to the task. how clearly the candidate opens the discourse, establishes their position and formulates conclusions. how appropriate the format of the response is to the task.

Source: [IELTS. Writing Key Assessment Criteria](https://ielts.org/cdn/Guides/ielts-writing-key-assessment-criteria.pdf), IELTS Writing Key Assessment Criteria (ielts.org PDF), Task 2 / Task 1 and Task 2, TASK RESPONSE (TR); levels: IELTS Writing Task 2 Band Descriptors (updated May 2023).

Published levels (0 to 9) are not reproduced here because of the source's licence; a local copy in restricted/levels.json is used when present.

### ielts_t2_coherence_cohesion — Coherence and Cohesion

> This criterion is concerned with the overall organisation and logical development of the message: how the response organises and links information, ideas and language. Coherence refers to the linking of ideas through logical sequencing, while cohesion refers to the varied and appropriate use of cohesive devices (e.g. logical connectors, conjunctions and pronouns) to assist in making clear the relationships between and within sentences.

Source: [IELTS. Writing Key Assessment Criteria](https://ielts.org/cdn/Guides/ielts-writing-key-assessment-criteria.pdf), IELTS Writing Key Assessment Criteria (ielts.org PDF), Task 2 / Task 1 and Task 2, COHERENCE AND COHESION (CC); levels: Task 2 Band Descriptors.

Published levels (0 to 9) are not reproduced here because of the source's licence; a local copy in restricted/levels.json is used when present.

### act_ideas_analysis — Ideas and Analysis

> Ideas and Analysis—Scores in this domain reflect the ability to generate productive ideas and engage critically with multiple perspectives on the given issue. Competent writers understand the issue they are invited to address, the purpose for writing, and the audience. They generate ideas that are relevant to the situation.

Source: [ACT. Understanding Your Scores: Writing test scores](https://www.act.org/content/act/en/products-and-services/the-act/scores/writing-test-scores.html), ACT, Writing scores page (domain definitions); levels: The ACT Writing Test Scoring Rubric (PDF, 2015), column "Ideas and Analysis".

Published levels (1 to 6) are not reproduced here because of the source's licence; a local copy in restricted/levels.json is used when present.

### act_development_support — Development and Support

> Development and Support—Scores in this domain reflect the ability to discuss ideas, offer rationale, and bolster an argument. Competent writers explain and explore their ideas, discuss implications, and illustrate through examples. They help the reader understand their thinking about the issue.

Source: [ACT. Understanding Your Scores: Writing test scores](https://www.act.org/content/act/en/products-and-services/the-act/scores/writing-test-scores.html), ACT, Writing scores page (domain definitions); levels: The ACT Writing Test Scoring Rubric (PDF, 2015), column "Development and Support".

Published levels (1 to 6) are not reproduced here because of the source's licence; a local copy in restricted/levels.json is used when present.

### act_organization — Organization

> Organization—Scores in this domain reflect the ability to organize ideas with clarity and purpose. Organizational choices are integral to effective writing. Competent writers arrange their essay in a way that clearly shows the relationship between ideas, and they guide the reader through their discussion.

Source: [ACT. Understanding Your Scores: Writing test scores](https://www.act.org/content/act/en/products-and-services/the-act/scores/writing-test-scores.html), ACT, Writing scores page (domain definitions); levels: The ACT Writing Test Scoring Rubric (PDF, 2015), column "Organization".

Published levels (1 to 6) are not reproduced here because of the source's licence; a local copy in restricted/levels.json is used when present.

### act_language_use_conventions — Language Use and Conventions

> Language Use and Conventions—Scores in this domain reflect the ability to use written language to convey arguments with clarity. Competent writers make use of the conventions of grammar, syntax, word usage, and mechanics. They are also aware of their audience and adjust the style and tone of their writing to communicate effectively.

Source: [ACT. Understanding Your Scores: Writing test scores](https://www.act.org/content/act/en/products-and-services/the-act/scores/writing-test-scores.html), ACT, Writing scores page (domain definitions); levels: The ACT Writing Test Scoring Rubric (PDF, 2015), column "Language Use" (the PDF column header; the scores page names the domain "Language Use and Conventions").

Published levels (1 to 6) are not reproduced here because of the source's licence; a local copy in restricted/levels.json is used when present.

Contributes rather than criteria:

- [National Assessment Governing Board. Writing Framework for the 2011 National Assessment of Educational Progress](https://www.nagb.gov/content/dam/nagb/en/documents/publications/frameworks/writing/2011-writing-framework.pdf) (methods): NAEP scores holistically: 'a response will not be evaluated with a separate score for each writing feature'. The guides are 'preliminary'; 'the language used to describe expectations for the level of performance may differ in the final scoring rubrics', and 'the interpretation, or application, of the rubric will be different at each grade'. Scorers are trained on 'actual responses selected at each grade level'.
- [IELTS. Writing Band Descriptors (Task 1 and Task 2), updated May 2023](https://ielts.org/cdn/ielts-guides/ielts-writing-band-descriptors.pdf) (methods): Rating rule printed on every page: 'A script must fully fit the positive features of the descriptor at a particular level. Bolded text indicates negative features that will limit a rating.' The bold marking is lost in the text extraction, so which descriptor phrases are negative features is not recorded here. Band 1 in every criterion also says 'Responses of 20 words or fewer are rated at Band 1.' 'The criteria are weighted equally and the score on the task is the average.' (ielts.org scoring page).
- [IELTS. Writing Key Assessment Criteria](https://ielts.org/cdn/Guides/ielts-writing-key-assessment-criteria.pdf) (bias_checks): Genre clash to keep in mind before reusing IELTS levels on technical documents: 'Candidates should note that scripts may be penalised if they are a) partly or wholly plagiarised, b) not written as full, connected text (e.g. using bullet points in any part of the response, or note form, is not appropriate, etc.).' Task 2 also requires 'a minimum of 250 words'.
- [ACT. The ACT Writing Test Scoring Rubric](https://www.act.org/content/dam/act/unsecured/documents/Writing-Test-Scoring-Rubric.pdf) (methods): Each ACT score row has a holistic lead (e.g. 'Score 6: Responses at this scorepoint demonstrate effective skill in writing an argumentative essay.') not repeated in the domain levels above. Scoring: 'Two trained readers will score your essay on a scale of 1-6 in each of the four writing domains. Each domain score represents the sum of the two readers' scores. If the readers' ratings disagree by more than one point, a third reader will evaluate the essay and resolve the discrepancy.' The rubric is for an argumentative essay on a given issue.
- [ETS. GRE Practice General Test #3: Analytical Writing Sample Essays and Commentaries (large print edition)](https://www.ets.org/content/dam/ets-org/pdfs/gre/gre-practice-test-3-writing-responses-18-point.pdf) (examples): Official scored sample Issue responses with reader commentaries at every score point ('There are responses and scoring comments for essays with scores of 6, 5, 4, 3, 2, and 1.'), topic: 'The best way for a society to prepare its young people for leadership in government, industry, or other fields is by instilling in them a sense of cooperation, not competition.' Cite by URL and score; essays not copied (ETS copyright). The ETS Issue/Argument pages also point to separate scored-sample pages that were not fetched.
- [Crossley, S. A. et al. The English Language Learner Insight, Proficiency and Skills Evaluation (ELLIPSE) Corpus. International Journal of Learner Corpus Research 9(2), 248–269](https://github.com/scrosseye/ELLIPSE-Corpus) (examples): Human-scored analytic traits suitable for calibrating judges on generic writing quality: 'scored for overall holistic language proficiency as well as analytic proficiency scores related to cohesion, syntax, vocabulary, phraseology, grammar, and conventions'. Licence: 'CC BY-NC-SA 4.0'. Test split and raw rater scores are in password-protected zips whose passwords the README publishes. Learner (ELL, grades 8–12) essays, not professional documents. Not downloaded.
- [Crossley, S. A. et al. A large-scale corpus for assessing written argumentation: PERSUADE 2.0. Assessing Writing 61](https://github.com/scrosseye/persuade_corpus_2.0) (examples): Holistic essay scores and 'proficiency scores for each argumentative and discourse element' on 25,000+ student essays; licence 'CC BY-NC-SA 4.0'. Useful for element-level effectiveness, not analytic writing traits. Data are on Google Drive links from the README; not downloaded.
- [Mathias, S. and Bhattacharyya, P. ASAP++: Enriching the ASAP Automated Essay Grading Dataset with Essay Attribute Scores. LREC 2018, 1169–1173](https://lwsam.github.io/ASAP++/lrec2018.html) (examples): Trait scores per essay for ASAP prompts 1–6; CSV headers verified from the first bytes only: prompt 1 'EssayID,Content,Organization,Word Choice,Sentence Fluency,Conventions'; prompt 3 'Essay ID,Content,Prompt Adherence,Language,Narrativity'. No licence stated; underlying essays from the Kaggle ASAP competition (terms not verified). The original ASAP 'Feedback Prize' Kaggle competitions (Learning Agency Lab) were not checked; the ELLIPSE and PERSUADE repos above are their openly licensed releases only as far as their READMEs say (the ELLIPSE README does not mention Kaggle).

Gaps: All four instruments score student essays on a set prompt (NAEP: grades 4/8/12; GRE/ACT/IELTS: test takers), not professional documents; GRE Issue and ACT are argument tasks and NAEP To Explain is the closest to expository writing. NAEP and GRE are holistic: the NAEP 'Development/Organization/Language' criteria have no separate levels (levels sit only on the holistic To Explain guide) and the GRE five-item reader list has no levels. NAEP guides are labelled 'preliminary'; operational item-specific scoring guides and scored samples in the NAEP Questions Tool were not fetched. The GRE Argument guide applies only to tests before 22 September 2023. IELTS bold 'negative features' could not be recovered from text extraction, and IELTS levels were reconstructed from a 4-column layout; each sentence was verified against the non-layout extraction, but the cell assignment rests on column positions. The ACT Score 3 'Ideas and Analysis' cell needed one hand fix ('given' split across the column boundary). Licences: NAEP is U.S. government work (public domain); ETS, IELTS partners and ACT reserve all rights, so the levels are quoted for internal research and should not be republished wholesale. AAC&U VALUE and 6+1 Traits are already in references.json and were not repeated here. Datasets: ELLIPSE and PERSUADE 2.0 are CC BY-NC-SA 4.0 (non-commercial); ASAP++ states no licence; the Kaggle Feedback Prize competition pages were not checked.

## Generic automatic text measures and their critiques

Sources: [Coleman, M. and Liau, T. L. A computer readability formula designed for machine scoring. Journal of Applied Psychology 60(2), 283–284](https://doi.org/10.1037/h0076540); [Dale, E. and Chall, J. S. A Formula for Predicting Readability. Educational Research Bulletin 27, 11–20, 28](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dale%E2%80%93Chall_readability_formula); [Flesch, R. A new readability yardstick. Journal of Applied Psychology 32(3), 221–233](https://doi.org/10.1037/h0057532); [Flesch, R. How to Write Plain English, Chapter 2 'Let's Start With the Formula' (as reproduced on a University of Canterbury writing-guide page)](https://web.archive.org/web/20170112043649/http://www.mang.canterbury.ac.nz/writing_guide/writing/flesch.shtml); [Gunning, R. The Technique of Clear Writing. McGraw-Hill (pp. 36–37 per Wikipedia)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gunning_fog_index); [ISO 24495-1:2023 Plain language — Part 1: Governing principles and guidelines](https://www.iso.org/standard/78907.html); [Jarrett, C. and Redish, G. Readability Formulas: 7 Reasons to Avoid Them and What to Do Instead. UXmatters, July 29, 2019](https://www.uxmatters.com/mt/archives/2019/07/readability-formulas-7-reasons-to-avoid-them-and-what-to-do-instead.php); [Kincaid, J. P., Fishburne, R. P., Rogers, R. L. and Chissom, B. S. Derivation of New Readability Formulas (Automated Readability Index, Fog Count and Flesch Reading Ease Formula) for Navy Enlisted Personnel. Research Branch Report 8-75, Naval Technical Training Command (DTIC ADA006655)](https://apps.dtic.mil/sti/pdfs/ADA006655.pdf); [Klare, G. R. The measurement of readability (reprint of chapter 1 of The Measurement of Readability, 1963). ACM Journal of Computer Documentation 24(3), 107–121](https://doi.org/10.1145/344599.344630); [McLaughlin, G. H. SMOG Grading — a New Readability Formula. Journal of Reading 12(8), 639–646](https://web.archive.org/web/20110409151559/http://www.harrymclaughlin.com/SMOG_Readability_Formula_G._Harry_McLaughlin_(1969).pdf); [McCarthy, P. M. and Jarvis, S. MTLD, vocd-D, and HD-D: A validation study of sophisticated approaches to lexical diversity assessment. Behavior Research Methods 42(2), 381–392](https://doi.org/10.3758/BRM.42.2.381); [Government of Canada, Our Languages blog. Readability formulas, programs and tools: Do they work for plain language?](https://www.noslangues-ourlanguages.gc.ca/en/blogue-blog/readability-formulas-eng); [Redish, J. Readability formulas have even more limitations than Klare discusses. ACM Journal of Computer Documentation 24(3), 132–137](https://doi.org/10.1145/344599.344637); [U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission. A Plain English Handbook: How to create clear SEC disclosure documents](https://www.sec.gov/pdf/handbook.pdf).

Contributes rather than criteria:

- [Flesch, R. How to Write Plain English, Chapter 2 'Let's Start With the Formula' (as reproduced on a University of Canterbury writing-guide page)](https://web.archive.org/web/20170112043649/http://www.mang.canterbury.ac.nz/writing_guide/writing/flesch.shtml) (methods): Flesch Reading Ease: predicted ease from average sentence length and average word length in syllables. Flesch's own wording: 'Multiply the average sentence length by 1.015. Multiply the average word length by 84.6. Add the two numbers. Subtract this sum from 206.835. The balance is your readability score.' Scale: 'The scale shows scores from 0 to 100. Zero means practically unreadable and 100 means extremely easy.' Original: Flesch 1948 (flesch-1948), not consulted. The Kincaid report's 'Old' row prints the syllable term as '.836 (syllables/100 words)' in OCR, which disagrees with 84.6 per word (0.846 per 100 words); likely OCR or typo, unresolved.
- [Kincaid, J. P., Fishburne, R. P., Rogers, R. L. and Chissom, B. S. Derivation of New Readability Formulas (Automated Readability Index, Fog Count and Flesch Reading Ease Formula) for Navy Enlisted Personnel. Research Branch Report 8-75, Naval Technical Training Command (DTIC ADA006655)](https://apps.dtic.mil/sti/pdfs/ADA006655.pdf) (methods): Flesch–Kincaid grade level: Flesch's two counts recalculated against the reading grade of Navy enlisted readers. Table 3: 'New: GL = .39 (words/sentence) +11.8 (syllables/word) - 15.59' (GL is grade level). The report states the formulas 'are specifically for Navy use' (derived from 531 personnel reading 18 Rate Training Manual passages).
- [Gunning, R. The Technique of Clear Writing. McGraw-Hill (pp. 36–37 per Wikipedia)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gunning_fog_index) (methods): Gunning fog index: years of schooling from average sentence length plus percentage of 'complex' (3+ syllable) words, times 0.4 ('Add the average sentence length and the percentage of complex words; and Multiply the result by 0.4.' — Wikipedia, secondary). Kincaid et al. 1975 also give a Navy 'Fog Count' (easy words + 3 hard words per sentence), a different formula.
- [McLaughlin, G. H. SMOG Grading — a New Readability Formula. Journal of Reading 12(8), 639–646](https://web.archive.org/web/20110409151559/http://www.harrymclaughlin.com/SMOG_Readability_Formula_G._Harry_McLaughlin_(1969).pdf) (methods): SMOG: reading grade from the count of polysyllabic words in 30 sentences. 'SMOG grade = 3 + square root of polysyllable count.' Panel: 'Count 10 consecutive sentences near the beginning of the text to be assessed, 10 in the middle and 10 near the end.' Calibrated on 100% comprehension, so it reads higher than other formulas.
- [Dale, E. and Chall, J. S. A Formula for Predicting Readability. Educational Research Bulletin 27, 11–20, 28](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dale%E2%80%93Chall_readability_formula) (methods): Dale–Chall: difficulty from the percentage of words not on a list of familiar words plus average sentence length; raw score 0.1579 × (difficult words/words × 100) + 0.0496 × (words/sentences), and 'If the percentage of difficult words is above 5%, then add 3.6365 to the raw score' (Wikipedia, secondary). Critique: the list fixes 1948 fourth-grade meanings ('"enter" on a tax form is not the same "enter" that fourth-graders know' — Redish 2000).
- [Coleman, M. and Liau, T. L. A computer readability formula designed for machine scoring. Journal of Applied Psychology 60(2), 283–284](https://doi.org/10.1037/h0076540) (methods): Coleman–Liau: grade from letters per 100 words and sentences per 100 words, so no syllable counting; CLI = 0.0588·L − 0.296·S − 15.8 where 'L is the average number of letters per 100 words and S is the average number of sentences per 100 words.' (Wikipedia, secondary).
- [McCarthy, P. M. and Jarvis, S. MTLD, vocd-D, and HD-D: A validation study of sophisticated approaches to lexical diversity assessment. Behavior Research Methods 42(2), 381–392](https://doi.org/10.3758/BRM.42.2.381) (methods): MTLD (measure of textual lexical diversity): 'calculated as the mean length of word strings that maintain a criterion level of lexical variation'; the authors report it is 'the only index not found to vary as a function of text length' and advise 'using MTLD, vocd-D (or HD-D), and Maas in their studies, rather than any single index'. Criterion TTR threshold and the bidirectional computation are in the full paper, which was not consulted.
- [Redish, J. Readability formulas have even more limitations than Klare discusses. ACM Journal of Computer Documentation 24(3), 132–137](https://doi.org/10.1145/344599.344637) (bias_checks): Critique for adult technical text: 'For technical materials for adults, using any readability formula means generalizing from situations that are decades old and not directly relevant to the audience or type of material.' 'Readability formulas assume that you are writing prose paragraphs. They count sentence length by going from period to period. If you use bulleted lists to chunk your material and lay your text out with white space, readability formulas will say you have long sentences.' 'The most serious problem with using a readability formula is that a good score does not mean you have a usable or useful document.' Pair: 'I wave my hand.' / 'I waive my rights.' score identically. Redish quotes Klare's limitations: 'Formulas measure only style. They do not touch on content, organization, word order, format, or imagery.'
- [Redish, J. Readability formulas have even more limitations than Klare discusses. ACM Journal of Computer Documentation 24(3), 132–137](https://doi.org/10.1145/344599.344637) (bias_checks): Rewriting to the score: 'Klare, Flesch, Gunning, and all the other developers of readability formulas insist that the formulas are not to be used for revision.' Klare (quoted by Redish): 'the temptation simply to substitute an easy word for a hard one, to cut sentences in half, or to eliminate prepositional phrases even when they are needed may produce exactly the opposite of the desired result.' Redish's pair (from Redish and Selzer 1985): 'He is the defendant. He is fifteen years old. He is in his teens. Someone says he stole from the store.' vs 'The defendant is a fifteen-year old teenager who is accused of shoplifting.' — 'The second, longer sentence is actually easier to understand, although it has the poorer readability score.'
- [Jarrett, C. and Redish, G. Readability Formulas: 7 Reasons to Avoid Them and What to Do Instead. UXmatters, July 29, 2019](https://www.uxmatters.com/mt/archives/2019/07/readability-formulas-7-reasons-to-avoid-them-and-what-to-do-instead.php) (bias_checks): Reliability: 'the SMOG Index typically scores at least two grade levels higher than the Dale-Chall Formula. The Gunning Fog Index sometimes comes out three grade levels higher than other formulas.' and 'different computer programs running the same text through the same formula gave different scores.' Lists: 'Just ending each item with or without a period could change your content’s score dramatically.'
- [U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission. A Plain English Handbook: How to create clear SEC disclosure documents](https://www.sec.gov/pdf/handbook.pdf) (bias_checks): SEC Plain English Handbook ch. 9: 'No formula takes into account the content of the document being evaluated. In other words, no formula can tell you if you have conveyed the information clearly' and 'by some formulas’ calculations, Einstein’s theory of relativity reads at a 5th grade level.'
- [ISO 24495-1:2023 Plain language — Part 1: Governing principles and guidelines](https://www.iso.org/standard/78907.html) (bias_checks): ISO 24495-1, as quoted with permission on the Government of Canada Our Languages blog (our-languages-readability): 'plain language focuses on how successfully readers can use the document rather than on mechanical measures such as readability formulas.' Not checked against the standard itself (paywalled).

Gaps: These are methods, not criteria. Primary papers for Flesch 1948, Gunning 1952, Dale-Chall 1948 and Coleman-Liau 1975 were not consulted; their formulas were verified only against Flesch's own 1979 restatement (Flesch), the Kincaid report (FK grade level) and Wikipedia snapshots (Fog, Dale-Chall, Coleman-Liau), which are secondary. The Kincaid report text is OCR, and its 'Old' Flesch row reads '.836 (syllables/100 words)', which does not match Flesch's 84.6 per word; unresolved. The MTLD threshold (TTR 0.72) and procedure are not verified (only the PubMed abstract was available). Klare's own text was not consulted; his limitations are quoted from Redish 2000. Schriver 2000 (ACM JCD 24(3)) was downloaded but is image-only and was not used. Coh-Metrix (already in references.json) also computes readability and lexical-diversity indices, but that was not re-verified here.

## Published examples

Sources: [Headquarters, Department of the Army. DA Pamphlet 600-67, Effective Writing for Army Leaders](https://armywriter.com/DAP600-67.pdf); [Federal Plain Language Guidelines (PLAIN, plainlanguage.gov)](https://www.plainlanguage.gov/guidelines/); [Google for Developers. Technical Writing One](https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/one); [Google for Developers. Technical Writing Two: Organizing large documents](https://developers.google.com/tech-writing/two/large-docs); [Gopen, G. D. and Swan, J. A. The Science of Scientific Writing. American Scientist 78(6):550–558](https://www.americanscientist.org/blog/the-long-view/the-science-of-scientific-writing); [Kent, S. Words of Estimative Probability. Studies in Intelligence 8(4)](https://www.cia.gov/resources/csi/static/Words-of-Estimative-Probability.pdf); [kubernetes/enhancements pull request 4439: KEP-127 user namespaces, graduate to beta](https://github.com/kubernetes/enhancements/pull/4439); [kubernetes/enhancements pull request 3859: KEP-2033 Kubelet-in-UserNS](https://github.com/kubernetes/enhancements/pull/3859); [python/peps pull request 4050: PEP 661, add missing sections](https://github.com/python/peps/pull/4050); [Quiller-Couch, A. On the Art of Writing: Lectures delivered in the University of Cambridge, 1913–1914 (Project Gutenberg eBook #17470)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/17470); [rust-lang/rfcs pull request 1861: extern types](https://github.com/rust-lang/rfcs/pull/1861); [rust-lang/rfcs pull request 2539: cfg_attr expanding to multiple attributes](https://github.com/rust-lang/rfcs/pull/2539); [rust-lang/rfcs pull request 2909: destructuring assignment](https://github.com/rust-lang/rfcs/pull/2909); [rust-lang/rfcs pull request 3013: checking conditional compilation at compile time](https://github.com/rust-lang/rfcs/pull/3013); [rust-lang/rfcs pull request 3641: export_ordinal attribute](https://github.com/rust-lang/rfcs/pull/3641); [U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission. A Plain English Handbook: How to create clear SEC disclosure documents](https://www.sec.gov/pdf/handbook.pdf); [Strunk, W., Jr. The Elements of Style. Ithaca, NY: privately printed (Project Gutenberg eBook #37134)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/37134).

Gaps: Supplies the stronger/weaker pairs used as examples and fixtures; these sources state no criteria of their own beyond the guidance already listed in the families that publish them.
